\chapter{Properties of Statistics}

\section{Introduction}

Suppose an experiment has been designed and has produced data
$X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n$, where the sample size $n$ is fixed and known. Statistical
inference uses the observed values to learn about the distribution, or the
parameter, that generated them. It has two main branches.
\begin{itemize}
\item In \emph{estimation} the observations are used to give an approximate value
for some numerical characteristic of the true distribution. The answer may be a
single number (point estimation) or a set of plausible values (interval
estimation).
\item In \emph{hypothesis testing} the observations are used to decide whether
the true distribution, or the true parameter, belongs to a specified subset of
the possibilities.
\end{itemize}

We write the sample compactly as the random vector $X = (X_1, \dots, X_n)$, and its
joint probability (density) function as $f_\theta(x) = f_\theta(x_1, \dots, x_n)$.
A \emph{model} is a family $\{f_\theta(x) : \theta\in\Omega\}$, where the
\emph{parameter space} $\Omega$ is the set of possible values of $\theta$.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:normalmodel}
If $X = (X_1, \dots, X_n)$ is a random sample from $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$, then
$\theta = (\mu, \sigma^2)$,
\[f_\theta(x) = \prod^n_{i = 1} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi \sigma^2}}\,
e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}(x_i - \mu)^2}
= (2\pi\sigma^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2\right),\]
and $\Omega = \{(\mu, \sigma^2) : -\infty < \mu < \infty,\ \sigma^2 > 0\}$.
\end{exa}

\begin{defn}
\label{def:statistic}
A \emph{statistic} $T(X)$ is a function of the data that does not depend on the
unknown parameter $\theta$. An \emph{estimator} is a statistic used to estimate
$\theta$, or a function $\tau(\theta)$; its observed value $T(x)$ is an
\emph{estimate}.
\end{defn}

Examples of statistics are the sample mean $\bar X = \frac1n\sum X_i$, the sample
variance $S^2 = \frac{1}{n-1}\sum (X_i - \bar X)^2$, the smallest and largest
observations $X_{(1)} = \min_i X_i$ and $X_{(n)} = \max_i X_i$, and the vector of
order statistics $(X_{(1)}, X_{(2)}, \dots, X_{(n)})$. The quantity
$\frac{\bar X - \mu}{\sigma/\sqrt n}$ is not a statistic when $\mu$ or $\sigma^2$
is unknown, since it cannot be computed from the data.

A statistic does not depend on $\theta$, but its distribution usually does: the
distribution of $\bar X$ moves with $\mu$. An estimator is therefore a random
variable, such as $\bar X$, while an estimate, such as the number $\bar x$, is one
observed value of it.

\section{Unbiasedness and Mean Square Error}

Two questions we ask of an estimator are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Is it centred on the right value, or is it consistently too large or too
small?
\item How far from the true value is it likely to be?
\end{enumerate}
The first leads to unbiasedness, the second to the mean square error. Both are
expectations, so we first fix notation. We write $E_\theta$ for an expectation
computed under the parameter value $\theta$:
\[E_\theta\left[h(X)\right] = \begin{cases}
\int h(x)\, f_\theta(x)\, dx & \text{if } X \text{ is continuous},\\[1ex]
\sum_x h(x)\, f_\theta(x) & \text{if } X \text{ is discrete},
\end{cases}\]
provided $E_\theta|h(X)| < \infty$. When this fails the expectation does not exist,
and an estimator without a mean cannot be unbiased.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:cauchymean}
Let $X$ have the Cauchy distribution with p.d.f.
$f(x) = \frac{1}{\pi(1 + x^2)}$, $-\infty < x < \infty$. Show that $E(X)$ does not
exist.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
By symmetry,
\[E|X| = \frac{2}{\pi}\int^{\infty}_{0} \frac{x}{1 + x^2}\, dx
= \lim_{m\to\infty} \frac{1}{\pi}\ln\left(1 + m^2\right) = \infty .\]
Since $E|X|$ is infinite, $E(X)$ is not defined. (The symmetric integral
$\int_{-m}^m \frac{x}{\pi(1+x^2)}\,dx = 0$ for every $m$, but that is not the
definition of an expectation: the positive and negative parts are each infinite.)
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:paretomoments}
Let $X$ have p.d.f. $f_\theta(x) = \frac{\theta}{x^{\theta + 1}}$, $x\ge 1$,
$\theta>0$. For which $\theta$ do $E_\theta(X)$ and $\var_\theta(X)$ exist? Find
them.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
For $\theta\ne 1$,
\[E_\theta(X) = \int_1^\infty x\,\frac{\theta}{x^{\theta + 1}}\, dx
= \int_1^\infty \theta\, x^{-\theta}\, dx
= \left[\frac{\theta}{1 - \theta}\, x^{1 - \theta}\right]_1^\infty .\]
The term $x^{1-\theta}$ tends to $0$ as $x\to\infty$ only if $\theta>1$; for
$\theta\le 1$ the integral diverges. So $E_\theta(X) = \frac{\theta}{\theta - 1}$
for $\theta>1$. In the same way
\[E_\theta(X^2) = \int_1^\infty \theta\, x^{1 - \theta}\, dx = \frac{\theta}{\theta - 2},
\qquad \theta>2 ,\]
and for $\theta>2$
\[\var_\theta(X) = \frac{\theta}{\theta - 2} - \frac{\theta^2}{(\theta - 1)^2}
= \frac{\theta(\theta - 1)^2 - \theta^2(\theta - 2)}{(\theta - 1)^2(\theta - 2)}
= \frac{\theta}{(\theta - 1)^2(\theta - 2)} .\]
For $1<\theta\le 2$ the mean exists but the variance is infinite.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:gammamoments}
If $X\thicksim$ GAM$(\alpha\, , \, \beta)$, show that 
\[E_{\theta}\left(X^p\right) = \frac{\beta^p\, \Gamma\left(\alpha + p\right)}{\Gamma (\alpha)}\]
For what values of $p$ does this expectation exist?
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The GAM$(\alpha,\beta)$ density is
\[f(x) = \frac{x^{\alpha-1}e^{-x/\beta}}{\beta^{\alpha}\Gamma(\alpha)},\qquad x>0 .\]
Hence
\[E\left(X^{p}\right)
= \frac{1}{\beta^{\alpha}\Gamma(\alpha)}\int_0^{\infty} x^{\alpha+p-1}e^{-x/\beta}\,dx .\]
The integral is the normalising constant of a GAM$(\alpha+p,\beta)$ density, so
it equals $\beta^{\alpha+p}\,\Gamma(\alpha+p)$ whenever $\alpha+p>0$, and
\[E\left(X^{p}\right) = \frac{\beta^{\alpha+p}\Gamma(\alpha+p)}{\beta^{\alpha}\Gamma(\alpha)}
= \frac{\beta^{p}\,\Gamma(\alpha+p)}{\Gamma(\alpha)} .\]

\subhead{When it exists}
The integral converges at the origin only if $\alpha+p>0$, so the expectation
exists precisely for $p>-\alpha$. Positive moments always exist; negative moments
exist only down to $-\alpha$, which is why $E(1/X)$ requires $\alpha>1$ and
$E(1/X^{2})$ requires $\alpha>2$. Convergence at infinity is never in doubt --- the
exponential dominates any power.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\, m(t) = E\left(e^{tx}\right)\,$ be the moment generating function of a continued random variable $X$. The function $m(-t)$ is often called the Laplace Transform of the probability function $X$. Show that
\[E\left(X^{-p}\right) = \frac{1}{\Gamma (p)}\, \int_0^{\infty}\, m(-t)\, t^{p - 1}\, dt\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Assume $X>0$ with probability one and $p>0$. The key is the integral
representation of a negative power: substituting $u=tx$ in
\[\int_0^{\infty} e^{-tx}\,t^{p-1}\,dt
= \frac{1}{x^{p}}\int_0^{\infty}e^{-u}u^{p-1}\,du = \frac{\Gamma(p)}{x^{p}},
\qquad x>0 .\]
Now start from the right-hand side of the claim and use $m(-t)=E\left(e^{-tX}\right)$:
\[\frac{1}{\Gamma(p)}\int_0^{\infty} m(-t)\,t^{p-1}\,dt
= \frac{1}{\Gamma(p)}\int_0^{\infty} E\left(e^{-tX}\right) t^{p-1}\,dt
= \frac{1}{\Gamma(p)}\, E\left[\int_0^{\infty} e^{-tX}t^{p-1}\,dt\right],\]
the exchange of expectation and integral being justified by Tonelli's theorem,
since the integrand is non-negative throughout. By the displayed identity the
inner integral is $\Gamma(p)/X^{p}$, so the whole expression is
\[\frac{1}{\Gamma(p)}\,E\left[\frac{\Gamma(p)}{X^{p}}\right] = E\left(X^{-p}\right).\]

\begin{note}
The result converts a negative moment, which is usually awkward, into an
integral of the moment generating function, which is often known in closed form.
The requirement $X>0$ is essential: without it $X^{-p}$ need not be defined, and
$m(-t)$ need not be finite for all $t>0$.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1\, , \, X_2\, ,\, \cdots \, X_n\,$ be a random sample from the POI$(\theta)$ distribution. Find
\[E_{\theta}\left(X^{(k)}\right) = E_{\theta}\left[X(X-1)(X-2)\cdots (X-k+1)\right]\]
the $k^{\text{th}}$ factorial moment of $X$, and thus find an unbiased estimator of $\theta^k\,\, , \,\ k = 1\,, \, 2\, \, \cdots $
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\subhead{The factorial moment}
For $X\sim$ POI$(\theta)$ the terms with $x<k$ vanish, because the product
$x(x-1)\cdots(x-k+1)$ contains a zero factor. For $x\geq k$,
\[\frac{x(x-1)\cdots(x-k+1)}{x!} = \frac{1}{(x-k)!},\]
so
\[E_{\theta}\left(X^{(k)}\right)
= \sum_{x=k}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-\theta}\theta^{x}}{(x-k)!}
= \theta^{k}\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\theta}\theta^{y}}{y!}
= \theta^{k},\]
substituting $y=x-k$ and recognising the Poisson probabilities, which sum to one.

\subhead{An unbiased estimator}
The calculation already gives one: $X_1^{(k)}$ is unbiased for $\theta^{k}$. It
uses a single observation, so it is a poor estimator, and the sufficient
statistic $T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i$ does better. Since $T\sim$ POI$(n\theta)$, the
same identity applied to $T$ gives $E_{\theta}\left(T^{(k)}\right) = (n\theta)^{k}$,
so
\[\widehat{\theta^{k}} = \frac{T^{(k)}}{n^{k}}
= \frac{T(T-1)\cdots(T-k+1)}{n^{k}}\]
is unbiased for $\theta^{k}$. As $T$ is complete and sufficient, this is the
UMVUE by the theorem of the previous section.

For $k=1$ it reduces to $\overline{X}$, as it should. For $k=2$ it is
$T(T-1)/n^{2}$, which is \emph{not} $\overline{X}^{2}$ --- a reminder that an
unbiased estimator of $\theta$ does not give an unbiased estimator of
$\theta^{2}$ by squaring.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Unbiased Estimators}

\begin{defn}
\label{def:unbiased}
A statistic $T(X)$ is an \emph{unbiased estimator} of $\tau(\theta)$ if
$E_\theta\left[T(X)\right] = \tau(\theta)$ for all $\theta\in\Omega$. The
\emph{bias} of $T$ is $\bias_\theta(T) = E_\theta(T) - \tau(\theta)$.
\end{defn}

The requirement is for \emph{all} $\theta$: an estimator that happens to be
centred correctly at one parameter value but not at others is biased.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissonunbiased}
Let $X_i\sim\text{POI}(i\theta)$, $i = 1, 2, \dots, n$, independently. Are
\[T_1 = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\frac{X_i}{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad
T_2 = \frac{2}{n(n+1)}\sum^n_{i=1}X_i\]
unbiased estimators of $\theta$?
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Since $E(X_i) = i\theta$,
\[E(T_1) = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^n\frac{i\theta}{i} = \theta,\qquad
E(T_2) = \frac{2}{n(n+1)}\sum_{i = 1}^n i\theta = \frac{2\theta}{n(n+1)}\cdot
\frac{n(n+1)}{2} = \theta .\]
Both are unbiased. Example~\ref{ex:poissoncompare} shows they are not equally
good.
\end{solution}

Unbiasedness is not preserved by nonlinear transformations: if $T$ is unbiased
for $\theta$, then $T^2$ is in general not unbiased for $\theta^2$, since
$E(T^2) = \var(T) + \theta^2$.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:samplevariance}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be uncorrelated with $E(X_i) = \mu$ and
$\var(X_i) = \sigma^2$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] Show that $T = \frac{1}{n}\sum (X_i - \mu)^2$ is unbiased for
$\sigma^2$ when $\mu$ is known.
\item[(ii)] Show that $S^2 = \frac{1}{n - 1}\sum (X_i - \bar X)^2$ is unbiased for
$\sigma^2$ when $\mu$ is unknown.
\item[(iii)] If the $X_i$ are a random sample from $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$, show that
$S$ is not unbiased for $\sigma$, and find a multiple of $S$ that is.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $E(T) = \frac{1}{n}\sum E(X_i - \mu)^2 = \frac{1}{n}\cdot n\sigma^2 = \sigma^2$.
\item[(ii)] Write $X_i - \bar X = (X_i - \mu) - (\bar X - \mu)$. Since
$\sum (X_i - \mu) = n(\bar X - \mu)$,
\[\sum (X_i - \bar X)^2 = \sum (X_i - \mu)^2 - n(\bar X - \mu)^2 .\]
The $X_i$ are uncorrelated, so $E(\bar X - \mu)^2 = \var(\bar X) = \frac{\sigma^2}{n}$,
and
\[E\left[\sum (X_i - \bar X)^2\right] = n\sigma^2 - n\cdot\frac{\sigma^2}{n} = (n - 1)\sigma^2 .\]
Dividing by $n - 1$ gives $E(S^2) = \sigma^2$.
\item[(iii)] For a normal sample, $W = \frac{(n - 1)S^2}{\sigma^2}\sim\chi^2(n - 1)
= \text{GAM}\left(\frac{n - 1}{2}, 2\right)$. By problem~\ref{prob:gammamoments}
with $p = \frac12$,
\[E\left(W^{1/2}\right) = \frac{2^{1/2}\,\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n - 1}{2}\right)},
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
E(S) = \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n - 1}}\,E\left(W^{1/2}\right)
= \sigma\sqrt{\frac{2}{n - 1}}\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n - 1}{2}\right)} .\]
This is not $\sigma$ (for $n = 2$ it is $\sigma\sqrt{2/\pi}\approx 0.80\,\sigma$),
so $S$ is biased. The estimator
\[T = \sqrt{\frac{n - 1}{2}}\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n - 1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\, S\]
is unbiased for $\sigma$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:binomialreciprocal}
Let $X\sim\text{BIN}(n, \theta)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] Find an unbiased estimator $T(X)$ of $\theta$.
\item[(ii)] Is $\frac{1}{T(X)}$ an unbiased estimator of $\frac{1}{\theta}$?
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $T = \frac{X}{n}$, since $E(T) = \frac{n\theta}{n} = \theta$.
\item[(ii)] $\frac1T = \frac nX$ is infinite when $X = 0$, which has probability
$(1 - \theta)^n>0$. So $E\left(\frac nX\right)$ is infinite and $\frac1T$ is not
unbiased for $\frac1\theta$. In fact no statistic is: any $U(X)$ has
$E_\theta(U) = \sum_{x=0}^n U(x)\binom nx\theta^x(1-\theta)^{n-x}$, a polynomial in
$\theta$ of degree at most $n$, and $\frac1\theta$ is not a polynomial.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\subsection{Mean square error}

Unbiasedness alone does not make an estimator good.
Figure~\ref{fig:biasvariance} shows the distributions of two estimators of
$\theta$: $T_1$ is unbiased but widely spread, while $T_2$ is slightly biased but
concentrated. Which is better depends on how we measure the distance between an
estimator and the truth.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[=>Stealth]
\draw[smooth,domain = -2.5:2.5, variable = \x]plot(\x, {6*1/sqrt(2*pi)*exp(-1/2*\x*\x)});
\draw[-Stealth](-2.8,0)--(3,0)node[below]{$t_1$};
\draw[-Stealth](-2.8,0)--(-2.8,3.5);
\draw[dashed](0,0)--(0,2.4);
\draw(0,0)node[below]{$\theta$}
(0,2.4)node[above]{$f_{\theta}(t_1) = $ p.f.d of $T_1$}
(0,-1)node[below]{$E(T_1) = \theta$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\qquad
\begin{tikzpicture}
\draw[smooth,domain = -2.5:2.5, variable = \x] plot(\x, {6*1/sqrt(pi)*exp(-1/2*\x*\x)});
\draw[-Stealth](-2.8,0)--(3,0)node[below]{$t_2$};
\draw[-Stealth](-2.8,0)--(-2.8,3);
\draw[dashed](0,0)--(0,3.4);
\draw(0,0)node[below]{$\theta + \frac{1}{n}$}
(0,3.4)node[above]{$g_{\theta}(t_2) = $ p.d.f of $T_2$}
(0,-1)node[below]{$E(T_2) = \theta + \dfrac{1}{n}$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Sampling distributions of an unbiased estimator with a large variance
(left) and a biased estimator with a small variance (right).}
\label{fig:biasvariance}
\end{figure}

A \emph{loss function} $L(\theta, T)$ measures the cost of estimating $\theta$ by
$T$. Its average over the distribution of the data is the \emph{risk}
\[R(\theta, T) = E_\theta\left[L(\theta, T(X))\right] ,\]
and a good estimator has small risk. The two standard choices are
\begin{itemize}
\item squared error loss $L(\theta, T) = (T - \theta)^2$, whose risk is the
\emph{mean square error}
\[\MSE_\theta(T) = E_\theta\left[(T(X) - \theta)^2\right] ;\]
\item absolute error loss $L(\theta, T) = |T - \theta|$, whose risk is the
\emph{mean absolute error} $E_\theta|T(X) - \theta|$.
\end{itemize}
The mean square error is the one used almost always, because it splits into
variance and bias.

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:msedecomp}
Show that $\,\, \MSE _{\theta} (T) = \var _{\theta}(T) + \left(\bias _{\theta}(T)\right)^2\,$ where $\bias _{\theta}(T) = E_{\theta}\left(T(X) - \theta\right)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Write $\mu_T = E_{\theta}\left(T\right)$ and add and subtract it inside the
square:
\begin{align*}
\MSE _{\theta}(T) &= E_{\theta}\left[\left(T-\theta\right)^{2}\right]
 = E_{\theta}\left[\left\{\left(T-\mu_T\right) + \left(\mu_T-\theta\right)\right\}^{2}\right]\\
 &= E_{\theta}\left[\left(T-\mu_T\right)^{2}\right]
  + 2\left(\mu_T-\theta\right)E_{\theta}\left[T-\mu_T\right]
  + \left(\mu_T-\theta\right)^{2}.
\end{align*}
The middle term vanishes because $E_{\theta}\left[T-\mu_T\right]=0$, and
$\mu_T-\theta$ is a constant that may be taken outside the expectation. The first
term is $\var _{\theta}(T)$ and the last is $\left[\bias _{\theta}(T)\right]^{2}$,
giving
\[\MSE _{\theta}(T) = \var _{\theta}(T) + \left[\bias _{\theta}(T)\right]^{2}.\]

\begin{note}
The decomposition is the reason unbiasedness is a convenience rather than a
virtue. Setting the bias to zero removes one term, but nothing guarantees the
sum is smallest there --- and the next two problems produce estimators that are
biased and beat the unbiased ones outright.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:uniformmse}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{UNIF}(0, \theta)$. Compare the
mean square errors of
\[T_1 = 2\bar X,\qquad T_2 = X_{(n)},\qquad T_3 = (n + 1)X_{(1)} .\]
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
We use $\MSE(T) = \var(T) + \left[\bias(T)\right]^2$ (problem~\ref{prob:msedecomp}).
For one observation $E(X) = \frac\theta2$, $\var(X) = \frac{\theta^2}{12}$ and
$F(x) = \frac x\theta$ on $(0, \theta)$.

\subhead{$T_1 = 2\bar X$}
$E(T_1) = 2\cdot\frac\theta2 = \theta$, so $T_1$ is unbiased and
\[\MSE(T_1) = \var(2\bar X) = \frac{4}{n}\cdot\frac{\theta^2}{12} = \frac{\theta^2}{3n} .\]

\subhead{$T_2 = X_{(n)}$}
$Y = X_{(n)}$ has distribution function $P(Y\le y) = \left[F(y)\right]^n =
\left(\frac y\theta\right)^n$, so
\[f_Y(y) = \frac{n y^{n-1}}{\theta^n},\qquad 0<y<\theta .\]
Then $E(Y) = \frac{n\theta}{n + 1}$ and $E(Y^2) = \frac{n\theta^2}{n + 2}$, so
\[\bias(T_2) = -\frac{\theta}{n + 1},\qquad
\var(T_2) = \frac{n\theta^2}{n + 2} - \frac{n^2\theta^2}{(n + 1)^2} = \frac{n\theta^2}{(n + 1)^2(n + 2)},\]
and
\[\MSE(T_2) = \frac{n\theta^2}{(n + 1)^2(n + 2)} + \frac{\theta^2}{(n + 1)^2}
= \frac{(2n + 2)\theta^2}{(n + 1)^2(n + 2)} = \frac{2\theta^2}{(n + 1)(n + 2)} .\]

\subhead{$T_3 = (n + 1)X_{(1)}$}
$Y = X_{(1)}$ has $P(Y>y) = \left[1 - F(y)\right]^n = \left(1 - \frac y\theta\right)^n$,
so
\[f_Y(y) = \frac n\theta\left(1 - \frac y\theta\right)^{n - 1},\qquad 0<y<\theta .\]
With the substitution $u = \frac y\theta$ the moments are beta integrals:
$E(Y) = \frac{\theta}{n + 1}$ and $E(Y^2) = \frac{2\theta^2}{(n + 1)(n + 2)}$. So
$E(T_3) = \theta$, $T_3$ is unbiased, and
\[\MSE(T_3) = (n + 1)^2\var(Y) = (n + 1)^2\left[\frac{2\theta^2}{(n + 1)(n + 2)} - \frac{\theta^2}{(n + 1)^2}\right]
= \frac{n\theta^2}{n + 2} .\]

\subhead{Comparison}
For every $n$,
\[\frac{2\theta^2}{(n + 1)(n + 2)}\ \le\ \frac{\theta^2}{3n}\ \le\ \frac{n\theta^2}{n + 2} .\]
The first inequality is $(n - 1)(n - 2)\ge 0$ and the second is $n + 2\le 3n^2$.
All three coincide at $n = 1$, and $T_1$ and $T_2$ tie at $n = 2$; for $n\ge 3$
the inequalities are strict. The biased estimator
$X_{(n)}$ is best: its mean square error falls like $\frac{1}{n^2}$, against
$\frac1n$ for $2\bar X$, while $T_3$ does not improve at all as $n$ grows. It uses
only the smallest observation, which carries little information about the upper
end point.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\, X_1\, , \, X_2\, , \, \cdots \, , \, X_n\,$ be a random sample from a UNIF$(0\, , \, \theta)$ distribution. Consider the following estimators of $\theta$:
\begin{align*}
T & = X_{(1)}  + X_{(n)}\\
R & = X_{(n)} - X_{(1)}
\end{align*}
which estimator is unbiased?
 Compare the MSE's of these estimators.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
For a sample from UNIF$(0,\theta)$ the extreme order statistics satisfy
\[E\left(X_{(1)}\right) = \frac{\theta}{n+1},\qquad
E\left(X_{(n)}\right) = \frac{n\theta}{n+1},\]
\[\var\left(X_{(1)}\right) = \var\left(X_{(n)}\right) = \frac{n\theta^{2}}{(n+1)^{2}(n+2)},
\qquad
\cov\left(X_{(1)},X_{(n)}\right) = \frac{\theta^{2}}{(n+1)^{2}(n+2)} .\]

\subhead{Which is unbiased}
\[E(T) = \frac{\theta}{n+1} + \frac{n\theta}{n+1} = \theta ,\qquad
E(R) = \frac{n\theta}{n+1} - \frac{\theta}{n+1} = \frac{(n-1)\theta}{n+1}.\]
So $T$ is unbiased and $R$ is not; $R$ has bias $-2\theta/(n+1)$, which is
negative because the range of a sample can never reach the full length $\theta$.

\subhead{Comparing mean square errors}
Adding the variances with the covariance term,
\[\var(T) = 2\var\left(X_{(1)}\right) + 2\cov\left(X_{(1)},X_{(n)}\right)
= \frac{2\theta^{2}}{(n+1)(n+2)},\]
\[\var(R) = 2\var\left(X_{(1)}\right) - 2\cov\left(X_{(1)},X_{(n)}\right)
= \frac{2(n-1)\theta^{2}}{(n+1)^{2}(n+2)} .\]
Since $T$ is unbiased, $\MSE (T) = \var(T)$. For $R$, adding the squared bias,
\[\MSE (R) = \frac{2(n-1)\theta^{2}}{(n+1)^{2}(n+2)} + \frac{4\theta^{2}}{(n+1)^{2}}
= \frac{6\theta^{2}}{(n+1)(n+2)} .\]
Therefore
\[\frac{\MSE (R)}{\MSE (T)} = 3 \qquad\text{for every } n .\]
$T$ beats $R$ by a factor of three at every sample size. The ratio does not
depend on $n$ or on $\theta$, so no sample size rescues $R$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\, X_1\, , \, X_2\, , \, \cdots \, , \, X_n\,$ be a random sample from the $N(\mu\, , \, \sigma^2)$ distribution. Consider the following estimators of $\sigma^2$:
\[T_1= \frac{n - 1}{n}S^2\quad , \quad  T_2 = \frac{n - 1}{n + 1}\, S^2\quad \text{where}\quad S^2 = \frac{1}{n - 1}\sum^n_{i = 1}\left(X_i - \overline{X}\right)^2\]
Compare the MSE's of these estimators by grouping them as a function of $\sigma^2$ for $n = 5$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Under normality $W = (n-1)S^{2}/\sigma^{2} \sim \chi^{2}_{(n-1)}$, so
$E(W)=n-1$ and $\var(W)=2(n-1)$. Both estimators have the form
$c\,\sigma^{2}W$: for $T_1$, $c=1/n$; for $T_2$, $c=1/(n+1)$. For such an
estimator
\[\MSE  = c^{2}\sigma^{4}\var(W) + \left[c\,\sigma^{2}E(W) - \sigma^{2}\right]^{2}
= \sigma^{4}\left\{2(n-1)c^{2} + \left[c(n-1)-1\right]^{2}\right\}.\]
Substituting the two values of $c$,
\[\MSE (T_1) = \frac{(2n-1)\,\sigma^{4}}{n^{2}},\qquad
\MSE (T_2) = \frac{2\,\sigma^{4}}{n+1} .\]
For comparison the unbiased $S^{2}$ itself has $\MSE (S^{2}) = 2\sigma^{4}/(n-1)$.

\subhead{At $n=5$}
\[\MSE (T_1) = \frac{9}{25}\sigma^{4} = 0.360\,\sigma^{4},\qquad
\MSE (T_2) = \frac{1}{3}\sigma^{4} \approx 0.333\,\sigma^{4},\qquad
\MSE (S^{2}) = \frac{1}{2}\sigma^{4} .\]
The ordering is $\MSE (T_2) < \MSE (T_1) < \MSE (S^{2})$, and it holds for every
$n$, not only $n=5$.

\begin{remark}
Minimising $2(n-1)c^{2} + \left[c(n-1)-1\right]^{2}$ over $c$ gives
$c = 1/(n+1)$ exactly, so $T_2$ is not merely better than the other two --- it is
the best estimator of the form $cS^{2}$ there is. The unbiased choice
$c=1/(n-1)$ is the worst of the three. This is the clearest illustration in the
chapter of the point made in the previous problem: insisting on unbiasedness
costs accuracy.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:normalmse}
Let $X\sim N(\theta, 1)$. Compare the mean square errors of
$T_1 = X$, $T_2 = \frac X2$ and $T_3 = 0$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$T_1$ is unbiased with variance $1$, so $\MSE(T_1) = 1$. $T_2$ has mean
$\frac\theta2$, bias $-\frac\theta2$ and variance $\frac14$, so
$\MSE(T_2) = \frac14 + \frac{\theta^2}{4} = \frac{1 + \theta^2}{4}$. $T_3$ has
variance $0$ and bias $-\theta$, so $\MSE(T_3) = \theta^2$.

The three curves are drawn in figure~\ref{fig:msecurves}. $T_3$ is best for
$|\theta|<\frac{1}{\sqrt3}$, $T_2$ for $\frac{1}{\sqrt3}<|\theta|<\sqrt3$, and $T_1$
for $|\theta|>\sqrt3$. No estimator is best for every $\theta$; even the absurd
estimator $T_3 = 0$, which ignores the data, wins when $\theta$ is near $0$.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[
    width=10cm, height=6cm,
    xlabel={$\theta$}, ylabel={MSE},
    xmin=-2.5, xmax=2.5, ymin=0, ymax=2.2,
    axis lines=left, grid=major, grid style={draw=gray!25},
    legend pos=north east, legend style={font=\footnotesize, draw=none, fill=none}]
\addplot[very thick, blue, domain=-2.5:2.5] {1};
\addplot[very thick, orange!80!black, dashed, smooth, samples=80, domain=-2.5:2.5] {(1+x^2)/4};
\addplot[very thick, green!50!black, dotted, smooth, samples=80, domain=-1.48:1.48] {x^2};
\legend{$T_1 = X$, $T_2 = X/2$, $T_3 = 0$}
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Mean square errors of three estimators of the mean of $N(\theta, 1)$.
Each is best on some range of $\theta$.}
\label{fig:msecurves}
\end{figure}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissoncompare}
Compare the variances of the two unbiased estimators $T_1$ and $T_2$ of
example~\ref{ex:poissonunbiased}.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Since $\var(X_i) = i\theta$ and the $X_i$ are independent,
\[\var(T_1) = \frac{1}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{i\theta}{i^2} = \frac{\theta}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1i,
\qquad
\var(T_2) = \frac{4}{n^2(n + 1)^2}\sum_{i=1}^n i\theta = \frac{2\theta}{n(n + 1)} .\]
For $n = 10$, $\sum_{i=1}^{10}\frac1i = 2.929$, so $\var(T_1) = 0.0293\,\theta$
and $\var(T_2) = 0.0182\,\theta$. In general $\var(T_1)$ falls like
$\frac{\ln n}{n^2}$ and $\var(T_2)$ like $\frac{2}{n^2}$, so $T_2$ is the better
estimator for large samples. Example~\ref{ex:poissonumvue} shows that $T_2$ is in
fact the best unbiased estimator.
\end{solution}

Example~\ref{ex:normalmse} shows that we cannot hope for an estimator with the
smallest mean square error for every $\theta$: the constant estimator $T = \theta_0$
has zero error at $\theta = \theta_0$, so a uniformly best estimator would need
zero error everywhere. To make progress we restrict the class of estimators and
look for the best one within it. The most common restriction is to unbiased
estimators, for which the mean square error is the variance.

\subsection{Uniformly Minimum Variance Unbiased Estimation}

\begin{defn}
\label{def:umvue}
An estimator $T(X)$ is a \emph{uniformly minimum variance unbiased estimator}
(UMVUE) of $\tau(\theta)$ if
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] it is unbiased for $\tau(\theta)$, and
\item[(ii)] $\var_\theta(T)\le\var_\theta(U)$ for every $\theta\in\Omega$ and every
other unbiased estimator $U$ of $\tau(\theta)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{defn}

The word \emph{uniformly} means that one estimator has the smallest variance at
every $\theta$ simultaneously. The rest of this chapter develops the tools
(sufficiency, completeness and the exponential family) that let us find UMVUEs,
and chapter~\ref{ch:mle} gives a lower bound for their variance.

\section{Sufficiency}

A sufficient statistic for $\theta$ captures all the information about $\theta$
that the sample contains. Once its value is known, the remaining detail of the
sample is pure noise as far as $\theta$ is concerned.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:sufficient}
A statistic $T(X)$ is \emph{sufficient} for the model
$\{f_\theta(x) : \theta\in\Omega\}$ if the conditional distribution of
$X = (X_1, \dots, X_n)$ given $T(X) = t$ does not depend on $\theta$, for every
value $t$.
\end{defn}

The definition suggests the following principle, which is not a theorem but a
statement of how inference ought to be done.

\begin{remark}[The sufficiency principle]
If $T(X)$ is sufficient for $\theta$, then any inference about $\theta$ should
depend on the sample only through $T(X)$: if $x$ and $y$ are sample points with
$T(x) = T(y)$, the inference should be the same whether $x$ or $y$ was observed.
\end{remark}

The justification is that, given $T = t$, the rest of the sample could be
generated by a random mechanism that does not involve $\theta$ at all. A second
statistician who knew only $t$ could simulate a sample with exactly the same
distribution as the original.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissonsufficient}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{POI}(\theta)$. Show that
$T = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i$ is sufficient.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
The joint probability function is
\[f_\theta(x_1, \dots, x_n) = \prod^n_{i = 1}\frac{e^{-\theta}\theta^{x_i}}{x_i!}
= \frac{e^{-n\theta}\theta^{\sum x_i}}{\prod x_i!},\qquad x_i = 0, 1, 2, \dots ,\]
and $T\sim\text{POI}(n\theta)$. The event $\{X = x\}$ is contained in
$\{T = t\}$ when $\sum x_i = t$, and is disjoint from it otherwise. So for
$\sum x_i = t$,
\[P(X = x\mid T = t) = \frac{P(X = x)}{P(T = t)}
= \frac{e^{-n\theta}\theta^{t}}{\prod x_i!}\cdot\frac{t!}{e^{-n\theta}(n\theta)^t}
= \frac{t!}{x_1!\,x_2!\cdots x_n!}\left(\frac1n\right)^t ,\]
and the probability is $0$ otherwise. This does not involve $\theta$, so $T$ is
sufficient. In fact, given $T = t$, the sample has the multinomial distribution
$\text{MULT}\left(t; \frac1n, \dots, \frac1n\right)$: the $t$ events are spread
over the $n$ observations at random.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:normalsufficient}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\theta, \sigma^2)$ with $\sigma^2$
known. Show that $T = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i$ is sufficient for $\theta$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Here $T\sim N(n\theta, n\sigma^2)$. Because $X$ determines $T$, the conditional
density of $X$ given $T = t$ on the set $\{\sum x_i = t\}$ is the ratio of the joint
density of $X$ to the density of $T$:
\[\frac{(2\pi\sigma^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum(x_i - \theta)^2\right)}
{(2\pi n\sigma^2)^{-1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2n\sigma^2}(t - n\theta)^2\right)} .\]
Expanding, $\sum (x_i - \theta)^2 = \sum x_i^2 - 2\theta t + n\theta^2$ and
$\frac1n(t - n\theta)^2 = \frac{t^2}{n} - 2\theta t + n\theta^2$. The terms in
$\theta$ cancel, leaving
\[\frac{\sqrt n}{(2\pi\sigma^2)^{(n-1)/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\left[\sum x_i^2 - \frac{t^2}{n}\right]\right)
= \frac{\sqrt n}{(2\pi\sigma^2)^{(n-1)/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum (x_i - \bar x)^2\right),\]
which does not depend on $\theta$. So $T$ is sufficient.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\, X_1\, , \, X_2\, , \, \cdots \, , \, X_n\,$  be a random sample from $X\thicksim BIN(1,\theta)\,$ and let $\, T = \sum^n_{i = 1} X_i$
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Show that $T$ is a sufficient statistic for this model.
\item[(b).] Explain how you would generate data with the same distribution as the original data using only the value of the sufficient statistic.
\item[(c).] Let $\, U = \begin{cases}
U\left(X_1\right) = 1\, & \text{if}\  X_1 = 1\\
0 & \text{otherwise}
\end{cases}
$\\
Find $\, E_{\theta}\left(U\right)\, $ and $\, E\left(U \mid T = t\right)$
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\subhead{(a)}
The joint probability function is
\[f_{\theta}(x_1,\dots,x_n) = \prod_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{x_i}(1-\theta)^{1-x_i}
= \theta^{t}(1-\theta)^{n-t},\qquad t=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_i ,\]
which depends on the data only through $t$. Taking $g(t,\theta)=\theta^{t}(1-\theta)^{n-t}$
and $h(x)=1$, the factorisation criterion gives sufficiency of $T$.

\subhead{(b)}
Given $T=t$, every arrangement of $t$ ones among $n$ positions has the same
conditional probability, namely $1/\binom{n}{t}$: the conditional probability of
a particular $x$ with $\sum x_i=t$ is
\[\frac{\theta^{t}(1-\theta)^{n-t}}{\binom{n}{t}\theta^{t}(1-\theta)^{n-t}}
= \frac{1}{\binom{n}{t}},\]
free of $\theta$. So it is enough to choose a subset of $\{1,\dots,n\}$ of size
$t$ uniformly at random and put a one in those positions and a zero elsewhere.
The result has exactly the distribution of the original sample, and $\theta$ was
never needed. That is what sufficiency means operationally: $T$ carries all the
information about $\theta$, and the rest of the data can be regenerated by
randomisation alone.

\subhead{(c)}
Here $U=X_1$, so
\[E_{\theta}(U) = P_{\theta}\left(X_1=1\right) = \theta .\]
For the conditional expectation, $U$ is an indicator, so
\begin{align*}
E\left(U \mid T=t\right) &= P\left(X_1=1 \mid T=t\right)\\
&= \frac{P\left(X_1=1,\ \sum_{i\geq 2}X_i = t-1\right)}{P(T=t)}\\
&= \frac{\theta\,\binom{n-1}{t-1}\theta^{t-1}(1-\theta)^{n-t}}
       {\binom{n}{t}\theta^{t}(1-\theta)^{n-t}}\\
&= \frac{\binom{n-1}{t-1}}{\binom{n}{t}} = \frac{t}{n}.
\end{align*}
So $E\left(U\mid T\right) = T/n = \overline{X}$.

\begin{note}
This is the Rao--Blackwell theorem in miniature. $U=X_1$ is unbiased for
$\theta$ but throws away $n-1$ observations; conditioning it on the sufficient
statistic returns $\overline{X}$, which is unbiased, has variance
$\theta(1-\theta)/n$ instead of $\theta(1-\theta)$, and is the UMVUE.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

To use the definition we must first guess the sufficient statistic and then
compute a conditional distribution. The next theorem lets us read a sufficient
statistic straight off the joint density.

\subsection{The Factorisation Criterion}

\begin{thm}[Factorisation criterion]
\label{thm:factorisation}
A statistic $T(X)$ is sufficient for $\{f_\theta(x) : \theta\in\Omega\}$ if and
only if there are non-negative functions $g$ and $h$ such that
\[f_\theta(x) = g\left(T(x); \theta\right) h(x)\qquad\text{for all } x \text{ and all } \theta\in\Omega .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
We give the proof for discrete $X$; the continuous case is the same in spirit but
needs measure theory.

\subhead{Only if}
Suppose $T$ is sufficient. For any $x$, put $t = T(x)$. The event $\{X = x\}$ is
contained in $\{T = t\}$, so
\[f_\theta(x) = P_\theta(X = x) = P_\theta(X = x,\ T = t)
= \underbrace{P(X = x\mid T = t)}_{h(x)}\ \underbrace{P_\theta(T = t)}_{g(t;\theta)} .\]
The first factor does not depend on $\theta$ by sufficiency and is a function of
$x$; the second depends on $x$ only through $t = T(x)$.

\subhead{If}
Suppose $f_\theta(x) = g(T(x);\theta)h(x)$. For a value $t$ with
$P_\theta(T = t)>0$,
\[P_\theta(T = t) = \sum_{y : T(y) = t} g(t;\theta)h(y) = g(t;\theta)\sum_{y : T(y) = t} h(y) .\]
Hence for $x$ with $T(x) = t$,
\[P_\theta(X = x\mid T = t) = \frac{g(t;\theta)h(x)}{g(t;\theta)\sum_{y : T(y) = t} h(y)}
= \frac{h(x)}{\sum_{y : T(y) = t} h(y)},\]
and for $x$ with $T(x)\ne t$ the conditional probability is $0$. Neither depends on
$\theta$, so $T$ is sufficient.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:normalfactor}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$ with both
parameters unknown. Show that $\left(\sum X_i, \sum X_i^2\right)$ and
$(\bar X, S^2)$ are both sufficient.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
With $\theta = (\mu, \sigma^2)$,
\begin{align*}
f_\theta(x) &= (2\pi\sigma^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum (x_i - \mu)^2\right)\\
&= \underbrace{(\sigma^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{\sum x_i^2 - 2\mu\sum x_i + n\mu^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)}_{g(T(x);\,\theta)}\ \underbrace{(2\pi)^{-n/2}}_{h(x)},
\end{align*}
so $T = \left(\sum X_i, \sum X_i^2\right)$ is sufficient by
theorem~\ref{thm:factorisation}. Alternatively, writing
$x_i - \mu = (x_i - \bar x) + (\bar x - \mu)$ and using $\sum (x_i - \bar x) = 0$,
\[\sum (x_i - \mu)^2 = \sum (x_i - \bar x)^2 + n(\bar x - \mu)^2 = (n - 1)s^2 + n(\bar x - \mu)^2,\]
so
\[f_\theta(x) = (\sigma^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{(n - 1)s^2 + n(\bar x - \mu)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)\cdot(2\pi)^{-n/2},\]
and $(\bar X, S^2)$ is sufficient too. The two statistics are functions of each
other, as corollary~\ref{cor:onetoone} below explains.
\end{solution}

Sufficient statistics are not unique: the whole sample $X$ is always sufficient
(take $T(x) = x$ and $h = 1$), and so is any one-to-one function of a sufficient
statistic.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:weibullsufficient}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the Weibull distribution with
p.d.f. $f_\theta(x) = \theta x^{\theta - 1}e^{-x^\theta}$, $x>0$, $\theta>0$. Find a
sufficient statistic.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[f_\theta(x_1, \dots, x_n) = \theta^n\left(\prod x_i\right)^{\theta - 1}\exp\left(-\sum x_i^\theta\right).\]
The term $\sum x_i^\theta$ cannot be written in terms of a fixed finite set of
summaries of the data for all $\theta$: it changes with $\theta$ in a way that
depends on every observation. It is, however, symmetric in the $x_i$, so it is a
function of the order statistics. Taking $h = 1$ and $g$ the whole expression,
$T(X) = (X_{(1)}, \dots, X_{(n)})$ is sufficient. Apart from forgetting the order in
which the observations arrived, no reduction of the data is possible here.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:uniformsufficient}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{UNIF}(0, \theta)$. Find a
sufficient statistic for $\theta$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Write $I(A)$ for the indicator of the event $A$, equal to $1$ if $A$ holds and
$0$ otherwise. Each density is $\frac1\theta I(0<x_i<\theta)$, and all $n$
observations lie in $(0, \theta)$ exactly when the smallest is positive and the
largest is below $\theta$. So
\[f_\theta(x) = \frac{1}{\theta^n}\prod_{i=1}^n I(0<x_i<\theta)
= \underbrace{\frac{1}{\theta^n}I\left(x_{(n)}<\theta\right)}_{g(x_{(n)};\,\theta)}\ \underbrace{I\left(x_{(1)}>0\right)}_{h(x)},\]
and $T = X_{(n)}$ is sufficient. When the support depends on the parameter, the
indicator functions must be kept in the factorisation; dropping them gives the
wrong answer.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\, X_1\, , \, X_2\, , \, \cdots \, , \, X_n\,$  be a random sample from  $X\thicksim N(\mu, \sigma^2)\,$ with $\mu$ known. Find a sufficient statistic for $\sigma^2$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
With $\mu$ known the joint density is
\[f_{\sigma^{2}}(x) = \left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{-n/2}
\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_i-\mu\right)^{2}\right\}.\]
The data enter only through $\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_i-\mu)^{2}$, so with
\[g(t,\sigma^{2}) = \left(2\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{-n/2}e^{-t/2\sigma^{2}},
\qquad h(x)=1,\]
the factorisation criterion gives that
\[T(X) = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(X_i-\mu\right)^{2}\]
is sufficient for $\sigma^{2}$.

\begin{note}
Note that $T$ is not $ (n-1)S^{2}$. Knowing $\mu$ means the deviations are taken
about the true mean rather than about $\overline{X}$, and $T/\sigma^{2}\sim\chi^{2}_{(n)}$
rather than $\chi^{2}_{(n-1)}$ --- one degree of freedom is recovered, which is
exactly the information that would otherwise have been spent estimating $\mu$.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\, X_1\, , \, X_2\, , \, \cdots \, , \, X_n\,$  be a random sample from the $EXP(1,\theta)$ distribution. Find a sufficient statistic for this model.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The EXP$(1,\theta)$ density is $f_{\theta}(x)=e^{-(x-\theta)}$ for $x>\theta$
and zero otherwise, so the joint density is
\[f_{\theta}(x) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} e^{-(x_i-\theta)}\,I\left(x_i>\theta\right)
= \underbrace{e^{n\theta}\,I\left(x_{(1)}>\theta\right)}_{g\left(x_{(1)},\,\theta\right)}\ \cdot\
\underbrace{e^{-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_i}}_{h(x)} .\]
Every $x_i$ exceeds $\theta$ exactly when the smallest of them does, which is
what turns $n$ indicator functions into one. By the factorisation criterion
$T(X)=X_{(1)}$ is sufficient.

\begin{remark}
The parameter here is a threshold rather than a location in the usual sense, and
the sufficient statistic is an order statistic rather than a sum. Whenever the
\emph{support} depends on the parameter, the indicator function carries the
information and an extreme order statistic is what the factorisation produces.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\begin{coro}
\label{cor:onetoone}
If $T(X)$ is sufficient for $\{f_\theta(x) : \theta\in\Omega\}$, so is
$T^*(X) = r(T(X))$ for any one-to-one function $r$.
\end{coro}

\begin{proof}
By theorem~\ref{thm:factorisation}, $f_\theta(x) = g(T(x);\theta)h(x)$. Since $r$ is
one-to-one, $T(x) = r^{-1}(T^*(x))$, so
\[f_\theta(x) = g\left(r^{-1}(T^*(x));\theta\right)h(x) = g^*\left(T^*(x);\theta\right)h(x)\]
with $g^*(s;\theta) = g(r^{-1}(s);\theta)$. By the theorem again, $T^*$ is
sufficient.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:gammasufficient}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{GAM}(\alpha, \beta)$ with
both parameters unknown. Find a two-dimensional sufficient statistic.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[f_\theta(x) = \prod_{i=1}^n\frac{x_i^{\alpha - 1}e^{-x_i/\beta}}{\beta^\alpha\Gamma(\alpha)}
= \left[\beta^\alpha\Gamma(\alpha)\right]^{-n}\left(\prod x_i\right)^{\alpha - 1}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}\sum x_i\right),\qquad x_i>0 .\]
With $h = 1$ this is a function of $\theta$ and of $\left(\prod X_i, \sum X_i\right)$
only, so that pair is sufficient. By corollary~\ref{cor:onetoone} so are
$\left(\sum\ln X_i, \sum X_i\right)$ and the pair formed by the geometric and
arithmetic means.
\end{solution}

\section{Minimal Sufficiency}

The point of a sufficient statistic is to reduce the data without losing
information about $\theta$. Since there are many sufficient statistics, we want
one that achieves the greatest reduction.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:minimal}
A sufficient statistic $T(X)$ is \emph{minimal sufficient} for
$\{f_\theta(x) : \theta\in\Omega\}$ if for every other sufficient statistic $U(X)$
there is a function $r$ with $T(X) = r(U(X))$.
\end{defn}

So a minimal sufficient statistic can be computed from any other sufficient
statistic: it is the coarsest summary of the data that is still sufficient.
The following theorem gives a practical way to find one.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:minimalratio}
Suppose there is a statistic $T(X)$ such that, for any two sample points $x$ and
$y$, the ratio $\frac{f_\theta(x)}{f_\theta(y)}$ is constant as a function of
$\theta$ if and only if $T(x) = T(y)$. Then $T(X)$ is minimal sufficient.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For simplicity assume $f_\theta(x)>0$ for all $x$ in the sample space and all
$\theta$.

\subhead{$T$ is sufficient}
For each value $t$ of $T$ choose one sample point $x_t$ with $T(x_t) = t$. For any
$x$, the points $x$ and $x_{T(x)}$ have the same value of $T$, so by hypothesis
$h(x) = \frac{f_\theta(x)}{f_\theta(x_{T(x)})}$ does not depend on $\theta$. Then
\[f_\theta(x) = f_\theta\left(x_{T(x)}\right)h(x) = g(T(x);\theta)h(x)\]
with $g(t;\theta) = f_\theta(x_t)$, and $T$ is sufficient by
theorem~\ref{thm:factorisation}.

\subhead{$T$ is minimal}
Let $U$ be any sufficient statistic, so $f_\theta(x) = g'(U(x);\theta)h'(x)$. If
$U(x) = U(y)$, then
\[\frac{f_\theta(x)}{f_\theta(y)} = \frac{g'(U(x);\theta)h'(x)}{g'(U(y);\theta)h'(y)} = \frac{h'(x)}{h'(y)},\]
which does not depend on $\theta$, and so $T(x) = T(y)$ by hypothesis. Thus $T$
takes the same value wherever $U$ does, that is, $T$ is a function of $U$.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:powerminimal}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $f_\theta(x) = \theta x^{\theta - 1}$,
$0<x<1$, $\theta>0$. Find a minimal sufficient statistic.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$f_\theta(x) = \theta^n\left(\prod x_i\right)^{\theta - 1}$, so for two sample points
$x$ and $y$,
\[\frac{f_\theta(x)}{f_\theta(y)} = \left(\frac{\prod x_i}{\prod y_i}\right)^{\theta - 1} .\]
This is constant in $\theta$ if and only if $\prod x_i = \prod y_i$. By
theorem~\ref{thm:minimalratio}, $T(X) = \prod X_i$ is minimal sufficient, and so is
$\sum\ln X_i$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:normalthetaminimal}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\theta, \theta^2)$, $\theta\ne 0$.
Find a minimal sufficient statistic.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[f_\theta(x) = (2\pi\theta^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\theta^2}\left[\sum x_i^2 - 2\theta\sum x_i + n\theta^2\right]\right),\]
so
\[\frac{f_\theta(x)}{f_\theta(y)} = \exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\theta^2}\left[\sum x_i^2 - \sum y_i^2\right]
+ \frac1\theta\left[\sum x_i - \sum y_i\right]\right).\]
The functions $\frac{1}{\theta^2}$ and $\frac1\theta$ are linearly independent, so the
exponent is constant in $\theta$ only if both brackets vanish. The ratio is
constant if and only if $\sum x_i^2 = \sum y_i^2$ and $\sum x_i = \sum y_i$, and
$T(X) = \left(\sum X_i, \sum X_i^2\right)$ is minimal sufficient. Although there is
only one parameter, the minimal sufficient statistic is two-dimensional.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:uniformminimal}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{UNIF}(0, \theta)$. Show that
$X_{(n)}$ is minimal sufficient.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Here the density can be zero, so we apply the idea of theorem~\ref{thm:minimalratio}
directly. For sample points $x$ and $y$ with positive entries,
\[\frac{f_\theta(x)}{f_\theta(y)} = \frac{I\left(x_{(n)}<\theta\right)}{I\left(y_{(n)}<\theta\right)} .\]
If $x_{(n)} = y_{(n)}$, numerator and denominator are equal for every $\theta$, and
the ratio is $1$ wherever it is defined. If $x_{(n)}>y_{(n)}$, the ratio is $1$ for
$\theta>x_{(n)}$ but $0$ for $y_{(n)}<\theta\le x_{(n)}$, so it depends on $\theta$;
the case $x_{(n)}<y_{(n)}$ is symmetric. Hence the ratio is free of $\theta$
exactly when $x_{(n)} = y_{(n)}$, and $X_{(n)}$ is minimal sufficient. Problem
\ref{prob:uniformminimal} gives a direct proof from the definition.
\end{solution}

In examples~\ref{ex:poissonsufficient}, \ref{ex:normalfactor} and
\ref{ex:uniformsufficient} the same argument shows that the sufficient statistics
found ($\sum X_i$, $\left(\sum X_i, \sum X_i^2\right)$ and $X_{(n)}$) are minimal.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:logisticminimal}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the logistic distribution with
p.d.f. $f_\theta(x) = \frac{e^{-(x - \theta)}}{\left(1 + e^{-(x - \theta)}\right)^2}$,
$-\infty<x<\infty$. Show that the order statistics are minimal sufficient.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[\frac{f_\theta(x)}{f_\theta(y)} = \exp\left(\sum y_i - \sum x_i\right)
\prod_{i=1}^n\left(\frac{1 + e^{-(y_i - \theta)}}{1 + e^{-(x_i - \theta)}}\right)^2 .\]
If the $x_i$ are a rearrangement of the $y_i$ the ratio is $1$. Conversely, put
$u = e^\theta$; the ratio is constant in $\theta$ only if
$\prod (1 + u e^{-y_i})^2$ and $\prod (1 + u e^{-x_i})^2$ are proportional as
polynomials in $u$. Two polynomials that are proportional have the same roots, and
the roots here are $-e^{y_i}$ and $-e^{x_i}$, so the sets $\{x_i\}$ and $\{y_i\}$
agree. Hence the ratio is constant if and only if $x$ and $y$ have the same order
statistics, and $(X_{(1)}, \dots, X_{(n)})$ is minimal sufficient. Even for a
one-parameter location family, no reduction beyond the order statistics may be
possible.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\, X_1\, , \, X_2\, , \, \cdots \, , \, X_n\,$ be a random sample from the $UNIF(\theta, \theta + 1)\,$ distribution. Find  a minimal sufficient statistic for $\, \{f_{\theta}(x)\, :\, \theta \in \Omega\}$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The joint density is one exactly when every observation lies in
$(\theta,\theta+1)$, that is
\[f_{\theta}(x) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} I\left(\theta<x_i<\theta+1\right)
= I\left(x_{(n)}-1 < \theta < x_{(1)}\right),\]
and zero otherwise. Sufficiency of $T=\left(X_{(1)},X_{(n)}\right)$ is immediate
from this display.

For minimality use the ratio criterion. For two samples $x$ and $y$ in the
support,
\[\frac{f_{\theta}(x)}{f_{\theta}(y)}
= \frac{I\left(x_{(n)}-1<\theta<x_{(1)}\right)}{I\left(y_{(n)}-1<\theta<y_{(1)}\right)} .\]
This ratio is a constant function of $\theta$ precisely when the two intervals
$\left(x_{(n)}-1,\ x_{(1)}\right)$ and $\left(y_{(n)}-1,\ y_{(1)}\right)$
coincide, and two intervals coincide exactly when their endpoints agree, that is
when $x_{(1)}=y_{(1)}$ and $x_{(n)}=y_{(n)}$. Hence
\[T(X) = \left(X_{(1)},\, X_{(n)}\right)\]
is minimal sufficient.

\begin{remark}
The parameter is one-dimensional and the minimal sufficient statistic is
two-dimensional. There is no contradiction --- minimal means no further
reduction is possible, not that the dimension matches. It also shows why this
model is a standard source of counterexamples: the same $T$ is minimal
sufficient but, as the next problem in the completeness section shows, it is not
complete.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\, X_1\, , \, X_2\, , \, \cdots \, , \, X_n\,$ be a random sample from the $EXP(1, \theta)$ distribution. Show that $X_{(1)}$ is sufficient statistic for the model and find the conditional distribution for $\, X_1\, , \, X_2\, , \, \cdots \, , \, X_n\,$ given $X_{(1)} = t$. Prove that $X_{(1)}$ is a minimal sufficient statistic.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\subhead{Sufficiency}
As in the previous section, the joint density factorises as
\[f_{\theta}(x) = e^{n\theta}\,I\left(x_{(1)}>\theta\right)\cdot e^{-\sum_i x_i},\]
so $X_{(1)}$ is sufficient.

\subhead{The conditional distribution}
The density of $X_{(1)}$ is that of the minimum of $n$ independent shifted
exponentials,
\[f_{X_{(1)}}(t) = n\,e^{-n(t-\theta)},\qquad t>\theta ,\]
so on the set where $\min_i x_i = t$,
\[f\left(x \mid X_{(1)}=t\right)
= \frac{e^{n\theta}e^{-\sum_i x_i}}{n\,e^{-n(t-\theta)}}
= \frac{e^{nt-\sum_i x_i}}{n} .\]
The result does not involve $\theta$, which confirms sufficiency directly rather
than through the factorisation criterion. Read another way: given
$X_{(1)}=t$, the label of the minimising index is uniform on $\{1,\dots,n\}$ and
the remaining $n-1$ observations are distributed as $t$ plus independent
EXP$(1,0)$ variables --- the memoryless property, with the threshold reset to
$t$.

\subhead{Minimality}
For two samples,
\[\frac{f_{\theta}(x)}{f_{\theta}(y)}
= e^{\sum_i y_i - \sum_i x_i}\cdot
\frac{I\left(x_{(1)}>\theta\right)}{I\left(y_{(1)}>\theta\right)} .\]
The first factor is free of $\theta$ already. The second is a constant function
of $\theta$ only if the two indicators switch at the same place, that is only if
$x_{(1)} = y_{(1)}$. Hence $X_{(1)}$ is minimal sufficient.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\, X_1\, , \, X_2\, , \, \cdots \, , \, X_n\,$ be a random sample from the $CAU(1,\theta)$ distribution. Find a minimal sufficient for $\, \{f_{\theta}(x)\, :\, \theta\in \Omega\}$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The Cauchy density with location $\theta$ and scale $1$ is
$f_{\theta}(x) = \left[\pi\left(1+(x-\theta)^{2}\right)\right]^{-1}$, so
\[f_{\theta}(x_1,\dots,x_n) = \frac{1}{\pi^{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1+(x_i-\theta)^{2}} .\]
For two samples $x$ and $y$ the ratio is
\[\frac{f_{\theta}(x)}{f_{\theta}(y)}
= \frac{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left[1+(y_i-\theta)^{2}\right]}
       {\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left[1+(x_i-\theta)^{2}\right]} .\]
Regard numerator and denominator as polynomials in $\theta$. Each is monic of
degree $2n$. The ratio is a constant function of $\theta$ exactly when the two
polynomials are proportional, and two monic polynomials of the same degree are
proportional only if they are equal. Equal polynomials have the same roots with
the same multiplicities; the roots of $\prod_i\left[1+(x_i-\theta)^{2}\right]$
are $\theta = x_i \pm i$, so the multiset $\{x_1,\dots,x_n\}$ is determined.
Hence the ratio is free of $\theta$ if and only if $y$ is a permutation of $x$,
and the minimal sufficient statistic is the full set of order statistics
\[T(X) = \left(X_{(1)},\, X_{(2)},\, \dots,\, X_{(n)}\right).\]

\begin{remark}
No reduction of the data is possible beyond discarding the order in which the
observations arrived. This is the standard warning against expecting a
low-dimensional summary: the Cauchy location model has one parameter and a
minimal sufficient statistic of dimension $n$. It is also why the sample mean is
useless here --- $\overline{X}$ has the same Cauchy distribution as a single
observation, so averaging buys nothing at all.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\section{Completeness}

Completeness is the property that makes unbiased estimators based on a statistic
unique, and it is the key to finding UMVUEs.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:complete}
A statistic $T$ (or its family of distributions $\{f_T(t;\theta) : \theta\in\Omega\}$)
is \emph{complete} if, for every function $h$,
\[E_\theta\left[h(T)\right] = 0\ \text{for all } \theta\in\Omega
\qquad\text{implies}\qquad
P_\theta\left(h(T) = 0\right) = 1\ \text{for all } \theta\in\Omega .\]
\end{defn}

In words: the only function of $T$ that is an unbiased estimator of zero is zero
itself. If some nonzero $h(T)$ had mean zero for every $\theta$, then $T$ would
contain a component whose distribution carries no information about $\theta$.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:notcomplete}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ ($n\ge 2$) be a random sample from $N(\theta, 1)$, and let
$T(X) = \left(X_1, \sum_{i=1}^n X_i\right)$. Show that $T$ is sufficient but not
complete.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[f_\theta(x) = (2\pi)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac12\sum x_i^2\right)\exp\left(\theta\sum x_i - \frac{n\theta^2}{2}\right),\]
which depends on the data, apart from a factor free of $\theta$, only through
$\sum x_i$. So $\sum X_i$ is sufficient, and so is $T$, which contains it. Now take
$h(T) = X_1 - \frac1n\sum X_i = X_1 - \bar X$, a function of $T$. Then
\[E_\theta\left[h(T)\right] = \theta - \theta = 0\qquad\text{for every } \theta ,\]
but $X_1 - \bar X\sim N\left(0, 1 - \frac1n\right)$ is a continuous random
variable, so $P_\theta(h(T) = 0) = 0\ne 1$. Hence $T$ is not complete. The first
component $X_1$ adds nothing to the sufficient statistic and spoils completeness.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:bernoullicomplete}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the Bernoulli$(\theta)$ distribution,
$0<\theta<1$, and let $T = \sum X_i$. Show that $T$ is complete and sufficient.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[f_\theta(x) = \prod^n_{i = 1}\theta^{x_i}(1 - \theta)^{1 - x_i}
= \theta^{\sum x_i}(1 - \theta)^{n - \sum x_i},\]
a function of $\theta$ and $\sum x_i$ only, so $T$ is sufficient by
theorem~\ref{thm:factorisation}, with $h = 1$. Now $T\sim\text{BIN}(n, \theta)$.
Suppose $E_\theta\left[h(T)\right] = 0$ for all $\theta\in(0, 1)$. Then
\[0 = \sum^n_{t = 0} h(t)\binom{n}{t}\theta^t(1 - \theta)^{n - t}
= (1 - \theta)^n\sum^n_{t = 0} h(t)\binom{n}{t} r^t,\qquad r = \frac{\theta}{1 - \theta} .\]
As $\theta$ ranges over $(0, 1)$, $r$ ranges over $(0, \infty)$. A polynomial in $r$
that vanishes on an interval has all its coefficients zero, so
$h(t)\binom nt = 0$ and hence $h(t) = 0$ for $t = 0, 1, \dots, n$. So
$P_\theta(h(T) = 0) = 1$ and $T$ is complete.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:uniformcomplete}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $UNIF(0,\theta)$ distribution. Show that $T(X) = X_{(n)}$ is a complete statistic for $\{f_{\theta}(x);\, \theta \in \Omega\}$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The maximum has density
\[f_T(t) = \frac{n\,t^{\,n-1}}{\theta^{n}},\qquad 0<t<\theta .\]
Suppose $h$ is a function with $E_{\theta}\left[h(T)\right]=0$ for every
$\theta>0$. Then
\[\frac{n}{\theta^{n}}\int_0^{\theta} h(t)\,t^{\,n-1}\,dt = 0
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \int_0^{\theta} h(t)\,t^{\,n-1}\,dt = 0
\quad\text{for every }\theta>0 ,\]
since $n/\theta^{n}\neq 0$. The left-hand side is a function of its upper limit
which vanishes identically, so differentiating with respect to $\theta$ by the
fundamental theorem of calculus gives
\[h(\theta)\,\theta^{\,n-1} = 0 \qquad\text{for almost every }\theta>0 .\]
As $\theta^{n-1}\neq0$ on $(0,\infty)$, this forces $h(\theta)=0$ almost
everywhere, which is exactly completeness.

\begin{note}
The whole argument is that the family of intervals $(0,\theta)$ grows to fill
$(0,\infty)$ as $\theta$ varies. Completeness here is a statement about the
richness of the parameter set, not about the shape of the density --- which is
why restricting $\theta$ to a finite set would destroy it.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:normalaminimal}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $N(\theta, a\theta^2)$ distribution where $a>0$ is a known constant and $\theta >0$. Show that the minimal sufficient statistic is not complete.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\subhead{The minimal sufficient statistic}
Expanding the exponent,
\[-\frac{1}{2a\theta^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_i-\theta)^{2}
= -\frac{1}{2a\theta^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_i^{2}
  + \frac{1}{a\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_i - \frac{n}{2a},\]
so the density depends on the data only through
$T = \left(\sum_i X_i,\ \sum_i X_i^{2}\right)$, and the ratio criterion shows no
further reduction is possible: the two coefficients $1/(2a\theta^{2})$ and
$1/(a\theta)$ move independently enough that matching the ratio for all $\theta$
forces both components to agree. So $T$ is minimal sufficient.

\subhead{Why it is not complete}
Both components can be scaled to estimate $\theta^{2}$ unbiasedly. Since
$E(X_i)=\theta$ and $\var(X_i)=a\theta^{2}$,
\[E\left(\sum_i X_i^{2}\right) = n\left(a\theta^{2}+\theta^{2}\right) = n(1+a)\theta^{2},\]
\[E\left[\left(\sum_i X_i\right)^{2}\right] = \var\left(\sum_i X_i\right) + \left[E\left(\sum_i X_i\right)\right]^{2}
= na\theta^{2} + n^{2}\theta^{2} = n(a+n)\theta^{2}.\]
Hence the non-zero function
\[h(T) = \frac{\sum_i X_i^{2}}{n(1+a)} - \frac{\left(\sum_i X_i\right)^{2}}{n(a+n)}\]
satisfies $E_{\theta}\left[h(T)\right] = \theta^{2}-\theta^{2} = 0$ for every
$\theta>0$, while $h$ is not identically zero. So $T$ is not complete.

\begin{remark}
The model has one parameter but a two-dimensional minimal sufficient statistic,
and that mismatch is the whole reason completeness fails: there are two separate
routes to $\theta^{2}$ and their difference is a non-trivial function of $T$
with zero mean. It also means the Lehmann--Scheff\'e theorem is unavailable
here, so an unbiased estimator based on $T$ carries no guarantee of being the
UMVUE.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\subsection{Completeness, Sufficiency and Minimality}

\begin{thm}[Bahadur]
\label{thm:bahadur}
If $T(X)$ is a complete sufficient statistic for $\{f_\theta(x) : \theta\in\Omega\}$
and a minimal sufficient statistic exists, then $T(X)$ is minimal sufficient.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Assume $E_\theta|T|<\infty$, and let $S = S(X)$ be a minimal sufficient statistic.
Since $T$ is sufficient and $S$ is minimal, $S$ is a function of $T$: $S = r(T)$
for some $r$.

Because $S$ is sufficient, the conditional expectation $h(S) = E_\theta(T\mid S)$
does not depend on $\theta$, so $h$ is a genuine statistic. By the law of total
expectation (theorem~\ref{thm:totalexpectation}), for every $\theta$,
\[E_\theta\left[T - h(r(T))\right] = E_\theta(T) - E_\theta\left[E(T\mid S)\right] = 0 .\]
The quantity in brackets is a function of $T$ alone with zero mean for every
$\theta$, so completeness gives $T = h(r(T)) = h(S)$ with probability one. Thus $T$
is a function of $S$ and $S$ is a function of $T$; the two statistics are
equivalent, and $T$ is minimal sufficient because $S$ is.
\end{proof}

Bahadur's theorem is a reason to check completeness: it upgrades a sufficient
statistic to a minimal one without comparing likelihood ratios. The converse is
false; problem~\ref{prob:minimalnotcomplete} below gives the standard
counterexample, and problem~\ref{prob:normalaminimal} another.

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:minimalnotcomplete}
The converse of the above theorem is not true. Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $UNIF(\theta - 1, \theta + 1)$ distribution. Show that $T(X) = \left(X_{(1)}, X_{(n)}\right)$ is minimal sufficient. Show also that for the nonzero function \[h(T) = \frac{X_{(n)} - X_{(1)}}{2} - \frac{n - 1}{n  + 1}\] $E_{\theta}\left[h(T)\right] =0$ for all $\theta \in \Omega$ and therefore $T$ is not a complete statistic.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\subhead{Minimal sufficiency}
The joint density is one exactly when every observation lies in
$(\theta-1,\theta+1)$:
\[f_{\theta}(x) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} \frac{1}{2}\,I\left(\theta-1<x_i<\theta+1\right)
= 2^{-n}\,I\left(x_{(n)}-1<\theta<x_{(1)}+1\right).\]
For two samples the ratio $f_{\theta}(x)/f_{\theta}(y)$ is a constant function of
$\theta$ precisely when the intervals
$\left(x_{(n)}-1,\ x_{(1)}+1\right)$ and $\left(y_{(n)}-1,\ y_{(1)}+1\right)$
coincide, that is when $x_{(1)}=y_{(1)}$ and $x_{(n)}=y_{(n)}$. So
$T=\left(X_{(1)},X_{(n)}\right)$ is minimal sufficient.

\subhead{It is not complete}
Put $R = X_{(n)}-X_{(1)}$, the sample range. The observations are uniform on an
interval of length $2$, and the range of $n$ independent uniforms on an interval
of length $L$ has mean $L(n-1)/(n+1)$. With $L=2$,
\[E_{\theta}(R) = \frac{2(n-1)}{n+1}\qquad\text{for every }\theta ,\]
the value being free of $\theta$ because the range is invariant under the shift
$x\mapsto x+c$ that carries one parameter value to another. Therefore
\[E_{\theta}\left[h(T)\right]
= E_{\theta}\left[\frac{X_{(n)}-X_{(1)}}{2}\right] - \frac{n-1}{n+1}
= \frac{n-1}{n+1} - \frac{n-1}{n+1} = 0
\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in\Omega ,\]
while $h$ is plainly not the zero function --- the range is genuinely random. So
$T$ is minimal sufficient but not complete, which is the promised counterexample
to the converse.

\begin{note}
The mechanism is worth naming. The range is an \emph{ancillary} statistic: its
distribution does not involve $\theta$ at all. Any non-constant ancillary
function of a statistic destroys completeness immediately, by centring it. This
is the same phenomenon Basu's theorem exploits from the other direction.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:uniformminimal}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $UNIF(0,\theta)$ distribution. prove that $T(X) = X_{(n)}$ is a minimal sufficient statistic for this model.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
For the UNIF$(0,\theta)$ model the joint density is
\begin{align*}
f_{\theta}(x) &= \frac{1}{\theta^{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}I\left(0<x_i<\theta\right)\\
&= \frac{1}{\theta^{n}}\,I\left(x_{(1)}>0\right)\,I\left(x_{(n)}<\theta\right).
\end{align*}
Sufficiency of $X_{(n)}$ follows from the factorisation criterion at once, with
$g\left(x_{(n)},\theta\right)=\theta^{-n}I\left(x_{(n)}<\theta\right)$ and
$h(x)=I\left(x_{(1)}>0\right)$.

For minimality, take two samples in the support and form
\[\frac{f_{\theta}(x)}{f_{\theta}(y)}
= \frac{I\left(x_{(n)}<\theta\right)}{I\left(y_{(n)}<\theta\right)} .\]
Viewed as a function of $\theta$, this is constant only if the two indicators
change value at the same point, that is only if $x_{(n)} = y_{(n)}$. Hence
$X_{(n)}$ is minimal sufficient.

Combined with the completeness shown above, $X_{(n)}$ is complete and sufficient,
so by Bahadur's theorem its minimality also follows without this calculation ---
but the direct argument is shorter than the theorem it instantiates.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $EXP(1, \theta)$ distribution. Show that $T(X) = X_{(1)}$ is a minimal sufficient statistic for the model.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
This is the calculation already carried out in the previous section, and it is
worth recording that the two are the same problem. The joint density is
\[f_{\theta}(x) = e^{n\theta}\,I\left(x_{(1)}>\theta\right)\cdot e^{-\sum_i x_i},\]
so $X_{(1)}$ is sufficient by factorisation, and the ratio
\[\frac{f_{\theta}(x)}{f_{\theta}(y)}
= e^{\sum_i y_i-\sum_i x_i}\cdot\frac{I\left(x_{(1)}>\theta\right)}{I\left(y_{(1)}>\theta\right)}\]
is free of $\theta$ only when the two indicators switch at the same place, that
is only when $x_{(1)}=y_{(1)}$. Hence $X_{(1)}$ is minimal sufficient.

Completeness holds as well, by the same upper-limit differentiation used for the
UNIF$(0,\theta)$ maximum: $X_{(1)}$ has density $n\,e^{-n(t-\theta)}$ on
$(\theta,\infty)$, and $\int_{\theta}^{\infty}h(t)e^{-nt}\,dt = 0$ for every
$\theta$ forces $h=0$ almost everywhere. So $X_{(1)}$ is complete and sufficient,
which is what the next problem needs.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Conditioning: Total Expectation and Total Variance}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:totalexpectation}
For any random variables $X$ and $Y$ for which the expectations exist,
\[E(X) = E\left[E(X\mid Y)\right]\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\var(X) = E\left[\var(X\mid Y)\right] + \var\left[E(X\mid Y)\right].\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
\subhead{The first identity}
Writing the inner expectation out and exchanging the order of integration,
\begin{align*}
E\left[E(X\mid Y)\right] &= \int \left[\int x\, f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)\,dx\right] f_Y(y)\,dy\\
&= \iint x\, f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)\, f_Y(y)\,dx\,dy .
\end{align*}
The product $f_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)f_Y(y)$ is the joint density $f(x,y)$, so the
double integral is $\iint x\,f(x,y)\,dx\,dy = E(X)$. In the discrete case
replace the integrals by sums.

\subhead{The second identity}
Apply the first identity to $X^2$ and use
$\var(X\mid Y) = E\left(X^2\mid Y\right) - \left[E(X\mid Y)\right]^2$:
\[E\left(X^2\right) = E\left[E\left(X^2\mid Y\right)\right]
= E\left[\var(X\mid Y)\right] + E\left[\left\{E(X\mid Y)\right\}^2\right].\]
Subtract $\left[E(X)\right]^2 = \left\{E\left[E(X\mid Y)\right]\right\}^2$ from both
sides. The left side becomes $\var(X)$, and on the right
$E\left[\left\{E(X\mid Y)\right\}^2\right] - \left\{E\left[E(X\mid Y)\right]\right\}^2
= \var\left[E(X\mid Y)\right]$.
\end{proof}

The second identity says that conditioning on $Y$ splits the variance of $X$ into
the part explained by $Y$ and the average variance left over. Applied to an
estimator and a sufficient statistic, it gives the Rao--Blackwell theorem.

\begin{thm}[Rao--Blackwell]
\label{thm:raoblackwell}
Let $U$ be an unbiased estimator of $\tau(\theta)$ and let $T$ be a sufficient
statistic. Then $\phi(T) = E(U\mid T)$ is a statistic, it is unbiased for
$\tau(\theta)$, and
\[\var_\theta\left[\phi(T)\right]\le\var_\theta(U)\qquad\text{for all } \theta ,\]
with equality only if $U = \phi(T)$ with probability one.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
By sufficiency the conditional distribution of the sample given $T$, and hence of
$U$ given $T$, does not involve $\theta$; so $\phi(T)$ can be computed from the data
and is a statistic. By theorem~\ref{thm:totalexpectation},
$E_\theta\left[\phi(T)\right] = E_\theta(U) = \tau(\theta)$, and
\[\var_\theta(U) = E_\theta\left[\var(U\mid T)\right] + \var_\theta\left[\phi(T)\right]
\ge\var_\theta\left[\phi(T)\right] .\]
Equality holds only if $E_\theta\left[\var(U\mid T)\right] = 0$, that is, $U$ is
constant given $T$, which means $U = \phi(T)$.
\end{proof}

Sufficiency is essential in this theorem. Without it $E(U\mid T)$ would depend on
$\theta$ and would not be an estimator.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:raoblackwellpoisson}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{POI}(\theta)$. The estimator
$U = X_1$ is unbiased for $\theta$. Improve it by conditioning on $T = \sum X_i$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
By example~\ref{ex:poissonsufficient}, given $T = t$ the sample is multinomial
with $t$ trials and equal probabilities $\frac1n$, so $X_1\mid T = t\sim
\text{BIN}\left(t, \frac1n\right)$ and $E(X_1\mid T = t) = \frac tn$. The improved
estimator is $\phi(T) = \frac Tn = \bar X$, with variance $\frac\theta n$ in place of
$\var(X_1) = \theta$.
\end{solution}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:uniqueunbiased}
If $T(X)$ is a complete statistic, then there is at most one function of $T$
that is an unbiased estimator of $\tau(\theta)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Suppose $g_1(T)$ and $g_2(T)$ are both unbiased for $\tau(\theta)$. Then
$h(T) = g_1(T) - g_2(T)$ has $E_\theta\left[h(T)\right] = 0$ for every $\theta$, and
completeness gives $P_\theta(h(T) = 0) = 1$. So $g_1(T) = g_2(T)$ with probability
one.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Minimum Variance Unbiased Estimation}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:umvue}
If $T(X)$ is a complete sufficient statistic and $E_\theta\left[g(T)\right] = \tau(\theta)$
for all $\theta$, then $g(T)$ is the UMVUE of $\tau(\theta)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Let $U$ be any unbiased estimator of $\tau(\theta)$. By
theorem~\ref{thm:raoblackwell}, $\phi(T) = E(U\mid T)$ is an unbiased function of
$T$ with $\var_\theta(\phi(T))\le\var_\theta(U)$. By theorem~\ref{thm:uniqueunbiased}
there is only one unbiased function of $T$, so $\phi(T) = g(T)$. Hence
$\var_\theta(g(T))\le\var_\theta(U)$ for every $\theta$ and every unbiased $U$.
\end{proof}

The theorem gives two ways of finding a UMVUE once a complete sufficient statistic
$T$ is known:
\begin{enumerate}
\item find a function $g(T)$ with $E_\theta\left[g(T)\right] = \tau(\theta)$, usually by
computing moments of $T$ and adjusting; or
\item take any simple unbiased estimator $U$ and compute $E(U\mid T)$.
\end{enumerate}
The second method is the subject of theorem~\ref{thm:lehmannscheffe}.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:bernoullithetasq}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ ($n\ge 2$) be a random sample from Bernoulli$(\theta)$. Find
the UMVUE of $\theta^2$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
By example~\ref{ex:bernoullicomplete}, $T = \sum X_i\sim\text{BIN}(n, \theta)$ is
complete and sufficient. Its first two moments are $E(T) = n\theta$ and
$E(T^2) = \var(T) + \left[E(T)\right]^2 = n\theta(1 - \theta) + n^2\theta^2$, so
\[E\left[T(T - 1)\right] = E(T^2) - E(T) = n^2\theta^2 - n\theta^2 = n(n - 1)\theta^2 .\]
Hence $\frac{T(T - 1)}{n(n - 1)}$ is an unbiased function of $T$, and by
theorem~\ref{thm:umvue} it is the UMVUE of $\theta^2$. In terms of the sample mean
it is $\frac{\bar X(n\bar X - 1)}{n - 1}$. Note that the obvious estimator $\bar X^2$
is biased: $E(\bar X^2) = \theta^2 + \frac{\theta(1 - \theta)}{n}$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:uniformumvue}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{UNIF}(0, \theta)$. Find the
UMVUE of $\theta$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$T = X_{(n)}$ is complete (problem~\ref{prob:uniformcomplete}) and sufficient
(example~\ref{ex:uniformsufficient}). By example~\ref{ex:uniformmse},
$E(T) = \frac{n}{n + 1}\theta$, so $E\left[\frac{n + 1}{n}T\right] = \theta$ and
$\frac{n + 1}{n}X_{(n)}$ is the UMVUE of $\theta$. Its variance is
$\left(\frac{n + 1}{n}\right)^2\frac{n\theta^2}{(n + 1)^2(n + 2)} = \frac{\theta^2}{n(n + 2)}$,
smaller than the $\frac{\theta^2}{3n}$ of the unbiased estimator $2\bar X$ for every
$n\ge 2$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the Bernoulli$(\theta)$ distribution. Find the $UMVUE$ of $\tau(\theta) = \theta(1 -\theta)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
$T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i\sim$ BIN$(n,\theta)$ is complete and sufficient, so by
Lehmann--Scheff\'e it is enough to find \emph{any} function of $T$ that is
unbiased for $\theta(1-\theta)$; it is then automatically the UMVUE.

Try $T(n-T)$. Using $E(T)=n\theta$ and
$E\left(T^{2}\right)=n\theta(1-\theta)+n^{2}\theta^{2}$,
\begin{align*}
E\left[T(n-T)\right] &= nE(T) - E\left(T^{2}\right)\\
&= n^{2}\theta - n\theta(1-\theta) - n^{2}\theta^{2}\\
&= n^{2}\theta(1-\theta) - n\theta(1-\theta)
 = n(n-1)\,\theta(1-\theta).
\end{align*}
Dividing by the constant $n(n-1)$,
\[\widehat{\tau} = \frac{T(n-T)}{n(n-1)}
= \frac{n}{n-1}\,\overline{X}\left(1-\overline{X}\right)\]
is unbiased, and therefore is the UMVUE of $\theta(1-\theta)$.

\begin{note}
The naive estimator $\overline{X}\left(1-\overline{X}\right)$ is biased
downwards by exactly the factor $(n-1)/n$. The correction is the same one that
turns the sample variance into its unbiased form, and for the same reason: a
product of an estimator with itself underestimates the product of the
parameters, because it inherits the estimator's own variability.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $EXP(1,\theta)$ distribution. Find the $UMVUE$ of $\tau(\theta) = \theta^2$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
$T=X_{(1)}$ is complete and sufficient for this model, as shown above. Its
density is $n\,e^{-n(t-\theta)}$ on $(\theta,\infty)$, so $T-\theta$ is
exponential with rate $n$ and
\[E(T) = \theta + \frac{1}{n},\qquad
\var(T) = \frac{1}{n^{2}},\qquad
E\left(T^{2}\right) = \frac{1}{n^{2}} + \left(\theta+\frac1n\right)^{2}
= \theta^{2} + \frac{2\theta}{n} + \frac{2}{n^{2}} .\]
Look for an unbiased estimator of the form $g(T)=T^{2}+\alpha T+\beta$:
\[E\left[g(T)\right]
= \theta^{2} + \left(\frac{2}{n}+\alpha\right)\theta
  + \left(\frac{2}{n^{2}} + \frac{\alpha}{n} + \beta\right).\]
Matching $\theta^{2}$ requires the coefficient of $\theta$ and the constant to
vanish, giving $\alpha=-2/n$ and then $\beta=0$. Hence
\[\widehat{\theta^{2}} = X_{(1)}^{2} - \frac{2X_{(1)}}{n},\]
and by Lehmann--Scheff\'e this is the UMVUE of $\theta^{2}$.

\begin{remark}
Note the estimator can be negative --- it is negative whenever
$0<X_{(1)}<2/n$ --- even though $\theta^{2}\geq0$ always. This is not an error.
Unbiasedness is a statement about an average over repeated samples and carries
no promise that individual values land in the parameter space, and the UMVUE can
be inadmissible for exactly this reason. Truncating at zero would reduce the
mean square error while destroying unbiasedness.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\subsection{The Lehmann--Scheff\'e Theorem}

\begin{thm}[Lehmann--Scheff\'e]
\label{thm:lehmannscheffe}
If $T(X)$ is a complete sufficient statistic and $U(X)$ is any unbiased estimator
of $\tau(\theta)$, then $E(U\mid T)$ is the UMVUE of $\tau(\theta)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
By theorem~\ref{thm:raoblackwell}, $E(U\mid T)$ is a statistic, a function of $T$,
and unbiased for $\tau(\theta)$. By theorem~\ref{thm:umvue} it is the UMVUE.
\end{proof}

The method is most useful when $\tau(\theta)$ is a probability. If
$\tau(\theta) = P_\theta(X_1\in A)$, then the indicator $U = I(X_1\in A)$ is unbiased,
and the UMVUE is $E(U\mid T) = P(X_1\in A\mid T)$, a conditional probability that
does not involve $\theta$. Examples~\ref{ex:poissonzero} and~\ref{ex:normalcdf} use
this idea.

\section{The Exponential Family}

Most of the distributions met so far share a common structure, and for that
structure complete sufficient statistics can be written down at once.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:expfamily}
A random vector $X$ has a distribution in the \emph{exponential family} if its
probability (density) function has the form
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:expfamily}
f_\theta(x) = C(\theta)\exp\left\{\sum^k_{j = 1} q_j(\theta)T_j(x)\right\}h(x)
\end{equation}
for functions $C(\theta)>0$, $q_j(\theta)$, $T_j(x)$ and $h(x)\ge 0$, where the set
$\{x : h(x)>0\}$ does not depend on $\theta$. The statistic
$(T_1(X), \dots, T_k(X))$ is the \emph{natural sufficient statistic}.
\end{defn}

The natural sufficient statistic is not unique: multiplying $T_j$ by a constant
and dividing $q_j$ by the same constant leaves $f_\theta$ unchanged. The condition
on the support excludes families such as $\text{UNIF}(0, \theta)$, whose
indicator $I(x<\theta)$ cannot be absorbed into the form \eqref{eq:expfamily}.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:expfamilysufficient}
Show that the natural sufficient statistic $(T_1(X), \dots, T_k(X))$ of
\eqref{eq:expfamily} is sufficient.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Take $g(t;\theta) = C(\theta)\exp\left\{\sum_j q_j(\theta)t_j\right\}$ and the given $h$.
Then $f_\theta(x) = g(T(x);\theta)h(x)$, and theorem~\ref{thm:factorisation}
applies.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:binomialexpfamily}
Show that $\text{BIN}(n, \theta)$, with $n$ known, is an exponential family and
find its natural sufficient statistic.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
For $x = 0, 1, \dots, n$,
\[f_\theta(x) = \binom{n}{x}\theta^x(1 - \theta)^{n - x}
= \underbrace{(1 - \theta)^n}_{C(\theta)}\exp\left\{\underbrace{\ln\frac{\theta}{1 - \theta}}_{q(\theta)}\cdot\underbrace{x}_{T(x)}\right\}\underbrace{\binom{n}{x}}_{h(x)} .\]
So the binomial is an exponential family with $k = 1$ and natural sufficient
statistic $T(X) = X$.
\end{solution}

The exponential family is closed under independent sampling.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:expfamilysample}
If $X_1, \dots, X_n$ is a random sample from a distribution of the form
\eqref{eq:expfamily}, then the joint probability (density) function is
\begin{align*}
f_\theta(x_1, \dots, x_n) &= C(\theta)^n\exp\left\{\sum^k_{j = 1} q_j(\theta)\sum^n_{i = 1} T_j(x_i)\right\}\\
&\qquad\times\prod^n_{i = 1}h(x_i),
\end{align*}
again of exponential family form, with natural sufficient statistic
$\left(\sum_{i=1}^n T_1(X_i), \dots, \sum_{i=1}^n T_k(X_i)\right)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Multiply the $n$ densities: the factors $C(\theta)$ multiply to $C(\theta)^n$, the
exponents add, and the $h(x_i)$ multiply.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissonexpfamily}
Show that a random sample from $\text{POI}(\theta)$ has an exponential family
distribution, and find its natural sufficient statistic.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
For one observation,
\[f_\theta(x) = \frac{e^{-\theta}\theta^x}{x!} = \underbrace{e^{-\theta}}_{C(\theta)}\exp\{\underbrace{(\ln\theta)}_{q(\theta)}\,\underbrace{x}_{T(x)}\}\underbrace{\frac{1}{x!}}_{h(x)},\qquad x = 0, 1, \dots .\]
By theorem~\ref{thm:expfamilysample} the sample has the exponential family density
\[f_\theta(x_1, \dots, x_n) = e^{-n\theta}\exp\left\{(\ln\theta)\sum x_i\right\}\prod\frac{1}{x_i!},\]
with natural sufficient statistic $\sum X_i$.
\end{solution}

\subsection{The Canonical Form}

\begin{defn}
\label{def:canonical}
Writing $\eta_j = q_j(\theta)$ in \eqref{eq:expfamily} gives the \emph{canonical form}
\[f_\eta(x) = C(\eta)\exp\left\{\sum^k_{j = 1}\eta_jT_j(x)\right\}h(x) .\]
The \emph{natural parameter space} is the set of $\eta$ for which this can be
normalised,
\[\Omega_\eta = \left\{\eta : \int\exp\left\{\sum_j\eta_jT_j(x)\right\}h(x)\,dx<\infty\right\},\]
with the integral replaced by a sum when $X$ is discrete.
\end{defn}

If the $T_j$ satisfy a linear constraint, for example $T_1(x) + T_2(x) = n$ for all
$x$, one of them can be eliminated and the number of terms reduced; otherwise the
parameters $\eta_j$ are not identifiable. The same applies to linear constraints
among the $\eta_j$.

\subsection{Regular Exponential Families}

\begin{defn}
\label{def:ref}
$X$ has a \emph{regular exponential family} (REF) distribution if its canonical form
is of full rank, in the sense that neither the $T_j$ nor the $\eta_j$ satisfy a
linear constraint, and the natural parameter space contains a $k$-dimensional
open rectangle.
\end{defn}

By theorem~\ref{thm:expfamilysample}, if each $X_i$ has a REF distribution then so
does the sample $(X_1, \dots, X_n)$.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:binomialref}
Show that $\text{BIN}(n, \theta)$ is a regular exponential family.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
From example~\ref{ex:binomialexpfamily}, $\eta = \ln\frac{\theta}{1 - \theta}$. As
$\theta$ ranges over $(0, 1)$, $\frac\theta{1-\theta}$ ranges over $(0, \infty)$ and
$\eta$ over $(-\infty, \infty)$. Inverting, $\theta = \frac{e^\eta}{1 + e^\eta}$ and
$1 - \theta = \frac{1}{1 + e^\eta}$, so the canonical form is
\[f_\eta(x) = \left(1 + e^\eta\right)^{-n}e^{\eta x}\binom{n}{x},\qquad
\Omega_\eta = \mathbb{R} .\]
There is one statistic $T = X$ and one parameter, with no linear constraints, and
$\Omega_\eta$ contains an open interval. So the binomial is a REF.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Completeness in the Exponential Family}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:refcomplete}
If $X$ has a regular exponential family distribution, then its natural sufficient
statistic $(T_1(X), \dots, T_k(X))$ is complete and sufficient.
\end{thm}

Sufficiency is example~\ref{ex:expfamilysufficient}. Completeness rests on the
uniqueness of the Laplace transform: $E_\eta\left[g(T)\right] = 0$ on an open
rectangle of $\eta$ values says that a certain Laplace transform vanishes on an
open set, which forces $g = 0$. Example~\ref{ex:bernoullicomplete} is the case of a
binomial, where the transform is a polynomial. A full proof is in Lehmann and
Romano, \emph{Testing Statistical Hypotheses}, theorem~4.3.1.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:normalumvue}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$. Find a complete
sufficient statistic, and the UMVUEs of $\mu$ and $\sigma^2$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
For one observation,
\[f_\theta(x) = \underbrace{\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{-\mu^2/(2\sigma^2)}}_{C(\theta)}
\exp\left\{\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}}_{q_1(\theta)}\underbrace{x^2}_{T_1(x)}
+ \underbrace{\frac{\mu}{\sigma^2}}_{q_2(\theta)}\underbrace{x}_{T_2(x)}\right\}\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}}_{h(x)} .\]
The natural parameters are $\eta_1 = -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}$ and $\eta_2 = \frac{\mu}{\sigma^2}$.
As $(\mu, \sigma^2)$ ranges over $\mathbb{R}\times(0, \infty)$, $(\eta_1, \eta_2)$
ranges over the half-plane
\[\Omega_\eta = \{(\eta_1, \eta_2) : \eta_1<0,\ -\infty<\eta_2<\infty\},\]
shaded in figure~\ref{fig:halfplane}, which contains an open rectangle. Neither
$x^2$ and $x$ nor $\eta_1$ and $\eta_2$ satisfy a linear constraint, so the normal
is a REF. By theorems~\ref{thm:expfamilysample} and~\ref{thm:refcomplete},
$T = \left(\sum X_i, \sum X_i^2\right)$ is complete and sufficient.

The pair $(\bar X, S^2)$ is a one-to-one function of $T$, since
\[\bar X = \frac1n\sum X_i\qquad\text{and}\qquad S^2 = \frac{1}{n - 1}\left(\sum X_i^2 - \frac1n\left(\sum X_i\right)^2\right),\]
so functions of $T$ and functions of $(\bar X, S^2)$ are the same thing. Since
