$E(\bar X) = \mu$ and $E(S^2) = \sigma^2$, theorem~\ref{thm:umvue} shows that
$\bar X$ is the UMVUE of $\mu$ and $S^2$ is the UMVUE of $\sigma^2$.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\draw[-Stealth](-2,0)--(2,0)node[below]{$\eta_1$};
\draw[-Stealth](0,-2)--(0,2)node[above]{$\eta_2$};
% A solid wash rather than TikZ's `pattern=` hatching. tex4ht converts each
% picture by way of PostScript, where pgf's pattern definitions are undefined:
% the conversion fails with "undefined in pgfpat1" and writes no file at all,
% so the page shows a broken image and the log says nothing. The hatch only
% marked which region was meant; a wash of the same colour says the same.
\path[fill=teal,opacity=0.25](-2,1.9)--(0,1.9)--(0,-1.9)--(-2,-1.9)--cycle;
\draw(-0.5,1)--(-0.5,-1)--(-1.5,-1)--(-1.5,1)--cycle;
\end{tikzpicture}
\label{fig:halfplane}
\end{figure}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:normalthetanotref}
Show that $N(\theta, \theta^2)$, $\theta\ne 0$, is an exponential family but not a
regular one.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[f_\theta(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\theta^2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2\theta^2}(x^2 - 2\theta x + \theta^2)}
= \underbrace{\frac{1}{|\theta|}}_{C(\theta)}\exp\left\{\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2\theta^2}}_{q_1(\theta)}\underbrace{x^2}_{T_1(x)}
+ \underbrace{\frac1\theta}_{q_2(\theta)}\underbrace{x}_{T_2(x)}\right\}\underbrace{\frac{e^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}}_{h(x)},\]
so this is an exponential family with natural sufficient statistic $(X^2, X)$. But
$\eta_1 = -\frac{1}{2\theta^2}$ and $\eta_2 = \frac1\theta$ satisfy
$\eta_1 = -\frac12\eta_2^2$, so the parameter set
\[\left\{(\eta_1, \eta_2) : \eta_1 = -\tfrac12\eta_2^2,\ \eta_2\ne 0\right\}\]
is a curve (figure~\ref{fig:curvedfamily}) and contains no two-dimensional
rectangle. The family is not regular, and theorem~\ref{thm:refcomplete} does not
apply; indeed problem~\ref{prob:normalaminimal} shows that its minimal sufficient
statistic is not complete. Families like this are called \emph{curved}
exponential families.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[
    width=8cm, height=6cm,
    xlabel={$\eta_2$}, ylabel={$\eta_1$},
    xmin=-3, xmax=3, ymin=-4.6, ymax=0.6,
    axis lines=middle, ticks=none]
\addplot[very thick, blue, smooth, samples=80, domain=-3:3] {-x^2/2};
\addplot[only marks, mark=*, mark options={fill=white}] coordinates {(0,0)};
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{For $N(\theta, \theta^2)$ the natural parameters lie on the curve
$\eta_1 = -\frac12\eta_2^2$ (with the origin removed), which contains no
rectangle.}
\label{fig:curvedfamily}
\end{figure}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissonumvue}
Let $X_i\sim\text{POI}(i\theta)$, $i = 1, \dots, n$, independently, as in
example~\ref{ex:poissonunbiased}. Show that $T_2 = \frac{2}{n(n + 1)}\sum X_i$ is the
UMVUE of $\theta$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
The joint probability function is
\[\prod_{i=1}^n\frac{e^{-i\theta}(i\theta)^{x_i}}{x_i!}
= e^{-\theta n(n + 1)/2}\exp\left\{(\ln\theta)\sum x_i\right\}\prod_{i=1}^n\frac{i^{x_i}}{x_i!},\]
a one-parameter REF with $\eta = \ln\theta\in\mathbb{R}$ and natural sufficient
statistic $\sum X_i$, which is therefore complete and sufficient. $T_2$ is an
unbiased function of it, so $T_2$ is the UMVUE by theorem~\ref{thm:umvue}. This
explains the comparison in example~\ref{ex:poissoncompare}: $T_1$ is unbiased but is
not a function of $\sum X_i$, and so has larger variance.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:multinomialumvue}
Let $(X_1, X_2, X_3)\sim\text{MULT}(n; \theta_1, \theta_2, \theta_3)$, $n\ge 2$,
with $\theta_1 + \theta_2 + \theta_3 = 1$. Find the UMVUE of $\theta_1\theta_2$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Since $x_3 = n - x_1 - x_2$ and $\theta_3 = 1 - \theta_1 - \theta_2$, the
probability function depends on two free statistics and two free parameters:
\begin{align*}
f_\theta(x_1, x_2) &= \frac{n!}{x_1!\,x_2!\,(n - x_1 - x_2)!}\theta_1^{x_1}\theta_2^{x_2}(1 - \theta_1 - \theta_2)^{n - x_1 - x_2}\\
&= (1 - \theta_1 - \theta_2)^n\exp\left\{x_1\ln\frac{\theta_1}{1 - \theta_1 - \theta_2} + x_2\ln\frac{\theta_2}{1 - \theta_1 - \theta_2}\right\}h(x).
\end{align*}
(Writing all three $x_j\ln\theta_j$ terms would give statistics with the linear
constraint $X_1 + X_2 + X_3 = n$, which is why we eliminate $X_3$.) The natural
parameters $\eta_j = \ln\frac{\theta_j}{1 - \theta_1 - \theta_2}$, $j = 1, 2$,
range over all of $\mathbb{R}^2$, and in canonical form
\[f_\eta(x_1, x_2) = \left(1 + e^{\eta_1} + e^{\eta_2}\right)^{-n}\exp\{\eta_1x_1 + \eta_2x_2\}h(x).\]
So this is a REF and $(X_1, X_2)$ is complete and sufficient. For the multinomial,
$E(X_j) = n\theta_j$ and $\cov(X_1, X_2) = -n\theta_1\theta_2$, so
\[E(X_1X_2) = \cov(X_1, X_2) + E(X_1)E(X_2) = -n\theta_1\theta_2 + n^2\theta_1\theta_2 = n(n - 1)\theta_1\theta_2 .\]
Hence $\frac{X_1X_2}{n(n - 1)}$ is unbiased for $\theta_1\theta_2$ and, being a function
of the complete sufficient statistic, it is the UMVUE.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissonzero}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ ($n\ge 2$) be a random sample from $\text{POI}(\theta)$. Find
the UMVUE of $\tau(\theta) = e^{-\theta} = P(X = 0)$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$U = I(X_1 = 0)$ is unbiased, since $E(U) = P(X_1 = 0) = e^{-\theta}$. By
example~\ref{ex:poissonexpfamily} the Poisson is a REF with $\eta = \ln\theta\in\mathbb{R}$,
so $T = \sum X_i$ is complete and sufficient. By theorem~\ref{thm:lehmannscheffe}
the UMVUE is
\[E(U\mid T = t) = P(X_1 = 0\mid T = t) = \frac{P\left(X_1 = 0,\ \sum_{i=2}^n X_i = t\right)}{P(T = t)} .\]
The events $X_1 = 0$ and $\sum_{i=2}^n X_i = t$ are independent, the second sum is
$\text{POI}((n - 1)\theta)$ and $T\sim\text{POI}(n\theta)$, so
\[P(X_1 = 0\mid T = t) = \frac{e^{-\theta}\cdot e^{-(n - 1)\theta}\frac{((n - 1)\theta)^t}{t!}}{e^{-n\theta}\frac{(n\theta)^t}{t!}}
= \left(\frac{n - 1}{n}\right)^t .\]
The UMVUE of $e^{-\theta}$ is $\left(1 - \frac1n\right)^T$. As a check, with
$a = 1 - \frac1n$,
$E\left(a^T\right) = e^{-n\theta}\sum_t\frac{(an\theta)^t}{t!} = e^{-n\theta(1 - a)} = e^{-\theta}$.
For large $n$, $\left(1 - \frac1n\right)^T\approx e^{-T/n} = e^{-\bar X}$, the
maximum likelihood estimator.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:normalcdf}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ ($n\ge 2$) be a random sample from $N(\theta, 1)$, and let $c$
be a constant. Find the UMVUE of $\tau(\theta) = P_\theta(X_1\le c) = \Phi(c - \theta)$,
where $\Phi$ is the standard normal distribution function.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$U = I(X_1\le c)$ is unbiased for $\tau(\theta)$, and $T = \sum X_i$ is complete
and sufficient (the normal with known variance is a REF with $\eta = \theta$). By
theorem~\ref{thm:lehmannscheffe} the UMVUE is $P(X_1\le c\mid T)$.

The pair $(X_1, T)$ is bivariate normal with $E(X_1) = \theta$, $E(T) = n\theta$,
$\var(X_1) = 1$, $\var(T) = n$ and $\cov(X_1, T) = \var(X_1) = 1$. The conditional
distribution of one component of a bivariate normal given the other is normal,
with
\[E(X_1\mid T = t) = \theta + \frac{1}{n}(t - n\theta) = \frac tn,\qquad
\var(X_1\mid T = t) = 1 - \frac{1^2}{n} = \frac{n - 1}{n} .\]
As sufficiency promises, $\theta$ has disappeared. Hence
\[P(X_1\le c\mid T = t) = \Phi\left(\frac{c - t/n}{\sqrt{(n - 1)/n}}\right),\]
and the UMVUE of $\Phi(c - \theta)$ is $\Phi\left(\sqrt{\frac{n}{n - 1}}\,(c - \bar X)\right)$.
The plug-in estimator $\Phi(c - \bar X)$ is close to it for large $n$ but is
biased.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:exponentialumvue}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the exponential distribution with
mean $\theta$, $f_\theta(x) = \frac1\theta e^{-x/\theta}$, $x>0$. Find the UMVUEs of
$\theta$, $\theta^2$ and $\frac1\theta$ (the last for $n\ge 2$).
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$f_\theta(x) = \frac1\theta\exp\left\{-\frac1\theta x\right\}$ is a REF with
$\eta = -\frac1\theta$ ranging over $(-\infty, 0)$, so $T = \sum X_i$ is complete and
sufficient, and $T\sim\text{GAM}(n, \theta)$. By problem~\ref{prob:gammamoments},
$E(T^p) = \frac{\theta^p\Gamma(n + p)}{\Gamma(n)}$ for $p>-n$. So
\[E(T) = n\theta,\qquad E(T^2) = n(n + 1)\theta^2,\qquad E(T^{-1}) = \frac{1}{(n - 1)\theta} .\]
The UMVUEs are therefore $\frac Tn = \bar X$ for $\theta$, $\frac{T^2}{n(n + 1)}$ for
$\theta^2$, and $\frac{n - 1}{T}$ for $\frac1\theta$. The last is not the obvious
$\frac{1}{\bar X} = \frac nT$, which overestimates $\frac1\theta$ by the factor
$\frac{n}{n - 1}$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample for the pdf \[f_{\theta}(x) = \theta\, x^{\theta - 1}\, , \,\  0 < x < 1, \, \, \theta > 0.\]
Show that the geometric mean \[\left(\prod^n_{i = 1} X_i \right)^{1/n}\] is a complete sufficient statistic and find the $UMVUE$ of $\theta$.
\[\textit{Hint:} - \log X_i \, \thicksim \, Exp\left(\frac{1}{\theta}\right)\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Put $Y_i = -\log X_i$. Since $x=e^{-y}$ and $\left|dx/dy\right| = e^{-y}$,
\[f_Y(y) = \theta\left(e^{-y}\right)^{\theta-1}e^{-y} = \theta e^{-\theta y},
\qquad y>0 ,\]
so $Y_i$ is exponential with rate $\theta$, as the hint states.

Writing the density as
$f_{\theta}(x) = \theta\exp\left\{(\theta-1)\log x\right\}$ exhibits a
one-parameter exponential family with natural statistic $\sum_i \log X_i$.
Hence $T = -\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log X_i = \sum_i Y_i$ is complete and sufficient, and
$T\sim$ GAM$(n,1/\theta)$ --- shape $n$, rate $\theta$. The geometric mean
\[G = \left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}X_i\right)^{1/n} = e^{-T/n}\]
is a strictly monotone function of $T$, so it is complete and sufficient too.

For the UMVUE, a gamma variable of shape $n$ and rate $\theta$ has
\[E\left(\frac{1}{T}\right) = \frac{\theta}{n-1}\qquad (n>1),\]
so $(n-1)/T$ is unbiased for $\theta$ and, being a function of a complete
sufficient statistic, is the UMVUE:
\[\widehat{\theta} = \frac{n-1}{T} = \frac{-(n-1)}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log X_i}
= \frac{n-1}{-n\log G}.\]
The maximum likelihood estimator is $n/T$, so the two differ only in replacing
$n$ by $n-1$ --- the usual correction that converts a maximum likelihood
estimator into an unbiased one.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $GAM(\alpha, \beta)$ distribution and $\theta = (\alpha , \beta)$. Find the $UMVE$ of $\tau(\theta) = \alpha\beta$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
With both parameters unknown the density is
\[f_{\theta}(x) = \frac{x^{\alpha-1}e^{-x/\beta}}{\beta^{\alpha}\Gamma(\alpha)}
= \frac{1}{\beta^{\alpha}\Gamma(\alpha)}
\exp\left\{(\alpha-1)\log x - \frac{x}{\beta}\right\},\]
a two-parameter exponential family whose natural statistic is
\[T = \left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i,\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\log X_i\right),\]
complete and sufficient because the natural parameter space
$\left\{(\alpha-1,\,-1/\beta):\alpha>0,\beta>0\right\}$ contains an open
rectangle.

Now $E(X_i)=\alpha\beta$, so $\overline{X}$ is unbiased for $\tau(\theta)$, and
it is a function of the first component of $T$. By Lehmann--Scheff\'e it is
therefore the UMVUE:
\[\widehat{\alpha\beta} = \overline{X}.\]

\begin{note}
No calculation was needed beyond identifying the family. That is the point of
the exponential-family machinery: once completeness is established, producing
\emph{any} unbiased function of the sufficient statistic settles the question,
and here the obvious estimator already is one.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X\thicksim NBIN(k, \theta)$. Find the $UMVUE$ of $\theta$. \[\textit{Hint:}\ \, \text{Find}\,\,\, \, E\left(\frac{1}{X + K - 1}\right).\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Take the negative binomial in the form
\[P_{\theta}(X=x) = \binom{x+k-1}{x}\theta^{k}(1-\theta)^{x},
\qquad x = 0,1,2,\dots\]
the number of failures before the $k$-th success. This is a one-parameter
exponential family in $\theta$ and $X$ is the whole sample, so $X$ is complete
and sufficient.

Following the hint, use the binomial identity
\[\frac{1}{x+k-1}\binom{x+k-1}{x}
= \frac{(x+k-2)!}{x!\,(k-1)!}
= \frac{1}{k-1}\binom{x+k-2}{x},\]
valid for $k\geq 2$. Then
\[E_{\theta}\left(\frac{1}{X+k-1}\right)
= \frac{1}{k-1}\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}\binom{x+k-2}{x}\theta^{k}(1-\theta)^{x}
= \frac{\theta}{k-1}\underbrace{\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}\binom{x+k-2}{x}\theta^{k-1}(1-\theta)^{x}}_{\textstyle =\,1},\]
the sum being the total mass of a NBIN$(k-1,\theta)$ distribution. Hence
\[E_{\theta}\left(\frac{k-1}{X+k-1}\right) = \theta ,\]
and since this is a function of the complete sufficient statistic,
\[\widehat{\theta} = \frac{k-1}{X+k-1}\]
is the UMVUE, provided $k\geq2$.

\begin{remark}
For $k=1$ --- the geometric case --- no unbiased estimator of $\theta$ exists at
all. Any candidate $g(X)$ would have to satisfy
$\sum_x g(x)\theta(1-\theta)^{x} = \theta$ for every $\theta$, forcing
$g(0)=1$ and $g(x)=0$ thereafter; that estimator is unbiased but takes only the
values $0$ and $1$, which is a reminder that unbiasedness on its own is a weak
requirement.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $N(\theta, 1)$ distribution. Find the $UMVUE$ of $\tau(\theta) = \theta^2$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
With unit variance the family is exponential with natural statistic
$\sum_i X_i$, so $\overline{X}$ is complete and sufficient. Since
$\overline{X}\sim N\left(\theta,\ 1/n\right)$,
\[E\left(\overline{X}^{2}\right) = \var\left(\overline{X}\right) + \left[E\left(\overline{X}\right)\right]^{2}
= \frac{1}{n} + \theta^{2}.\]
Subtracting the constant gives an unbiased estimator which, being a function of
the complete sufficient statistic, is the UMVUE:
\[\widehat{\theta^{2}} = \overline{X}^{2} - \frac{1}{n}.\]

As in the EXP$(1,\theta)$ problem earlier, the estimator is negative whenever
$\left|\overline{X}\right| < 1/\sqrt{n}$, although $\theta^{2}$ cannot be. The
same comment applies: unbiasedness constrains the average, not the individual
value.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $N(0, \theta)$ distribution. Find the $UMVUE$ of $\tau (\theta) = \theta^2$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Here $\theta$ is the variance and the mean is known to be zero, so
\[f_{\theta}(x) = \left(2\pi\theta\right)^{-1/2}\exp\left\{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\theta}\right\}\]
is a one-parameter exponential family with natural statistic $\sum_i X_i^{2}$.
Thus $T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i^{2}$ is complete and sufficient, and $T/\theta\sim\chi^{2}_{(n)}$,
so
\[E(T) = n\theta,\qquad \var(T) = 2n\theta^{2},\qquad
E\left(T^{2}\right) = 2n\theta^{2} + n^{2}\theta^{2} = n(n+2)\,\theta^{2}.\]
Therefore
\[\widehat{\theta^{2}} = \frac{T^{2}}{n(n+2)}
= \frac{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i^{2}\right)^{2}}{n(n+2)}\]
is unbiased and hence the UMVUE.

\begin{note}
Contrast this with the previous problem: because the parameter is now the
variance, the natural statistic is the sum of squares rather than the sum, and
the estimator of $\theta^{2}$ is a multiple of $T^{2}$ with no correction term
needed. The divisor $n(n+2)$ rather than $n^{2}$ is exactly the variance of the
chi-square distribution making itself felt.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $POI(\theta)$ distribution. Find the $UMVUE$ for $\tau(\theta) = \left(1 + \theta\right)\, e^{-\theta}$.\\ \, \textit{Hint:}\, \, Find $P(X \leq 1)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Note first that $\tau(\theta) = P_{\theta}\left(X_1\leq 1\right)$, as the hint
suggests, since a Poisson variable takes the values $0$ and $1$ with
probabilities $e^{-\theta}$ and $\theta e^{-\theta}$.

So $U = I\left(X_1\leq 1\right)$ is an unbiased estimator of $\tau(\theta)$,
though a poor one --- it uses one observation and takes only two values.
Rao--Blackwellise it on the complete sufficient statistic
$T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i\sim$ POI$(n\theta)$. Given $T=t$ the conditional
distribution of $X_1$ is binomial with $t$ trials and success probability $1/n$,
so
\begin{align*}
E\left(U\mid T=t\right) &= P\left(X_1\leq 1\mid T=t\right)
 = \left(1-\frac1n\right)^{t} + t\cdot\frac1n\left(1-\frac1n\right)^{t-1}\\[2pt]
 &= \frac{(n-1)^{t} + t\,(n-1)^{t-1}}{n^{t}}
  = \frac{(n-1)^{\,t-1}\left(n-1+t\right)}{n^{t}} .
\end{align*}
Hence the UMVUE is
\[\widehat{\tau} = \frac{(n-1)^{\,T-1}\left(n-1+T\right)}{n^{T}},\]
interpreted as $1$ when $T=0$.

\begin{note}
The three moving parts here are worth separating. Finding \emph{an} unbiased
estimator was easy, because $\tau$ was recognised as a probability. Improving it
was mechanical, because conditioning on a sufficient statistic can only help.
Knowing the result is best required completeness. Every UMVUE in this section is
built the same way.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $POI(\theta)$ distribution. Find the $UMVUE$ for $\tau(\theta) = e^{-2\theta}$.
\[\textit{Hint:}\  E_{\theta}\left[(-1)^{X_1}\right]\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Following the hint,
\[E_{\theta}\left[(-1)^{X_1}\right]
= \sum_{x=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{x}\frac{e^{-\theta}\theta^{x}}{x!}
= e^{-\theta}\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-\theta)^{x}}{x!}
= e^{-\theta}e^{-\theta} = e^{-2\theta},\]
so $U=(-1)^{X_1}$ is unbiased for $\tau(\theta)$.

Rao--Blackwellise on $T=\sum_i X_i$. Given $T=t$ we have $X_1\sim$ BIN$(t,1/n)$,
and the binomial theorem gives
\[E\left[(-1)^{X_1}\mid T=t\right]
= \sum_{x=0}^{t}\binom{t}{x}\left(-\frac1n\right)^{x}\left(1-\frac1n\right)^{t-x}
= \left(1-\frac1n-\frac1n\right)^{t} = \left(\frac{n-2}{n}\right)^{t}.\]
Since $T$ is complete and sufficient, the UMVUE is
\[\widehat{\tau} = \left(\frac{n-2}{n}\right)^{T}.\]

\begin{remark}
For $n=2$ the estimator is $0^{T}$, which is $1$ if $T=0$ and $0$ otherwise, and
for $n=1$ it is $(-1)^{T}$ --- an estimator of a positive quantity that is
negative half the time. Both are the UMVUE at those sample sizes, which shows
how little ``uniformly minimum variance unbiased'' guarantees when the sample is
small. The estimator becomes sensible only once $n$ is large enough that
$(n-2)/n$ is close to one.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $GAM(2, \theta)$ distribution. Find the $UMVUE$ of $\tau_1(\theta) = \frac{1}{\theta}$ and the $UMVUE$ of $\tau_2(\theta) = P\left(X_1 > c\right)$ where $c>0$ is a constant.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Take GAM$(2,\theta)$ to have shape $2$ and scale $\theta$, so that
$f_{\theta}(x) = x e^{-x/\theta}/\theta^{2}$ for $x>0$. This is a one-parameter
exponential family with natural statistic $\sum_i X_i$, so
$T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i\sim$ GAM$(2n,\theta)$ is complete and sufficient.

\subhead{The UMVUE of $\tau_1(\theta)=1/\theta$}
For a gamma variable of shape $a$ and scale $\theta$,
$E\left(T^{-1}\right) = 1/\left[\theta(a-1)\right]$. With $a=2n$,
\[E\left(\frac{1}{T}\right) = \frac{1}{\theta\,(2n-1)}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\widehat{\tau_1} = \frac{2n-1}{T} .\]

\subhead{The UMVUE of $\tau_2(\theta)=P\left(X_1>c\right)$}
Start from the unbiased indicator $U = I\left(X_1>c\right)$ and condition on
$T$. Given $T=t$, the ratio $X_1/T$ has the Beta$(2,\,2n-2)$ distribution ---
the standard result for independent gamma variables with a common scale, and
note the scale $\theta$ has cancelled, which is what makes the conditional law
free of the parameter. Hence for $t>c$,
\[P\left(X_1>c \mid T=t\right)
= \int_{c/t}^{1}(2n-1)(2n-2)\,u\,(1-u)^{2n-3}\,du
= \left(1-\frac{c}{t}\right)^{2n-2}\left[1+(2n-2)\frac{c}{t}\right],\]
and the probability is zero when $t\leq c$. The UMVUE is therefore
\[\widehat{\tau_2} =
\begin{cases}
\left(1-\dfrac{c}{T}\right)^{2n-2}\left[1+(2n-2)\dfrac{c}{T}\right], & T>c,\\[10pt]
0, & T\leq c .
\end{cases}\]

A check: at $n=1$ the sample is the single observation $X_1=T$, the exponent
$2n-2$ is zero and the bracket is one, so the estimator reduces to
$I\left(X_1>c\right)$ --- exactly the indicator we started from, as it must.
\end{solution}

\section{Ancillary Statistics}

Information about $\theta$ comes from the way the distribution of a statistic
changes with $\theta$. A statistic whose distribution does not change with $\theta$
at all would seem to carry no information about it.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:ancillary}
A statistic $U(X)$ is \emph{ancillary} if its distribution does not depend on
$\theta$.
\end{defn}

For example, if $X_1, \dots, X_n$ is a sample from a location family
$f(x - \theta)$, then $X_i - \theta$ has a distribution free of $\theta$, and so does
any function of the differences $X_i - X_j$, such as the range
$X_{(n)} - X_{(1)}$ or $S^2$. For a scale family $\frac1\theta f\left(\frac x\theta\right)$,
ratios such as $\frac{X_1}{X_2}$ are ancillary.

The next theorem uses completeness and ancillarity to prove that two statistics
are independent without finding their joint distribution.

\begin{thm}[Basu]
\label{thm:basu}
If $T(X)$ is a complete sufficient statistic, then $T(X)$ is independent of every
ancillary statistic $U(X)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
We must show that $P_\theta(U\in B, T\in C) = P_\theta(U\in B)P_\theta(T\in C)$ for
all sets $B$, $C$ and all $\theta$. Fix $B$ and define
\[g(t) = P(U\in B\mid T = t) - P(U\in B) .\]
The first term does not depend on $\theta$ by sufficiency of $T$, and the second
does not by ancillarity of $U$, so $g(T)$ is a statistic. By the law of total
expectation,
\[E_\theta\left[g(T)\right] = E_\theta\left[P(U\in B\mid T)\right] - P(U\in B) = P(U\in B) - P(U\in B) = 0\]
for every $\theta$. Completeness gives $g(T) = 0$ with probability one, that is,
$P(U\in B\mid T = t) = P(U\in B)$ for almost all $t$. Hence, writing
$f_T(t;\theta)$ for the density of $T$,
\begin{align*}
P_\theta(U\in B, T\in C) &= \int_C P(U\in B\mid T = t)f_T(t;\theta)\,dt\\
&= P(U\in B)\int_C f_T(t;\theta)\,dt = P(U\in B)P_\theta(T\in C),
\end{align*}
with a sum in place of the integral in the discrete case.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:basuexponential}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the exponential distribution with
mean $\theta$. Show that $T = \sum X_i$ and $U = \left(\frac{X_1}{T}, \dots, \frac{X_n}{T}\right)$
are independent, and use this to find $E(X_1\mid T)$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
The variables $Z_i = \frac{X_i}{\theta}$ are independent $\text{EXP}(1)$, whose
distribution is free of $\theta$. Since
\[\frac{X_i}{T} = \frac{X_i/\theta}{\sum_j X_j/\theta} = \frac{Z_i}{\sum_j Z_j},\]
$U$ is a function of $(Z_1, \dots, Z_n)$ and is ancillary. By
example~\ref{ex:exponentialumvue}, $T$ is complete and sufficient. By
theorem~\ref{thm:basu}, $T$ and $U$ are independent.

Now $X_1 = \frac{X_1}{T}\cdot T$. Taking expectations and using independence,
$\theta = E(X_1) = E\left(\frac{X_1}{T}\right)E(T) = E\left(\frac{X_1}{T}\right)n\theta$,
so $E\left(\frac{X_1}{T}\right) = \frac1n$. Conditioning on $T$, and again using
independence,
\[E(X_1\mid T) = T\,E\left(\frac{X_1}{T} \mid  T\right) = T\,E\left(\frac{X_1}{T}\right) = \frac Tn = \bar X .\]
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:basunormal}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$. Prove that $\bar X$
and $S^2$ are independent.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Fix $\sigma^2$ and regard $\mu$ as the only parameter. The family
$N(\mu, \sigma^2)$ with $\sigma^2$ known is a REF with $\eta = \frac{\mu}{\sigma^2}$, so
$\bar X$ is complete and sufficient for $\mu$. Write $Z_i = X_i - \mu$, which are
$N(0, \sigma^2)$ with distribution free of $\mu$. Then $X_i - \bar X = Z_i - \bar Z$,
so
\[S^2 = \frac{1}{n - 1}\sum (X_i - \bar X)^2 = \frac{1}{n - 1}\sum (Z_i - \bar Z)^2\]
is a function of the $Z_i$ and is ancillary for $\mu$. By theorem~\ref{thm:basu},
$\bar X$ and $S^2$ are independent when the true variance is $\sigma^2$. Since
$\sigma^2$ was arbitrary, they are independent for all $\mu$ and $\sigma^2$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $GAM(\alpha, \beta)$ distribution. Show that $T(X) = \sum^n_{i = 1} X_i$ and $U(X) = \left(\frac{X_1}{T}, \, \cdots\, , \, \frac{X_n}{T}\right)$ are independent random variables. Find $E\left(\frac{X_1}{T}\right)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\subhead{Independence}
Treat $\alpha$ as known and $\beta$ as the parameter. The model is then a
one-parameter exponential family with natural statistic $T=\sum_i X_i$, so $T$
is complete and sufficient for $\beta$.

The vector $U$ is \emph{ancillary}: replacing each $X_i$ by $cX_i$ for a
constant $c>0$ leaves every ratio $X_i/T$ unchanged, and such a rescaling is
exactly what changes one value of $\beta$ into another. So the distribution of
$U$ does not depend on $\beta$ at all. By Basu's theorem a complete sufficient
statistic is independent of every ancillary statistic, hence $T$ and $U$ are
independent.

\subhead{The expectation}
The components of $U$ sum to one:
\[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{X_i}{T} = \frac{T}{T} = 1 .\]
Taking expectations, $\sum_{i=1}^{n}E\left(X_i/T\right) = 1$. The $X_i$ are
identically distributed and enter $T$ symmetrically, so all $n$ terms are equal,
giving
\[E\left(\frac{X_1}{T}\right) = \frac{1}{n}.\]

\begin{note}
The expectation was obtained without integrating anything. Symmetry plus the
fact that the ratios sum to one does all the work --- a pattern worth
remembering, because the direct calculation requires the Dirichlet distribution
of $U$ and is far longer.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\subsection*{Further reading}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Fisher, R. A. (1922), On the mathematical foundations of theoretical
statistics, \emph{Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society A}, 222,
309--368.
\item Koopman, B. O. (1936), On distributions admitting a sufficient statistic,
\emph{Transactions of the American Mathematical Society}, 39, 399--409.
\item Rao, C. R. (1945), Information and the accuracy attainable in the estimation
of statistical parameters, \emph{Bulletin of the Calcutta Mathematical Society},
37, 81--91.
\item Blackwell, D. (1947), Conditional expectation and unbiased sequential
estimation, \emph{Annals of Mathematical Statistics}, 18, 105--110.
\item Lehmann, E. L., and Scheff\'e, H. (1950), Completeness, similar regions, and
unbiased estimation, part I, \emph{Sankhy\=a}, 10, 305--340.
\item Basu, D. (1955), On statistics independent of a complete sufficient
statistic, \emph{Sankhy\=a}, 15, 377--380.
\item Bahadur, R. R. (1957), On unbiased estimates of uniformly minimum variance,
\emph{Sankhy\=a}, 18, 211--224.
\item Boos, D. D., and Hughes-Oliver, J. M. (1998), Applications of Basu's theorem,
\emph{The American Statistician}, 52, 218--221.
\end{enumerate}


\chapter{Maximum Likelihood Estimation}
\label{ch:mle}

\section{Introduction}

Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the model
$\{f_\theta(x) : \theta\in\Omega\}$, with observed values $x = (x_1, \dots, x_n)$.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:likelihood}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The \emph{likelihood function} is $L(\theta) = L(\theta; x) = \prod^n_{i = 1} f_\theta(x_i)$,
regarded as a function of $\theta$ for the fixed data.
\item The \emph{log likelihood} is $\mathcal{L}(\theta) = \ln L(\theta)$.
\item The \emph{score function} is $S(\theta) = S(\theta; x) = \frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathcal{L}(\theta)$.
\item The \emph{information function} (observed information) is
$I(\theta) = I(\theta; x) = -\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}S(\theta) = -\frac{\partial^2}{\partial\theta^2}\mathcal{L}(\theta)$.
\item The \emph{Fisher information} (expected information) is
$J(\theta) = E_\theta\left[I(\theta; X)\right]$.
\item A value $\hat\theta = \hat\theta(x)$ that maximises $L(\theta)$ over $\Omega$ is a
\emph{maximum likelihood estimate} (ML estimate) of $\theta$; the random variable
$\hat\theta(X)$ is the \emph{maximum likelihood estimator}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{defn}

In many problems $\hat\theta$ is found by solving the \emph{score equation}
$S(\theta) = 0$ and checking that $I(\hat\theta)>0$, so that the stationary point is a
maximum. Since $\ln$ is increasing, maximising $L$ and maximising $\mathcal{L}$
are the same, and the logarithm turns the product into a sum.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:scoresum}
Show that for a random sample both the score and the information are sums of $n$
terms of identical form.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$\mathcal{L}(\theta) = \sum_{i=1}^n\ln f_\theta(x_i)$, so
\[S(\theta) = \sum^n_{i = 1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\ln f_\theta(x_i)
= \sum^n_{i = 1}\frac{f'_\theta(x_i)}{f_\theta(x_i)},\]
where the prime denotes $\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}$. Differentiating once more
by the quotient rule,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:infosum}
I(\theta) = -\sum^n_{i = 1}\frac{f_\theta(x_i)f''_\theta(x_i) - \left[f'_\theta(x_i)\right]^2}{\left[f_\theta(x_i)\right]^2}
= \sum^n_{i = 1}\left[\left(\frac{f'_\theta(x_i)}{f_\theta(x_i)}\right)^2 - \frac{f''_\theta(x_i)}{f_\theta(x_i)}\right].
\end{equation}
Each is a sum of $n$ terms, the $i$th depending on $x_i$ only. Writing $S_1$,
$I_1$ and $J_1$ for the score, information and Fisher information of a single
observation, $S(\theta) = \sum S_1(\theta; x_i)$, $I(\theta) = \sum I_1(\theta; x_i)$
and, taking expectations, $J(\theta) = nJ_1(\theta)$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissonmle}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{POI}(\theta)$. Find the ML
estimator of $\theta$ and the Fisher information, and compare $\frac{1}{J(\theta)}$
with $\var(\hat\theta)$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[L(\theta) = \frac{e^{-n\theta}\theta^{\sum x_i}}{\prod x_i!},\qquad
\mathcal{L}(\theta) = -n\theta + \left(\sum x_i\right)\ln\theta - \ln\prod x_i!,\]
\[S(\theta) = -n + \frac{\sum x_i}{\theta},\qquad I(\theta) = \frac{\sum x_i}{\theta^2} .\]
The score equation gives $\hat\theta = \bar x$, and $I(\hat\theta) = \frac{n}{\bar x}>0$
when $\bar x>0$, so this is a maximum. (If every $x_i = 0$ the likelihood $e^{-n\theta}$
is decreasing and is maximised at the boundary, $\hat\theta = 0 = \bar x$ again.)
The Fisher information is
\[J(\theta) = E\left[\frac{\sum X_i}{\theta^2}\right] = \frac{n\theta}{\theta^2} = \frac n\theta .\]
Using a single observation instead, $I_1(\theta) = \frac{x}{\theta^2}$ and
$J_1(\theta) = \frac1\theta$, and $J = nJ_1$ as example~\ref{ex:scoresum} says.
Finally $\var(\hat\theta) = \var(\bar X) = \frac\theta n = \frac{1}{J(\theta)}$. This is
no accident: section~\ref{sec:crlb} shows that $\frac{1}{J(\theta)}$ is a lower bound
for the variance of any unbiased estimator, and here it is attained.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Suppose that $X\thicksim BIN(n,\theta)$ and we observe $X$. Find the M.L estimator of $\theta$, the score function and the Fisher information.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\[\log L(\theta) = \log\binom{n}{x} + x\log\theta + (n-x)\log(1-\theta).\]
Differentiating gives the score
\[S(\theta,X) = \frac{X}{\theta} - \frac{n-X}{1-\theta}
= \frac{X-n\theta}{\theta(1-\theta)} .\]
Setting it to zero gives $X(1-\theta)=(n-X)\theta$, that is $X=n\theta$, so
\[\widehat{\theta} = \frac{X}{n}.\]
For the information, $\partial^{2}\log L/\partial\theta^{2}
= -X/\theta^{2} - (n-X)/(1-\theta)^{2}$, and since $E(X)=n\theta$,
\[I(\theta) = -E\left[\frac{\partial^{2}\log L}{\partial\theta^{2}}\right]
= \frac{n\theta}{\theta^{2}} + \frac{n(1-\theta)}{(1-\theta)^{2}}
= \frac{n}{\theta} + \frac{n}{1-\theta}
= \frac{n}{\theta(1-\theta)} .\]
Note that $\var\left(\widehat{\theta}\right)=\theta(1-\theta)/n = 1/I(\theta)$:
the estimator attains the information bound exactly, which the form of the score
--- affine in $X$ --- already predicted.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Suppose that $X\thicksim NB(k,\theta)$ and we observe $X$. Find the M.L estimator of $\theta$, the Score function and the Fisher information.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
With $P_{\theta}(X=x)=\binom{x+k-1}{x}\theta^{k}(1-\theta)^{x}$,
\[\log L(\theta) = \text{const} + k\log\theta + X\log(1-\theta),\]
so the score is
\[S(\theta,X) = \frac{k}{\theta} - \frac{X}{1-\theta} .\]
Setting it to zero, $k(1-\theta)=X\theta$, giving
\[\widehat{\theta} = \frac{k}{k+X}.\]
Since $\partial^{2}\log L/\partial\theta^{2} = -k/\theta^{2} - X/(1-\theta)^{2}$
and $E(X)=k(1-\theta)/\theta$,
\[I(\theta) = \frac{k}{\theta^{2}} + \frac{k(1-\theta)}{\theta(1-\theta)^{2}}
= \frac{k}{\theta^{2}} + \frac{k}{\theta(1-\theta)}
= \frac{k}{\theta^{2}(1-\theta)} .\]
Here the score is \emph{not} affine in $\widehat{\theta}$, and indeed the
maximum likelihood estimator is biased --- its expectation exceeds $\theta$ ---
so the information bound is not attained at finite $k$.
\end{solution}

The score equation is not always the way to the maximum. When the support depends
on $\theta$, the likelihood can be discontinuous and the maximum can occur at an
end point.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:uniformmle}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{UNIF}(0, \theta)$. Find the ML
estimator of $\theta$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
As in example~\ref{ex:uniformsufficient},
\[L(\theta) = \frac{1}{\theta^n}I\left(x_{(n)}<\theta\right)I\left(x_{(1)}>0\right).\]
For $\theta<x_{(n)}$ some observation exceeds $\theta$ and the likelihood is $0$;
for $\theta\ge x_{(n)}$ it is $\theta^{-n}$, which decreases in $\theta$
(figure~\ref{fig:uniformlik}). The maximum is at the smallest admissible value, so
$\hat\theta = X_{(n)}$. (Strictly, with the open interval $(0, \theta)$ the supremum is
approached as $\theta\downarrow x_{(n)}$; using the closed interval $[0, \theta]$, as is
usual, the maximum is attained at $x_{(n)}$.) The score $-\frac n\theta$ is never zero,
so the score equation would not have found it.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[
    width=9cm, height=5.5cm,
    xlabel={$\theta$}, ylabel={$L(\theta)$},
    xmin=0, xmax=4, ymin=0, ymax=1.15,
    axis lines=left, xtick={1.2}, xticklabels={$x_{(n)}$}, ytick=\empty]
\addplot[very thick, blue, domain=0:1.2] {0};
\addplot[very thick, blue, smooth, samples=60, domain=1.2:4] {(1.2/x)^3};
\addplot[only marks, mark=*] coordinates {(1.2,1)};
\addplot[only marks, mark=*, mark options={fill=white}] coordinates {(1.2,0)};
\draw[dashed, gray] (axis cs:1.2,0) -- (axis cs:1.2,1);
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The likelihood for a $\text{UNIF}(0, \theta)$ sample is zero below the
largest observation and decreasing above it, so it is maximised at $x_{(n)}$.}
\label{fig:uniformlik}
\end{figure}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from a distribution with pdf \[f_{\theta}(x) = \theta\, x^{\theta - 1}\  , \  0 < x < 1\ , \  \theta > 0.\]
Find the M.L estimator of $\theta$,  the scored function and the Fisher information.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\[\log L(\theta) = n\log\theta + (\theta-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log X_i ,\]
so the score is
\[S(\theta,X) = \frac{n}{\theta} + \sum_{i=1}^{n}\log X_i .\]
Setting it to zero and writing $T=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log X_i>0$,
\[\widehat{\theta} = \frac{n}{T} = \frac{-n}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log X_i}.\]
The second derivative is $-n/\theta^{2}$, which is already free of the data, so
\[I_n(\theta) = \frac{n}{\theta^{2}},\]
that is $1/\theta^{2}$ per observation.

Comparing with the earlier problem on this same density: the UMVUE was
$(n-1)/T$ while the maximum likelihood estimator is $n/T$. The latter is biased
upwards by a factor $n/(n-1)$, and the two agree to first order in $n$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Suppose $X_1, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$  is a random sample from the $UNIF(\theta, \theta + 1)$ distribution. Show that the M.L estimator of $\theta$  is not unique.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The likelihood is
\[L(\theta) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} I\left(\theta<x_i<\theta+1\right)
= I\left(x_{(n)}-1 < \theta < x_{(1)}\right),\]
which takes only the values $0$ and $1$. It equals $1$ for \emph{every} $\theta$
in the interval $\left(x_{(n)}-1,\ x_{(1)}\right)$ and $0$ outside it.

Since the likelihood is constant on that interval, every point of it maximises
the likelihood equally well. The interval has positive length whenever the
sample range is less than one, which happens with probability one, so the
maximum likelihood estimator is not unique --- there is a continuum of them.
Common conventions pick $x_{(n)}-1$, or $x_{(1)}$, or the midpoint
$\left(x_{(1)}+x_{(n)}-1\right)/2$, but nothing in the likelihood principle
prefers any of them.

\begin{note}
This is the standard warning that ``the'' maximum likelihood estimator is a
figure of speech. Uniqueness needs an argument, and here there is none to be
had: the data restrict $\theta$ to an interval and say nothing whatever about
where in that interval it lies.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{thm}[Invariance]
\label{thm:invariance}
If $\hat\theta$ is the ML estimator of $\theta$ and $\tau$ is any function, then
$\tau(\hat\theta)$ is the ML estimator of $\tau(\theta)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
When $\tau$ is one-to-one, write $\eta = \tau(\theta)$; the likelihood as a function of
$\eta$ is $L(\tau^{-1}(\eta))$, which is maximised where $\tau^{-1}(\eta) = \hat\theta$,
that is, at $\eta = \tau(\hat\theta)$. For a general $\tau$ the likelihood of $\eta$ is
defined as the \emph{induced likelihood}
$L^*(\eta) = \sup\{L(\theta) : \tau(\theta) = \eta\}$. Then for every $\eta$,
$L^*(\eta)\le\sup_\theta L(\theta) = L(\hat\theta)$, while at $\eta = \tau(\hat\theta)$ the
set over which the supremum is taken contains $\hat\theta$, so
$L^*(\tau(\hat\theta)) = L(\hat\theta)$. Hence $\tau(\hat\theta)$ maximises $L^*$.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissoninvariance}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{POI}(\theta)$. Find the ML
estimator of $\tau(\theta) = e^{-\theta} = P(X = 0)$, and compare it with the UMVUE.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
By example~\ref{ex:poissonmle}, $\hat\theta = \bar X$, so by
theorem~\ref{thm:invariance} the ML estimator of $e^{-\theta}$ is $e^{-\bar X}$. The
UMVUE found in example~\ref{ex:poissonzero} is $\left(1 - \frac1n\right)^{n\bar X}$. The
two differ: for $n = 10$ and $\bar x = 1.5$ they are $e^{-1.5} = 0.223$ and
$0.9^{15} = 0.206$. The ML estimator is biased, since
$E\left(e^{-\bar X}\right) = \exp\left(-n\theta(1 - e^{-1/n})\right)\ne e^{-\theta}$, but
the difference vanishes as $n\to\infty$.
\end{solution}

\section{Properties of the Score and Information}

Let $A = \{x : f_\theta(x)>0\}$ be the support. Since $\int_A f_\theta(x)\,dx = 1$
for every $\theta$,
\[\int_A\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}f_\theta(x)\,dx = \frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\int_A f_\theta(x)\,dx = 0,\]
provided the derivative and the integral can be interchanged, and similarly for
the second derivative. Models in which $A$ does not depend on $\theta$ and these
interchanges are valid are called \emph{regular}. The uniform model of
example~\ref{ex:uniformmle} is not regular.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:scoreproperties}
If $X = (X_1, \dots, X_n)$ is a random sample from a regular model, then
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $E_\theta\left[S(\theta; X)\right] = 0$, and
\item[(ii)] $\var_\theta\left[S(\theta; X)\right] = E_\theta\left[S(\theta; X)^2\right] = J(\theta)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] For one observation,
\[E_\theta\left[S_1(\theta; X)\right] = \int_A\frac{f'_\theta(x)}{f_\theta(x)}f_\theta(x)\,dx
= \int_A f'_\theta(x)\,dx = 0 ,\]
and $S$ is a sum of $n$ such terms.
\item[(ii)] By (i), $\var(S) = E(S^2)$. Since $S = \sum_i S_1(\theta; X_i)$ with
independent terms of mean zero, the cross terms
$E\left[S_1(\theta; X_i)S_1(\theta; X_j)\right] = 0$ for $i\ne j$, and
\[E(S^2) = \sum_{i=1}^n E\left[S_1(\theta; X_i)^2\right]
= \sum_{i=1}^n E\left[\left(\frac{f'_\theta(X_i)}{f_\theta(X_i)}\right)^2\right].\]
On the other hand, taking expectations in \eqref{eq:infosum},
\[J(\theta) = \sum_{i=1}^n E\left[\left(\frac{f'_\theta(X_i)}{f_\theta(X_i)}\right)^2\right]
- \sum_{i=1}^n E\left[\frac{f''_\theta(X_i)}{f_\theta(X_i)}\right],\]
and each term of the second sum is
$\int_A f''_\theta(x)\,dx = \frac{\partial^2}{\partial\theta^2}\int_A f_\theta(x)\,dx = 0$.
So $J(\theta) = E(S^2)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissonscore}
Verify theorem~\ref{thm:scoreproperties} for a random sample from $\text{POI}(\theta)$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
From example~\ref{ex:poissonmle}, $S(\theta) = -n + \frac1\theta\sum X_i$ and
$J(\theta) = \frac n\theta$. Then
\[E(S) = -n + \frac{n\theta}{\theta} = 0,\qquad
\var(S) = \frac{1}{\theta^2}\sum\var(X_i) = \frac{n\theta}{\theta^2} = \frac n\theta = J(\theta).\]
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:exponentialinfo}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the exponential distribution with
mean $\theta$. Find the ML estimator, the observed and Fisher information, and
check that $J(\theta) = \var(S)$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$\mathcal{L}(\theta) = -n\ln\theta - \frac1\theta\sum x_i$, so
\[S(\theta) = -\frac n\theta + \frac{\sum x_i}{\theta^2},\qquad
I(\theta) = -\frac{n}{\theta^2} + \frac{2\sum x_i}{\theta^3} .\]
The score equation gives $\hat\theta = \bar x$, and $I(\hat\theta) = -\frac{n}{\bar x^2} +
\frac{2n}{\bar x^2} = \frac{n}{\bar x^2}>0$. Since $E(X_i) = \theta$,
\[J(\theta) = -\frac{n}{\theta^2} + \frac{2n\theta}{\theta^3} = \frac{n}{\theta^2},\qquad
\var(S) = \frac{1}{\theta^4}\sum\var(X_i) = \frac{n\theta^2}{\theta^4} = \frac{n}{\theta^2} .\]
Note that $I(\theta)$ depends on the data and can be negative for $\theta$ far from
$\hat\theta$; only its expectation $J(\theta)$ is guaranteed to be positive.
\end{solution}

\subsection{The Multiparameter Score and Information}

When $\theta = (\theta_1, \dots, \theta_k)^{\mathsf T}$ is a vector the same ideas
apply componentwise.
\begin{itemize}
\item The score is the vector
$S(\theta) = \left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\theta_1}, \dots, \frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\theta_k}\right)^{\mathsf T}$.
\item The information is the $k\times k$ matrix
$I(\theta) = \left[-\frac{\partial^2\mathcal{L}}{\partial\theta_i\,\partial\theta_j}\right]$.
\item The Fisher information is the matrix $J(\theta)$ with entries
$J_{ij}(\theta) = E\left[I_{ij}(\theta; X)\right]$.
\end{itemize}
For a regular model $E\left[S(\theta; X)\right] = 0$ and
$J(\theta) = \var\left[S(\theta; X)\right] = E\left[S(\theta; X)S(\theta; X)^{\mathsf T}\right]$,
by the argument of theorem~\ref{thm:scoreproperties} applied to each pair of
components. Invariance (theorem~\ref{thm:invariance}) also holds for vector
parameters.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:normalmle}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$. Find the ML
estimator of $\theta = (\mu, \sigma^2)^{\mathsf T}$, the score and the Fisher
information.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Write $\theta_1 = \mu$ and $\theta_2 = \sigma^2$. Then
\[\mathcal{L}(\theta) = -\frac n2\ln(2\pi\theta_2) - \frac{1}{2\theta_2}\sum^n_{i = 1}(x_i - \theta_1)^2,\]
and the score is
\[S(\theta) = \begin{pmatrix}
\frac{1}{\theta_2}\sum(x_i - \theta_1)\\[1ex]
-\frac{n}{2\theta_2} + \frac{1}{2\theta_2^2}\sum(x_i - \theta_1)^2
\end{pmatrix}.\]
Setting the first component to zero gives $\hat\theta_1 = \bar x$; substituting into
the second gives $\hat\theta_2 = \frac1n\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2$. So
\[\hat\theta = \left(\bar X, \frac1n\sum(X_i - \bar X)^2\right)^{\mathsf T} = \left(\bar X, \frac{n - 1}{n}S^2\right)^{\mathsf T} .\]
Differentiating again,
\[I(\theta) = \begin{pmatrix}
\frac{n}{\theta_2} & \frac{1}{\theta_2^2}\sum(x_i - \theta_1)\\[1ex]
\frac{1}{\theta_2^2}\sum(x_i - \theta_1) & -\frac{n}{2\theta_2^2} + \frac{1}{\theta_2^3}\sum(x_i - \theta_1)^2
\end{pmatrix}.\]
Since $E(X_i - \theta_1) = 0$ and $E(X_i - \theta_1)^2 = \theta_2$,
\[J(\theta) = \begin{pmatrix}
\frac{n}{\theta_2} & 0\\[1ex]
0 & -\frac{n}{2\theta_2^2} + \frac{n}{\theta_2^2}
\end{pmatrix}
= \begin{pmatrix}
\frac{n}{\sigma^2} & 0\\[1ex]
0 & \frac{n}{2\sigma^4}
\end{pmatrix}.\]
At $\hat\theta$ the off-diagonal entries of $I$ vanish and the diagonal entries are
$\frac{n}{\hat\sigma^2}$ and $\frac{n}{2\hat\sigma^4}$, so $I(\hat\theta)$ is positive
definite and $\hat\theta$ is a maximum. The ML estimator of $\sigma^2$ is biased,
with mean $\frac{n - 1}{n}\sigma^2$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:multinomialmle}
Let $(X_1, \, X_2, \, X_3) \thicksim MULT(n, \, \theta_1, \, \theta_2, \, \theta_3)$. Find the M.L estimator of $\theta = (\theta_1, \theta_2)^t$, the score function and the Fisher information.
\textit{Hint:}\, use 
\[f_{\theta}(x_1, x_2, x_3)  = \frac{n!}{x_1!\, x_2!\, (n - x_1 - x_2)!}\, \theta_1^{x_1}\, \theta_2^{x_2}\, \left(1 - \theta_1 - \theta_2\right)^{n - x_1 - x_2}\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Write $\theta_3 = 1-\theta_1-\theta_2$ and $x_3 = n-x_1-x_2$. Then
\[\log L = \text{const} + x_1\log\theta_1 + x_2\log\theta_2 + x_3\log\left(1-\theta_1-\theta_2\right).\]

\subhead{Score}
\[S_j(\theta,X) = \frac{X_j}{\theta_j} - \frac{X_3}{1-\theta_1-\theta_2},
\qquad j=1,2 .\]

\subhead{Maximum likelihood estimator}
Setting both components to zero gives
$X_1/\theta_1 = X_2/\theta_2 = X_3/\theta_3$; with
$\theta_1+\theta_2+\theta_3=1$ and $X_1+X_2+X_3=n$ the common ratio is $n$, so
\[\widehat{\theta}_1 = \frac{X_1}{n},\qquad \widehat{\theta}_2 = \frac{X_2}{n} .\]

\subhead{Fisher information}
Differentiating again, and using $E(X_j)=n\theta_j$,
\[-\frac{\partial^{2}\log L}{\partial\theta_j^{2}} = \frac{X_j}{\theta_j^{2}} + \frac{X_3}{\theta_3^{2}},
\qquad
-\frac{\partial^{2}\log L}{\partial\theta_1\partial\theta_2} = \frac{X_3}{\theta_3^{2}},\]
so
\[J(\theta) = n\begin{pmatrix}
\dfrac{1}{\theta_1}+\dfrac{1}{\theta_3} & \dfrac{1}{\theta_3}\\[8pt]
\dfrac{1}{\theta_3} & \dfrac{1}{\theta_2}+\dfrac{1}{\theta_3}
\end{pmatrix}.\]
The off-diagonal entries are not zero: the two estimators are negatively
correlated, as they must be, because a larger $\widehat{\theta}_1$ leaves less
probability for $\theta_2$.
\end{solution}

\subsection{The Likelihood Equation and its Solution}

Often the score equation $S(\theta) = 0$ has no closed-form solution and must be
solved numerically. \emph{Newton's method} (the Newton--Raphson method) starts from
an approximate value $\theta^{(0)}$ and updates it by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:newton}
\theta^{(i + 1)} = \theta^{(i)} + \frac{S\left(\theta^{(i)}\right)}{I\left(\theta^{(i)}\right)},\qquad i = 0, 1, 2, \dots ,
\end{equation}
until successive values agree to the required accuracy. In the multiparameter case
the update is
\[\theta^{(i + 1)} = \theta^{(i)} + \left[I\left(\theta^{(i)}\right)\right]^{-1}S\left(\theta^{(i)}\right).\]
Replacing the observed information $I$ by the Fisher information $J$ gives
\emph{Fisher's method of scoring}, which is often more stable because $J$ is
positive definite.

The update comes from linearising the score about the current value. By Taylor's
theorem $S(\theta)\approx S(\theta^{(i)}) + (\theta - \theta^{(i)})S'(\theta^{(i)})$,
and $S' = -I$. Setting the approximation to zero and solving for $\theta$ gives
\eqref{eq:newton}. If the starting value is close enough to a root at which $I>0$,
the iteration converges, and the number of correct digits roughly doubles at each
step.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:weibullnewton}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the Weibull distribution
$f_\theta(x) = \theta x^{\theta - 1}e^{-x^\theta}$, $x>0$, $\theta>0$. Explain how to
find the ML estimate of $\theta$, and carry out the method for the data
$0.6,\ 1.2,\ 0.9,\ 1.8,\ 0.4$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[\mathcal{L}(\theta) = n\ln\theta + (\theta - 1)\sum\ln x_i - \sum x_i^\theta ,\]
\[S(\theta) = \frac n\theta + \sum\ln x_i - \sum x_i^\theta\ln x_i,\qquad
I(\theta) = \frac{n}{\theta^2} + \sum x_i^\theta(\ln x_i)^2 .\]
The equation $S(\theta) = 0$ cannot be solved explicitly, but $I(\theta)>0$ for every
$\theta$, so $\mathcal{L}$ is strictly concave and has exactly one maximum, which
\eqref{eq:newton} finds from any reasonable start.

For the data, $n = 5$ and $\sum\ln x_i = -0.7624$. Starting at $\theta^{(0)} = 1$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
$i$ & $\theta^{(i)}$ & $S(\theta^{(i)})$ & $I(\theta^{(i)})$\\
\hline
0 & 1.0000 & 3.7287 & 6.1642\\
1 & 1.6049 & 1.1236 & 3.1904\\
2 & 1.9571 & 0.1012 & 2.6892\\
3 & 1.9947 & 0.0006 & 2.6586\\
4 & 1.9949 & 0.0000 & 2.6584
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
So $\hat\theta = 1.995$, and $\frac{1}{\sqrt{I(\hat\theta)}} = 0.613$ is its approximate
standard error (section~\ref{sec:largesample}).
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:gammamle}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{GAM}(\alpha, \beta)$, with
$\theta = (\alpha, \beta)^{\mathsf T}$. Explain how to obtain the ML estimate, and
find the Fisher information.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[\mathcal{L}(\theta) = (\alpha - 1)\sum\ln x_i - \frac1\beta\sum x_i - n\alpha\ln\beta - n\ln\Gamma(\alpha).\]
With $\psi(\alpha) = \frac{d}{d\alpha}\ln\Gamma(\alpha)$, the digamma function, the
score is
\[S(\theta) = \begin{pmatrix}
\sum\ln x_i - n\ln\beta - n\psi(\alpha)\\[1ex]
\frac{1}{\beta^2}\sum x_i - \frac{n\alpha}{\beta}
\end{pmatrix}.\]
The second equation gives $\beta = \frac{\bar x}{\alpha}$; substituting into the first
leaves the single equation
\[\ln\alpha - \psi(\alpha) = \ln\bar x - \frac1n\sum\ln x_i ,\]
which has no closed-form solution and is solved by Newton's method. (The right
side is the log of the ratio of the arithmetic to the geometric mean, which is
positive unless all the $x_i$ are equal.) The information matrix is
\[I(\theta) = \begin{pmatrix}
n\psi'(\alpha) & \frac n\beta\\[1ex]
\frac n\beta & \frac{2}{\beta^3}\sum x_i - \frac{n\alpha}{\beta^2}
\end{pmatrix},\]
and since $E\left(\sum X_i\right) = n\alpha\beta$,
\[J(\theta) = n\begin{pmatrix}
\psi'(\alpha) & \frac1\beta\\[1ex]
\frac1\beta & \frac{\alpha}{\beta^2}
\end{pmatrix}.\]
Here $\psi'$ is the trigamma function. The iteration is
$\theta^{(i+1)} = \theta^{(i)} + \left[I(\theta^{(i)})\right]^{-1}S(\theta^{(i)})$,
started for example from the method of moments estimates
$\alpha^{(0)} = \frac{\bar x^2}{s^2}$ and $\beta^{(0)} = \frac{s^2}{\bar x}$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:lomaxmle}
Suppose $X_1, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ is a random sample from the distribution with pdf  \[f_{\theta}(x) = \frac{\alpha\beta}{\left(1 + \beta x\right)^{\alpha + 1}}\quad , \quad  x > 0;\quad  \alpha, \, \beta > 0\]
Explain how you find the $M.L$ estimate of $\theta = (\alpha, \beta)^t$. Find the Fisher information.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\subhead{Finding the estimate}
\[\log L = n\log\alpha + n\log\beta - (\alpha+1)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\left(1+\beta x_i\right).\]
Differentiating,
\[\frac{\partial \log L}{\partial\alpha} = \frac{n}{\alpha} - \sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\left(1+\beta x_i\right),
\qquad
\frac{\partial \log L}{\partial\beta} = \frac{n}{\beta} - (\alpha+1)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{x_i}{1+\beta x_i}.\]
The first equation solves explicitly for $\alpha$ in terms of $\beta$,
\[\widehat{\alpha}(\beta) = \frac{n}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\left(1+\beta x_i\right)} .\]
Substituting this into the second leaves a single equation in $\beta$ alone,
which has no closed-form solution and must be solved numerically --- Newton's
method or a one-dimensional search. Once $\widehat{\beta}$ is found,
$\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\alpha}(\widehat{\beta})$. This is the standard
profile-likelihood device: eliminate the parameter that can be eliminated, and
search over what is left.

\subhead{Fisher information}
The calculation is short once one notices that
\[U = \log\left(1+\beta X\right) \sim \text{EXP}(\alpha),\]
since $P(X>x) = (1+\beta x)^{-\alpha}$ gives $P(U>t)=e^{-\alpha t}$. Writing
$V=1+\beta X=e^{U}$, we get $E\left(V^{-1}\right)=\alpha/(\alpha+1)$ and
$E\left(V^{-2}\right)=\alpha/(\alpha+2)$, whence
\[E\left(\frac{X}{1+\beta X}\right) = \frac{1}{\beta}\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}\right) = \frac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)},\]
\[E\left(\frac{X^{2}}{\left(1+\beta X\right)^{2}}\right)
= \frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\left(1 - \frac{2\alpha}{\alpha+1} + \frac{\alpha}{\alpha+2}\right)
= \frac{2}{\beta^{2}(\alpha+1)(\alpha+2)} .\]
Taking second derivatives and expectations,
\[J(\theta) = n\begin{pmatrix}
\dfrac{1}{\alpha^{2}} & \dfrac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}\\[8pt]
\dfrac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)} & \dfrac{\alpha}{\beta^{2}(\alpha+2)}
\end{pmatrix}.\]
\end{solution}

\section{The Information Inequality}
\label{sec:crlb}

How small can the variance of an unbiased estimator be? For a regular model the
answer is given by the information inequality, also called the Cram\'er--Rao
inequality.

\begin{thm}[Information inequality]
\label{thm:crlb}
Let $T(X)$ be an unbiased estimator of $\tau(\theta)$ in a regular model with
scalar parameter $\theta$, and suppose $E_\theta(T^2)<\infty$ and that
$\frac{d}{d\theta}\int T(x)f_\theta(x)\,dx = \int T(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}f_\theta(x)\,dx$.
Then
\[\var_\theta(T)\ge\frac{\left[\tau'(\theta)\right]^2}{J(\theta)} .\]
Equality holds for all $\theta$ if and only if $f_\theta$ is an exponential family
with natural sufficient statistic a linear function of $T$, that is,
$T = a(\theta)S(\theta; X) + \tau(\theta)$ for some $a(\theta)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate $\int T(x)f_\theta(x)\,dx = \tau(\theta)$ under the integral sign:
\[\tau'(\theta) = \int T(x)\frac{\partial f_\theta(x)}{\partial\theta}\,dx
= \int T(x)\,S(\theta; x)\,f_\theta(x)\,dx = E_\theta\left[T\,S(\theta; X)\right].\]
Since $E_\theta(S) = 0$ (theorem~\ref{thm:scoreproperties}), this is
$\cov_\theta(T, S) = \tau'(\theta)$. By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cauchyschwarz}
\left[\cov(T, S)\right]^2\le\var(T)\var(S),
\end{equation}
that is, $\left[\tau'(\theta)\right]^2\le\var_\theta(T)J(\theta)$, which is the
inequality.

Equality in \eqref{eq:cauchyschwarz} holds if and only if $T$ and $S$ are linearly
related with probability one: $S(\theta; x) = A_1(\theta)T(x) + A_2(\theta)$.
Integrating with respect to $\theta$,
\[\ln f_\theta(x) = C_1(\theta)T(x) + C_2(\theta) + C_3(x),\]
where the ``constant'' of integration $C_3$ may depend on $x$. Hence
\[f_\theta(x) = e^{C_2(\theta)}\exp\left\{C_1(\theta)T(x)\right\}e^{C_3(x)},\]
an exponential family with natural sufficient statistic $T$. Conversely, in such a
family the score is linear in $T$, and equality holds.
\end{proof}

\begin{defn}
\label{def:efficient}
The number $\frac{\left[\tau'(\theta)\right]^2}{J(\theta)}$ is the
\emph{Cram\'er--Rao lower bound} (CRLB) for unbiased estimators of $\tau(\theta)$. An
unbiased estimator whose variance equals the bound is \emph{efficient}. The
\emph{efficiency} of an unbiased estimator $T$ is
$\frac{\text{CRLB}}{\var_\theta(T)}\le 1$.
\end{defn}

For $\tau(\theta) = \theta$ the bound is $\frac{1}{J(\theta)}$. The theorem says that the
bound can be attained only for one function $\tau$ (up to linear transformations)
and only in an exponential family. An estimator can be the UMVUE without attaining
it, as example~\ref{ex:powercrlb} shows.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissoncrlb}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{POI}(\theta)$. Show that the
UMVUE of $\theta$ attains the CRLB.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$T = \sum X_i$ is complete and sufficient (example~\ref{ex:poissonexpfamily}) and
$E(\bar X) = \theta$, so $\bar X$ is the UMVUE. From example~\ref{ex:poissonmle},
$J(\theta) = \frac n\theta$, so the CRLB is $\frac\theta n = \var(\bar X)$. Alternatively,
the Poisson is an exponential family with natural sufficient statistic $\sum X_i$, of
which $\bar X$ is a linear function, so equality holds by theorem~\ref{thm:crlb}.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:powercrlb}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ ($n\ge 3$) be a random sample from $f_\theta(x) = \theta x^{\theta - 1}$,
$0<x<1$, $\theta>0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Show that the UMVUE of $\theta$ does not attain the CRLB, and find its
efficiency.
\item[(b)] Find the CRLB for unbiased estimators of $\tau(\theta) = \theta(1 - \theta)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $f_\theta(x) = \theta\exp\{\theta\ln x\}\cdot\frac1x$ is a REF with
$\eta = \theta>0$ and natural statistic $\ln x$. So $\sum\ln X_i$, or equivalently
$T = -\sum\ln X_i$, is complete and sufficient. Since
$P(-\ln X>y) = P(X<e^{-y}) = e^{-\theta y}$, each $-\ln X_i$ is exponential with mean
$\frac1\theta$, and $T\sim\text{GAM}\left(n, \frac1\theta\right)$. By
problem~\ref{prob:gammamoments},
\[E(T^{-1}) = \frac{\theta\,\Gamma(n - 1)}{\Gamma(n)} = \frac{\theta}{n - 1},\qquad
E(T^{-2}) = \frac{\theta^2\Gamma(n - 2)}{\Gamma(n)} = \frac{\theta^2}{(n - 1)(n - 2)} .\]
So $\frac{n - 1}{T}$ is unbiased, and being a function of $T$ it is the UMVUE. Its
variance is
\[(n - 1)^2\left[\frac{\theta^2}{(n - 1)(n - 2)} - \frac{\theta^2}{(n - 1)^2}\right]
= \theta^2\left[\frac{n - 1}{n - 2} - 1\right] = \frac{\theta^2}{n - 2} .\]
For one observation $\mathcal{L}_1 = \ln\theta + (\theta - 1)\ln x$, $I_1 = \frac{1}{\theta^2}$,
so $J(\theta) = \frac{n}{\theta^2}$ and the CRLB is $\frac{\theta^2}{n}<\frac{\theta^2}{n - 2}$.
The bound is not attained, as theorem~\ref{thm:crlb} predicts, since $\frac{n - 1}{T}$
is not a linear function of $T$. The efficiency is
\[\frac{\theta^2/n}{\theta^2/(n - 2)} = \frac{n - 2}{n}\longrightarrow 1,\]
so the UMVUE is asymptotically efficient.
\item[(b)] $\tau'(\theta) = 1 - 2\theta$, so the CRLB is
$\frac{(1 - 2\theta)^2}{n/\theta^2} = \frac{\theta^2(1 - 2\theta)^2}{n}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:exponentialcrlb}
For a random sample from the exponential distribution with mean $\theta$, which of
$\theta$ and $\frac1\theta$ has an efficient unbiased estimator?
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
By example~\ref{ex:exponentialinfo}, $J(\theta) = \frac{n}{\theta^2}$. For
$\tau(\theta) = \theta$ the CRLB is $\frac{\theta^2}{n} = \var(\bar X)$, so $\bar X$ is
efficient; it is a linear function of the natural sufficient statistic $\sum X_i$.
For $\tau(\theta) = \frac1\theta$, $\tau'(\theta) = -\frac{1}{\theta^2}$ and the CRLB is
$\frac{1}{n\theta^2}$. The UMVUE is $\frac{n - 1}{T}$ (example~\ref{ex:exponentialumvue}),
and by the calculation of example~\ref{ex:powercrlb} with $\theta$ replaced by
$\frac1\theta$ its variance is $\frac{1}{(n - 2)\theta^2}$, larger than the bound. Only
$\theta$ (and linear functions of it) can be estimated efficiently.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
For each of the following, determine whether the variance of the $UMVUE$ of $\theta$ based on a random sample $X_1, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ achieve the Cramer Rao Lower bound and find the efficiency of the $UMVUE$. For each case find the $CRLB$ for variances of unbiased estimators of $\theta^2$.
\begin{multicols}{4}
\begin{itemize}
\item[(a)] $N(\theta,\mu)$
\item[(b)] $BIN(1,\theta)$
\item[(c)] $N(0,\theta^2)$
\item[(d)] $N(0,\theta)$
\end{itemize}
\end{multicols}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Throughout, $T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i^{2}$ where it appears, and the bound for a
target $\tau(\theta)$ is $\left[\tau'(\theta)\right]^{2}/I_n(\theta)$.

\subhead{(a) $N(\theta,\mu)$, variance $\mu$ known}
$I_n(\theta)=n/\mu$; the UMVUE is $\overline{X}$ with variance $\mu/n$. The
bound is $\mu/n$, so it \emph{is} attained and the efficiency is $1$. For
$\tau=\theta^{2}$ the bound is $\left(2\theta\right)^{2}\mu/n = 4\theta^{2}\mu/n$.

\subhead{(b) BIN$(1,\theta)$}
$I_n(\theta)=n/\left[\theta(1-\theta)\right]$; the UMVUE is $\overline{X}$ with
variance $\theta(1-\theta)/n$, equal to the bound. Efficiency $1$. For
$\tau=\theta^{2}$ the bound is $4\theta^{2}\cdot\theta(1-\theta)/n
= 4\theta^{3}(1-\theta)/n$.

\subhead{(c) $N\left(0,\theta^{2}\right)$}
Here $\theta$ is the standard deviation. Per observation
$-E\left[\partial^{2}\log f/\partial\theta^{2}\right] = 2/\theta^{2}$, so
$I_n(\theta)=2n/\theta^{2}$ and the bound for $\theta$ is $\theta^{2}/(2n)$.
The UMVUE is $c_n\sqrt{T}$ with
$c_n = \Gamma(n/2)\big/\left[\sqrt2\,\Gamma\left((n+1)/2\right)\right]$, and its
variance is $\theta^{2}\left(nc_n^{2}-1\right)$, which is strictly larger. The
bound is \emph{not} attained; the efficiency
$1\big/\left[2n\left(nc_n^{2}-1\right)\right]$ equals $0.915$ at $n=2$, $0.977$
at $n=10$ and $0.998$ at $n=100$ --- so it rises to one only in the limit.
For $\tau=\theta^{2}$ the bound is $\left(2\theta\right)^{2}\theta^{2}/(2n)
= 2\theta^{4}/n$, and this one \emph{is} attained, by $T/n$.

\subhead{(d) $N(0,\theta)$}
Now $\theta$ is the variance. Per observation the information is
$1/\left(2\theta^{2}\right)$, so $I_n(\theta)=n/\left(2\theta^{2}\right)$ and the
bound for $\theta$ is $2\theta^{2}/n$. The UMVUE is $T/n$ with variance
$2\theta^{2}/n$: attained, efficiency $1$. For $\tau=\theta^{2}$ the bound is
$4\theta^{2}\cdot 2\theta^{2}/n = 8\theta^{4}/n$.

\begin{remark}
Parts (c) and (d) are the same model written with different parameters, and they
answer the question differently. The bound is attained exactly when the score is
an affine function of the estimator, which for an exponential family happens for
the mean of the natural sufficient statistic and for nothing else. That
statistic is $\sum X_i^{2}$, whose mean is $n\theta^{2}$ in (c) and $n\theta$ in
(d) --- so the bound is attained for $\theta^{2}$ in (c) and for $\theta$ in
(d). Attainability is a property of the parameterisation, not of the model.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

The inequality extends to vector parameters.

\begin{thm}[Information inequality, vector parameter]
\label{thm:crlbvector}
Let $\theta = (\theta_1, \dots, \theta_k)^{\mathsf T}$, let $T(X)$ be an unbiased
estimator of $\tau(\theta)$ in a regular model with positive definite Fisher
information $J(\theta)$, and let
$D(\theta) = \left(\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial\theta_1}, \dots, \frac{\partial\tau}{\partial\theta_k}\right)^{\mathsf T}$.
Then for all $\theta\in\Omega$
\[\var_\theta(T)\ge D(\theta)^{\mathsf T}J(\theta)^{-1}D(\theta) .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Let $S(\theta,X)$ be the score vector, so that $E_{\theta}\left[S\right]=0$ and
$\var_{\theta}(S)=J(\theta)$. Differentiating the unbiasedness identity
$E_{\theta}\left[T(X)\right]=\tau(\theta)$ with respect to $\theta_j$ and
interchanging derivative and integral, which regularity permits,
\[\frac{\partial \tau}{\partial\theta_j}
= \int T(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_j} f_{\theta}(x)\,dx
= \int T(x)\, S_j(\theta,x)\, f_{\theta}(x)\,dx
= \cov_{\theta}\!\left(T, S_j\right),\]
the last equality because $E_{\theta}\left[S_j\right]=0$. Collecting the $k$
components, $\cov_{\theta}(T,S) = D(\theta)$.

Now take any fixed vector $c\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ and apply the Cauchy--Schwarz
inequality to the scalar random variables $T$ and $c^{t}S$:
\[\left[\cov_{\theta}\!\left(T,\, c^{t}S\right)\right]^{2}
\;\leq\; \var_{\theta}(T)\ \var_{\theta}\!\left(c^{t}S\right),\]
that is
\[\left(c^{t}D\right)^{2} \;\leq\; \var_{\theta}(T)\ c^{t}J(\theta)\,c .\]
Choose $c = J^{-1}(\theta)\,D(\theta)$, which is legitimate because $J(\theta)$
is positive definite in a regular model. Then $c^{t}D = D^{t}J^{-1}D$ and
$c^{t}Jc = D^{t}J^{-1}JJ^{-1}D = D^{t}J^{-1}D$, so the inequality reads
\[\left(D^{t}J^{-1}D\right)^{2} \;\leq\; \var_{\theta}(T)\ D^{t}J^{-1}D .\]
Since $J^{-1}$ is positive definite, $D^{t}J^{-1}D>0$ unless $D=0$, and
dividing through gives
\[\var_{\theta}(T) \;\geq\; D(\theta)^{t}\,J^{-1}(\theta)\,D(\theta).\]
If $D=0$ the inequality is trivial.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The scalar case is $k=1$, where $D=\tau'(\theta)$ and the bound collapses to
$\left[\tau'(\theta)\right]^{2}/J(\theta)$. Equality holds exactly when $T$ is
an affine function of the score, which is why the bound is attained in
exponential families and generally is not attained outside them.
\end{remark}

\section{Limiting Distributions (Review)}
\label{sec:limits}
\begin{defn}[convergence in probability]
A sequence of random variables $X_1, X_2, \dots$ converges in probability to a constant $C$ if for each $\varepsilon > 0$
\[\lim_{n\rightarrow \infty} P\left(\left|X_n - C\right| > \varepsilon\right) = 0\]
we write $X_n \underset{P}{\longrightarrow} C$.
\end{defn}

\begin{thm}[Chebyshev's Inequality]
 Suppose $X$ is a random variable with $E(X) = \mu$ and $\var (X) = \sigma^2 < \infty$, then for any $K > 0$
 \[P\left(\left|X - \mu\right| \geq K\right)  \leq \frac{\sigma^2}{K^2}.\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Let $A = \left\{\,\left|X-\mu\right| \geq K \,\right\}$ and let $I_A$ be its
indicator. On $A$ we have $(X-\mu)^2 \geq K^2$, and off $A$ the quantity
$K^2 I_A$ is zero while $(X-\mu)^2$ is not negative. Hence pointwise
\[(X-\mu)^2 \;\geq\; K^2 I_A .\]
Taking expectations preserves the inequality, so
\[\sigma^2 = E\left[(X-\mu)^2\right] \;\geq\; K^2\, E\left[I_A\right]
= K^2\, P\left(\left|X-\mu\right| \geq K\right),\]
and dividing by $K^2>0$ gives the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The inequality cannot be strengthened to a strict inequality: for the random
variable taking the values $\mu \pm K$ each with probability
$\sigma^2/(2K^2)$ and the value $\mu$ otherwise, both sides are equal. Nor does
it need any assumption beyond a finite variance, which is exactly why it is
weak --- and why the bound is useful for proving limits rather than for
computing probabilities.
\end{remark}

\begin{thm}[Weak Law of Large Numbers]
Suppose $X_1, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ is a random sample from a distribution with $E(X_i)   = \mu$ and $\var (X_i) = \sigma^2 < \infty$ then  \[\overline{X_n} = \frac{1}{n}\sum^n_{i = 1} X_i \underset{P}{\longrightarrow} \mu.\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Because the $X_i$ are independent with common mean $\mu$ and common variance
$\sigma^2$,
\[E\left(\overline{X_n}\right) = \mu ,\qquad
\var\left(\overline{X_n}\right) = \frac{1}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\var(X_i)
= \frac{\sigma^2}{n}.\]
Apply Chebyshev's inequality to $\overline{X_n}$: for any $\varepsilon>0$,
\[P\left(\left|\overline{X_n}-\mu\right| \geq \varepsilon\right)
\;\leq\; \frac{\var\left(\overline{X_n}\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}}
= \frac{\sigma^{2}}{n\,\varepsilon^{2}} \;\longrightarrow\; 0
\qquad\text{as } n\rightarrow\infty ,\]
which is the definition of $\overline{X_n}\underset{P}{\longrightarrow}\mu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}
Independence is used only to add the variances; it is not needed in full. The
same argument works whenever the $X_i$ are uncorrelated, and a version survives
even when $\var(X_i)$ is infinite, though Chebyshev's inequality is then no
longer the tool.
\end{note}

\begin{defn}[Convergence in distribution]
Let $X_n$ be a sequence of random variables. We say $X_n$ converges in distribution to a random variable $X$ if \[\lim_{n\rightarrow \infty}P\left(X_n \leq x\right) = P(X \leq x)\] for all values of $x$ at which the right hand side $\, F(x) = P(X\leq x)$ is continuous. We write $X_n \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} X$.
\end{defn}

\begin{thm}[Central Limit Theorem]
Suppose $X_1, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ is a  random sample from a distribution with  $E(X_i) = \mu$ and $\var (X_i) = \sigma^2 < \infty$. Then 
\[Y_n  = \frac{\sum^n_{i = 1} X_i - n\mu}{\sqrt{n\, \sigma^2}} = \frac{\sqrt{n}\left(\overline{X} - \mu\right)}{\sigma} \, \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} Z \sim N(0,1).\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Assume the moment generating function $m(t)=E\left(e^{tX}\right)$ exists in a
neighbourhood of the origin; this is more than the theorem needs but it makes
the argument elementary. Put
\[W_i = \frac{X_i-\mu}{\sigma},\qquad\text{so}\qquad
E(W_i)=0,\quad \var(W_i)=1,\quad Y_n = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_i .\]
Let $m_W(t)=E\left(e^{tW}\right)$. Then $m_W(0)=1$, $m_W'(0)=E(W)=0$ and
$m_W''(0)=E(W^2)=1$, so the second-order Taylor expansion about the origin is
\[m_W(t) = 1 + \frac{t^2}{2} + o\left(t^2\right)\qquad\text{as } t\rightarrow 0 .\]
By independence the moment generating function of $Y_n$ factorises:
\[m_{Y_n}(t) = \left[m_W\!\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right]^{n}
= \left[1 + \frac{t^{2}}{2n} + o\!\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\right]^{n}.\]
Taking logarithms and using $\log(1+u) = u + O(u^{2})$,
\[\log m_{Y_n}(t) = n\left[\frac{t^{2}}{2n} + o\!\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\right]
\;\longrightarrow\; \frac{t^{2}}{2},\]
so $m_{Y_n}(t) \rightarrow e^{t^{2}/2}$ for each fixed $t$. That limit is the
moment generating function of the standard normal distribution, and convergence
of moment generating functions on a neighbourhood of the origin implies
convergence in distribution. Hence $Y_n \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} Z\sim N(0,1)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The theorem is true without any moment generating function --- finite variance
alone suffices --- but the general proof replaces $m$ by the characteristic
function $\varphi(t)=E\left(e^{itX}\right)$, which always exists, and appeals to
L\'evy's continuity theorem in place of the last step. The structure of the
argument is identical.

Note also what is \emph{not} claimed. $Y_n$ does not converge in probability to
anything: its distribution settles down, its value does not. This is the
distinction the previous section drew, and the central limit theorem is the
clearest place where it matters.
\end{remark}

\begin{thm}[Limit Theorems]
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $X_n \underset{P}{\longrightarrow} a$ where $a$ is a constant and $g$ is a real-valued function which is continuous at $a$, then \[g(X_n) \underset{P}{\longrightarrow} g(a).\]

\item If $X_n \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} X$ and $g$ is a real-valued continuous function, then \[g(X_n) \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} g(X).\]

\item If $X_n \underset{P}{\longrightarrow} X$ then $X_n \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} X$.

\item (Slutsky) If $X_n \underset{P}{\longrightarrow} a$ and $Y_n \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} Y$, then 
   \begin{enumerate}
        \item[(a)] $X_n + Y_n \, \underset{D}{\longrightarrow}\, a + Y$
        \item[(b)] $X_nY_n \, \underset{D}{\longrightarrow}\, aY$
        \item[(c)] $\frac{Y_n}{X_n} \, \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} \, \frac{Y}{a};\  a\neq 0$
   \end{enumerate}
   
\item Suppose $a$ and $b>0$ are constants and \[n^b\left(X_n - a\right) \, \underset{D}{\longrightarrow}\, X.\] Let $g$ be a real-valued function that is differentiable and whose derivative $g'$ is continuous at $a$, then  \[n^b\, \left[g(X_n) - g(a)\right] \, \underset{D}{\longrightarrow}\, g'(a)\, X.\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
\subhead{(1)}
Let $\varepsilon>0$. Continuity of $g$ at $a$ gives a $\delta>0$ with
$\left|g(x)-g(a)\right|<\varepsilon$ whenever $\left|x-a\right|<\delta$.
Contrapositively, $\left|g(X_n)-g(a)\right|\geq\varepsilon$ forces
$\left|X_n-a\right|\geq\delta$, so
\[P\left(\left|g(X_n)-g(a)\right|\geq\varepsilon\right)
\;\leq\; P\left(\left|X_n-a\right|\geq\delta\right)\;\longrightarrow\;0 .\]

\subhead{(3)}
Let $x$ be a continuity point of $F$, the distribution function of $X$, and let
$\varepsilon>0$. Splitting on whether $\left|X_n-X\right|$ is small,
\[P(X_n\leq x) \leq P(X\leq x+\varepsilon) + P\left(\left|X_n-X\right|>\varepsilon\right),\]
\[P(X\leq x-\varepsilon) \leq P(X_n\leq x) + P\left(\left|X_n-X\right|>\varepsilon\right).\]
Letting $n\rightarrow\infty$ kills the last term in each line, so
\[F(x-\varepsilon) \;\leq\; \liminf_n P(X_n\leq x) \;\leq\; \limsup_n P(X_n\leq x)
\;\leq\; F(x+\varepsilon).\]
Now let $\varepsilon\downarrow 0$; continuity of $F$ at $x$ closes the two outer
bounds onto $F(x)$, so $P(X_n\leq x)\rightarrow F(x)$.

\subhead{(5), the delta method}
Write $Z_n = n^{b}\left(X_n-a\right)$, so $Z_n \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} X$
by hypothesis. Since $b>0$, $X_n - a = n^{-b}Z_n \underset{P}{\longrightarrow} 0$.
By the mean value theorem there is a point $\xi_n$ between $a$ and $X_n$ with
\[n^{b}\left[g(X_n)-g(a)\right] = g'(\xi_n)\, n^{b}\left(X_n-a\right) = g'(\xi_n)\, Z_n .\]
Because $\xi_n$ lies between $a$ and $X_n$ we have
$\xi_n \underset{P}{\longrightarrow} a$, and $g'$ is continuous at $a$, so part
(1) gives $g'(\xi_n)\underset{P}{\longrightarrow} g'(a)$. Slutsky's theorem,
part 4(b), applied to the product then yields
\[n^{b}\left[g(X_n)-g(a)\right] \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} g'(a)\,X .\]

\subhead{(2) and (4)}
Both are instances of the continuous mapping theorem, which rests on the
portmanteau characterisation --- that $X_n\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}X$ if and
only if $E\left[h(X_n)\right]\rightarrow E\left[h(X)\right]$ for every bounded
continuous $h$. Given that, (2) is immediate: if $h$ is bounded and continuous
then so is $h\circ g$. Part (4) follows by applying the same statement to the
pair $(X_n,Y_n)$, which converges jointly in distribution to $(a,Y)$ precisely
because the first coordinate has a constant limit; the maps
$(x,y)\mapsto x+y$, $xy$ and $y/x$ are continuous at every point $(a,y)$, the
last provided $a\neq 0$. The portmanteau theorem itself is a result in measure
theory and is taken as known here.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}
Part (4) is the workhorse. Almost every asymptotic argument later in this course
has the shape ``something converging in distribution, multiplied or divided by
something converging in probability to a constant'', and Slutsky's theorem is
what licenses treating the second as though it already were that constant. The
requirement that the limit be a \emph{constant} is not decorative: for two
sequences each converging in distribution the conclusion is false in general,
because their joint behaviour is not determined by their marginals.
\end{note}

\section{Large Sample Properties of ML Estimators}
\label{sec:largesample}

Exact sampling distributions of ML estimators are rarely available. What can
almost always be said is how they behave as the sample size grows, and this
behaviour is the main justification for maximum likelihood.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:consistent}
Let $T_n$ be an estimator computed from $X_1, \dots, X_n$. The sequence $(T_n)$ is a
\emph{consistent} sequence of estimators of $\tau(\theta)$ if
$T_n\underset{P}{\longrightarrow}\tau(\theta)$ for every $\theta\in\Omega$.
\end{defn}

By Chebyshev's inequality, $T_n$ is consistent whenever its mean square error
tends to zero: $P(|T_n - \tau|\ge\varepsilon)\le\frac{\MSE(T_n)}{\varepsilon^2}$, a
bound proved exactly as Chebyshev's inequality above. For example
$X_{(n)}$ is consistent for $\theta$ in a $\text{UNIF}(0, \theta)$ sample, since its mean
square error $\frac{2\theta^2}{(n + 1)(n + 2)}$ tends to zero
(example~\ref{ex:uniformmse}).

\subsection{Consistency and Asymptotic Normality}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:mleconsistent}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from a regular, identifiable model
$\{f_\theta(x) : \theta\in\Omega\}$ with $\Omega$ an open interval, and let $\theta_0$ be
the true parameter value. Write $S_1(\theta; x) = \frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\ln f_\theta(x)$.
Then with probability tending to one as $n\to\infty$, the likelihood equation
$\sum_{i=1}^n S_1(\theta; X_i) = 0$ has a root $\hat\theta_n$ with
$\hat\theta_n\underset{P}{\longrightarrow}\theta_0$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\varepsilon>0$ small enough that $[\theta_0-\varepsilon,\theta_0+\varepsilon]\subset\Omega$, and write
\[\ell_n(\theta) = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log f_{\theta}(X_i).\]
For each fixed $\theta$ the summands are independent and identically
distributed, so by the weak law of large numbers
\[\ell_n(\theta) - \ell_n(\theta_0) \underset{P}{\longrightarrow}
E_{\theta_0}\left[\log \frac{f_{\theta}(X)}{f_{\theta_0}(X)}\right] = -K(\theta_0,\theta),\]
where $K(\theta_0,\theta)$ is the Kullback--Leibler divergence. By Jensen's
inequality applied to the strictly concave logarithm,
\[E_{\theta_0}\left[\log \frac{f_{\theta}(X)}{f_{\theta_0}(X)}\right]
\;\leq\; \log E_{\theta_0}\left[\frac{f_{\theta}(X)}{f_{\theta_0}(X)}\right]
= \log \int f_{\theta}(x)\,dx = 0,\]
with equality only when $f_{\theta} = f_{\theta_0}$ almost everywhere. Since the
model is identifiable, $K(\theta_0,\theta) > 0$ for every $\theta \neq \theta_0$.

Apply this at the two endpoints $\theta_0 \pm \varepsilon$. With probability
tending to one,
\[\ell_n(\theta_0) > \ell_n(\theta_0-\varepsilon)
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\ell_n(\theta_0) > \ell_n(\theta_0+\varepsilon),\]
so $\ell_n$ attains a local maximum at some interior point
$\widehat{\theta_n}\in(\theta_0-\varepsilon,\theta_0+\varepsilon)$. At an
interior maximum of a differentiable function the derivative vanishes, so
$\widehat{\theta_n}$ solves the likelihood equation
$\sum_{i=1}^{n}S_1(\theta,X_i)=0$. As $\varepsilon>0$ was arbitrary,
\[P\left(\left|\widehat{\theta_n}-\theta_0\right|<\varepsilon\right)\longrightarrow 1
\qquad\text{for every } \varepsilon>0,\]
which is exactly $\widehat{\theta_n}\underset{P}{\longrightarrow}\theta_0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The theorem asserts that \emph{some} root of the likelihood equation is
consistent, not that every root is, and not that the global maximiser is. When
the likelihood equation has a unique root the distinction disappears, which is
the case in most models met here; when it does not, the consistent root is the
one to identify.
\end{remark}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:mlenormal}
Under the conditions of theorem~\ref{thm:mleconsistent}, suppose further that
$\frac{\partial^3}{\partial\theta^3}\ln f_\theta(x)$ exists and is bounded near $\theta_0$
by a function with finite mean, and that $0<J_1(\theta_0)<\infty$, where
$J_1(\theta) = E_\theta\left[-\frac{\partial^2}{\partial\theta^2}\ln f_\theta(X)\right]$ is the
Fisher information in one observation. Then the consistent root $\hat\theta_n$
satisfies
\[\sqrt n\left(\hat\theta_n - \theta_0\right)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}N\left(0, \frac{1}{J_1(\theta_0)}\right),
\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad
\sqrt{J(\theta_0)}\left(\hat\theta_n - \theta_0\right)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}N(0, 1),\]
where $J(\theta) = nJ_1(\theta)$. If $\tau$ is differentiable with $\tau'(\theta_0)\ne 0$,
then
\[\sqrt n\left(\tau(\hat\theta_n) - \tau(\theta_0)\right)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}N\left(0, \frac{\left[\tau'(\theta_0)\right]^2}{J_1(\theta_0)}\right).\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
Expand the score about $\theta_0$. Since $S(\hat\theta_n) = 0$ and $S' = -I$, Taylor's
theorem gives
\[0 = S(\hat\theta_n) = S(\theta_0) - (\hat\theta_n - \theta_0)I(\theta_n^*)\]
for some $\theta_n^*$ between $\theta_0$ and $\hat\theta_n$. Rearranging,
\[\sqrt n\left(\hat\theta_n - \theta_0\right) = \frac{S(\theta_0)/\sqrt n}{I(\theta_n^*)/n} .\]
The numerator is $\frac{1}{\sqrt n}\sum S_1(\theta_0; X_i)$, a normalised sum of
independent terms with mean $0$ and variance $J_1(\theta_0)$
(theorem~\ref{thm:scoreproperties}); by the central limit theorem it converges in
distribution to $N(0, J_1(\theta_0))$. In the denominator,
$\frac1n I(\theta_0) = \frac1n\sum I_1(\theta_0; X_i)\underset{P}{\longrightarrow}J_1(\theta_0)$ by
the weak law of large numbers, and the bound on the third derivative together with
$\theta_n^*\underset{P}{\longrightarrow}\theta_0$ shows that replacing $\theta_0$ by $\theta_n^*$
changes the limit by a term tending to zero. By Slutsky's theorem the ratio
converges to $\frac{1}{J_1(\theta_0)}N(0, J_1(\theta_0)) = N\left(0, \frac{1}{J_1(\theta_0)}\right)$.
The statement for $\tau(\hat\theta_n)$ then follows from the delta method, part (5)
of the limit theorems with $b = \frac12$.
\end{proof}

The theorem says that, for large $n$, the ML estimator is approximately unbiased,
approximately normal, and has variance approximately $\frac{1}{J(\theta_0)}$, the
Cram\'er--Rao bound. In this sense it is \emph{asymptotically efficient}: no
regular unbiased estimator can do better. The uniform and shifted exponential
models are not regular, and there the ML estimator converges at the faster rate
$\frac1n$ (example~\ref{ex:shiftedexp}).

\subsection{Pivotal Quantities and Confidence Intervals}

\begin{defn}
\label{def:pivot}
A \emph{pivotal quantity} is a function $Q(X, \theta)$ of the data and the parameter
whose distribution does not depend on $\theta$ or on any other unknown parameter. An
\emph{asymptotic pivotal quantity} is one whose distribution converges, as
$n\to\infty$, to a distribution free of unknown parameters.
\end{defn}

For a random sample from $N(\theta, \sigma^2)$ with $\sigma^2$ known,
$Q = \frac{\sqrt n(\bar X - \theta)}{\sigma}\sim N(0, 1)$ is pivotal. For a random sample
from any distribution with mean $\theta$ and known variance $\sigma^2$, the same $Q$ is
asymptotically pivotal by the central limit theorem.
