A pivot gives a confidence interval. Choose constants $c_1<c_2$ with
$P\left(c_1<Q(X, \theta)<c_2\right) = 1 - \alpha$. If $Q$ is monotone in $\theta$, the
event $c_1<Q(X, \theta)<c_2$ can be rewritten as $C_1(X)<\theta<C_2(X)$, and then
$\left[C_1(X), C_2(X)\right]$ is a $100(1 - \alpha)\%$ confidence interval for $\theta$.

When no exact pivot is available, theorem~\ref{thm:mlenormal} supplies an
asymptotic one. Since $J(\hat\theta_n)/J(\theta_0)\underset{P}{\longrightarrow}1$, Slutsky's
theorem allows the unknown information to be estimated, and both
\[\sqrt{J(\hat\theta_n)}\left(\hat\theta_n - \theta\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\sqrt{I(\hat\theta_n)}\left(\hat\theta_n - \theta\right)\]
converge in distribution to $N(0, 1)$. The resulting approximate $95\%$ interval is
\[\hat\theta_n\pm\frac{1.96}{\sqrt{J(\hat\theta_n)}} .\]

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:powerci}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $f_\theta(x) = \theta x^{\theta - 1}$,
$0<x<1$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Find the ML estimator of $\theta$.
\item[(b)] Find an approximate $95\%$ confidence interval for $\theta$ from the
asymptotic distribution of $\hat\theta_n$.
\item[(c)] Find an exact $95\%$ confidence interval for $\theta$.
\item[(d)] Compute both for $n = 10$ and $-\sum\ln x_i = 4.0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $\mathcal{L}(\theta) = n\ln\theta + (\theta - 1)\sum\ln x_i$, so
$S(\theta) = \frac n\theta + \sum\ln x_i$ and $\hat\theta_n = \frac nT$ with
$T = -\sum\ln X_i$.
\item[(b)] $I(\theta) = \frac{n}{\theta^2} = J(\theta)$, so the approximate interval is
\[\hat\theta_n\pm 1.96\frac{\hat\theta_n}{\sqrt n} = \frac nT\pm 1.96\frac{\sqrt n}{T} .\]
\item[(c)] As in example~\ref{ex:powercrlb}, $T\sim\text{GAM}\left(n, \frac1\theta\right)$, so
$Y = 2\theta T\sim\text{GAM}(n, 2) = \chi^2(2n)$ is an exact pivot. Let $a$ and $b$ be
the lower and upper $2.5\%$ points of $\chi^2(2n)$, so that $P(a<Y<b) = 0.95$, as in
figure~\ref{fig:chisqtails}. Then
\[0.95 = P(a<2\theta T<b) = P\left(\frac{a}{2T}<\theta<\frac{b}{2T}\right),\]
and $\left[\frac{a}{2T}, \frac{b}{2T}\right]$ is an exact $95\%$ confidence interval.
\item[(d)] $\hat\theta = \frac{10}{4} = 2.5$. The approximate interval is
$2.5\pm 1.96\cdot\frac{2.5}{\sqrt{10}} = 2.5\pm 1.550$, that is $(0.95, 4.05)$. For
$\chi^2(20)$, $a = 9.591$ and $b = 34.170$, so the exact interval is
$\left(\frac{9.591}{8}, \frac{34.170}{8}\right) = (1.20, 4.27)$. The exact interval is
not symmetric about $\hat\theta$; it reflects the skewness of the sampling
distribution, which the normal approximation ignores at this sample size.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[=>stealth]
\draw[-Stealth](0,0)--(7.5,0)node[right]{$y$};
\draw[-Stealth](0,0)--(0,3)node[above]{$g(y)$};
\draw[smooth,domain=0:14,scale=0.5,variable=\x]plot(\x,{8*\x*\x*exp(-1/2*\x)/4});
\draw(5,0)--(5,0.68);
\draw(0.7,0)--(0.7,0.95);
\path[fill=teal,opacity=0.25, smooth, domain=10:14,scale=0.5,variable=\x]plot(\x,{8*\x*\x*exp(-1/2*\x)/4})--(14,0.18)--(14,0)--(10,0)--(10,0.68)--cycle;
\path[fill=orange,opacity=0.25, smooth, domain=0:1.4,scale=0.5,variable=\x]plot(\x,{8*\x*\x*exp(-1/2*\x)/4})--(1.4,0.95)--(1.4,0)--(0,0)--cycle;
\draw[-Stealth](6.5,0.8)--(6,0.8)--(6,0.2);
\draw[-Stealth](0.3,0.8)--(0.5,0.8)--(0.5,0.3);
\draw(6.5,0.8)node[right]{0.025}
(5,0)node[below]{$b$}
(0.7,0)node[below]{$a$}
(0.3,0.8)node[left]{$0.025$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\label{fig:chisqtails}
\end{figure}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:poissonci}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{POI}(\theta)$. Find approximate
$95\%$ confidence intervals for $\theta$ and for $\tau(\theta) = e^{-\theta}$, and compute
them for $n = 50$ and $\bar x = 2.4$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
By example~\ref{ex:poissonmle}, $\hat\theta_n = \bar X$ and $J(\theta) = \frac n\theta$. The
approximate interval for $\theta$ is
\[\bar x\pm 1.96\sqrt{\frac{\bar x}{n}} .\]
For $\tau(\theta) = e^{-\theta}$, $\tau'(\theta) = -e^{-\theta}$, and by
theorem~\ref{thm:mlenormal} $\tau(\hat\theta_n)$ is approximately normal with mean
$\tau(\theta)$ and variance $\frac{[\tau'(\theta)]^2}{J(\theta)} = \frac{e^{-2\theta}\theta}{n}$.
The interval is
\[e^{-\bar x}\pm 1.96\,e^{-\bar x}\sqrt{\frac{\bar x}{n}} .\]
For $n = 50$ and $\bar x = 2.4$, $\sqrt{\bar x/n} = 0.2191$, so the interval for
$\theta$ is $2.4\pm 0.429 = (1.97, 2.83)$, and the interval for $e^{-\theta}$ is
$0.0907\pm 0.0390 = (0.052, 0.130)$. A slightly better interval for $e^{-\theta}$ is
obtained by transforming the endpoints of the first interval:
$(e^{-2.83}, e^{-1.97}) = (0.059, 0.139)$, which, unlike the delta-method interval,
can never contain negative values.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:shiftedexp}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the shifted exponential distribution
$f_\theta(x) = e^{-(x - \theta)}$, $x>\theta$. Find the ML estimator of $\theta$, show that
it is consistent, find the distribution of $n(\hat\theta_n - \theta)$, and use it to
construct a $95\%$ confidence interval for $\theta$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\[L(\theta) = \prod^n_{i = 1}e^{-(x_i - \theta)}I(x_i>\theta) = e^{n\theta - \sum x_i}I\left(x_{(1)}>\theta\right).\]
This increases in $\theta$ up to $x_{(1)}$ and is zero beyond it
(figure~\ref{fig:shiftedexplik}), so $\hat\theta_n = X_{(1)}$.

Each $X_i$ has $P(X_i>y) = e^{-(y - \theta)}$ for $y>\theta$, so
$P(X_{(1)}>y) = e^{-n(y - \theta)}$. Since $X_{(1)}>\theta$ always, for $\varepsilon>0$
\[P\left(\left|X_{(1)} - \theta\right|>\varepsilon\right) = P\left(X_{(1)}>\theta + \varepsilon\right) = e^{-n\varepsilon}\longrightarrow 0,\]
and $\hat\theta_n$ is consistent. Moreover, for $t>0$,
\[P\left(n(X_{(1)} - \theta)\le t\right) = 1 - P\left(X_{(1)}>\theta + \frac tn\right) = 1 - e^{-t},\]
so $Q = n(X_{(1)} - \theta)\sim\text{EXP}(1)$ exactly, whatever $n$. It is an exact pivot,
and the error of $\hat\theta_n$ is of order $\frac1n$ rather than $\frac{1}{\sqrt n}$: the
model is not regular, and theorem~\ref{thm:mlenormal} does not apply.

For an equal-tailed interval take $a = -\ln 0.975 = 0.0253$ and
$b = -\ln 0.025 = 3.689$, so that $P(a<Q<b) = 0.95$ (figure~\ref{fig:exptails}). Then
\[0.95 = P\left(a<n(X_{(1)} - \theta)<b\right) = P\left(X_{(1)} - \frac bn<\theta<X_{(1)} - \frac an\right),\]
giving the interval $\left[X_{(1)} - \frac{3.689}{n},\ X_{(1)} - \frac{0.0253}{n}\right]$.
Because the density of $Q$ is decreasing, the shortest $95\%$ interval puts all the
excluded probability in the upper tail: $P(0<Q<-\ln 0.05) = 0.95$ gives
$\left[X_{(1)} - \frac{2.996}{n},\ X_{(1)}\right]$, of length $\frac{2.996}{n}$ against
$\frac{3.663}{n}$.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[
    width=9cm, height=5.5cm,
    xlabel={$\theta$}, ylabel={$L(\theta)$},
    xmin=0, xmax=4, ymin=0, ymax=1.15,
    axis lines=left, xtick={2.5}, xticklabels={$x_{(1)}$}, ytick=\empty]
\addplot[very thick, blue, smooth, samples=60, domain=0:2.5] {exp(2*(x-2.5))};
\addplot[very thick, blue, domain=2.5:4] {0};
\addplot[only marks, mark=*] coordinates {(2.5,1)};
\addplot[only marks, mark=*, mark options={fill=white}] coordinates {(2.5,0)};
\draw[dashed, gray] (axis cs:2.5,0) -- (axis cs:2.5,1);
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The likelihood for a shifted exponential sample increases up to the smallest
observation and is zero beyond it.}
\label{fig:shiftedexplik}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[
    width=9cm, height=5.5cm,
    xlabel={$q$}, ylabel={$e^{-q}$},
    xmin=0, xmax=5, ymin=0, ymax=1.1,
    axis lines=left, xtick={0.0253,3.689}, xticklabels={$a$,$b$}, ytick={1}]
\addplot[fill=orange!30, draw=none, domain=0:0.0253] {exp(-x)} \closedcycle;
\addplot[fill=teal!30, draw=none, domain=3.689:5] {exp(-x)} \closedcycle;
\addplot[very thick, blue, smooth, samples=80, domain=0:5] {exp(-x)};
\node[font=\footnotesize, anchor=west] at (axis cs:3.75,0.12) {$0.025$};
\node[font=\footnotesize, anchor=west] at (axis cs:0.15,0.9) {$0.025$ to the left of $a$};

\label{fig:exptails}

\begin{problem}
Suppose $X_1, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ is a random sample from the $UNIF(0,\theta)$ distribution. Show that the $ML$ estimator $\widehat{\theta}_n$ is a consistent estimator of $\theta$ and find the asymptotic distribution of $n\left(\widehat{\theta}_n - \theta\right)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The maximum likelihood estimator is $\widehat{\theta}_n = X_{(n)}$, since the
likelihood $\theta^{-n}I\left(x_{(n)}<\theta\right)$ is decreasing in $\theta$
and so is largest at the smallest admissible value.

\subhead{Consistency}
For $0<\varepsilon<\theta$, and since $X_{(n)}\leq\theta$ always,
\[P\left(\left|X_{(n)}-\theta\right|>\varepsilon\right)
= P\left(X_{(n)}<\theta-\varepsilon\right)
= \left(\frac{\theta-\varepsilon}{\theta}\right)^{n}\longrightarrow 0 ,\]
because the base is strictly between $0$ and $1$. Hence
$\widehat{\theta}_n\underset{P}{\longrightarrow}\theta$.

\subhead{Asymptotic distribution}
For $w>0$,
\[P\left(n\left(\theta-X_{(n)}\right) > w\right)
= P\left(X_{(n)} < \theta - \frac{w}{n}\right)
= \left(1-\frac{w}{n\theta}\right)^{n} \longrightarrow e^{-w/\theta} .\]
So $n\left(\theta-\widehat{\theta}_n\right)$ converges in distribution to an
exponential variable with mean $\theta$, and equivalently
\[n\left(\widehat{\theta}_n - \theta\right) \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} -W,
\qquad W\sim\text{EXP}(\theta).\]

\begin{remark}
Two departures from the standard theory are worth naming. The rate is $n$, not
$\sqrt{n}$, so the estimator converges much faster than usual; and the limit is
exponential rather than normal, and is supported entirely on one side of zero.
Neither contradicts the large-sample theory of this chapter, because that theory
assumes the support of the density does not depend on the parameter --- and here
it does. This is the standard example of a non-regular model.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
The lifetimes of a certain type of electronic equipment follows the $EXP(\theta)$ distribution. Ten components where tested independently with the observed lifetimes, to the nearest days, given by 
\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{cccccccccc}
70 & 11 & 66 & 5 & 20 & 4 & 35 & 40 & 29 & 8\\
\end{tabular}
\end{table}
Find 
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $ML$ estimator of $\theta$ and verify its a maximum.
\item[(b)] Fisher information and evaluate an approximate $95\%$ C.I for $\theta$.
\item[(c)] The exact $95\%$ C.I for $\theta$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The ten observations sum to $\sum x_i = 288$, so $\overline{x}=28.8$.

\subhead{(a)}
For the EXP$(\theta)$ density $f_{\theta}(x)=\theta^{-1}e^{-x/\theta}$,
\[\log L = -n\log\theta - \frac{1}{\theta}\sum_i x_i,\qquad
S(\theta) = -\frac{n}{\theta} + \frac{\sum_i x_i}{\theta^{2}} .\]
Setting $S=0$ gives $\widehat{\theta}=\overline{x}=28.8$ days. It is a maximum
because
\[\left.\frac{\partial^{2}\log L}{\partial\theta^{2}}\right|_{\theta=\overline{x}}
= \frac{n}{\overline{x}^{2}} - \frac{2\sum_i x_i}{\overline{x}^{3}}
= \frac{n}{\overline{x}^{2}} - \frac{2n}{\overline{x}^{2}}
= -\frac{n}{\overline{x}^{2}} < 0 .\]

\subhead{(b)}
$-E\left[\partial^{2}\log L/\partial\theta^{2}\right] = n/\theta^{2}$, so
$I_n(\theta)=n/\theta^{2}$ and the asymptotic standard error is
$\widehat{\theta}/\sqrt{n} = 28.8/\sqrt{10} = 9.107$. An approximate $95\%$
interval is
\[28.8 \pm 1.96(9.107) = (10.95,\ 46.65).\]

\subhead{(c)}
Exactly, $2\sum_i X_i/\theta \sim \chi^{2}_{(2n)} = \chi^{2}_{(20)}$. From the
tables $\chi^{2}_{20,\,0.975}=34.170$ and $\chi^{2}_{20,\,0.025}=9.591$, so
\[\left(\frac{2(288)}{34.170},\ \frac{2(288)}{9.591}\right) = (16.86,\ 60.06).\]

\begin{remark}
The exact interval is both wider and markedly asymmetric about $28.8$, while the
approximate one is symmetric by construction. With $n=10$ the normal
approximation is simply not good enough --- and note the approximate interval's
lower endpoint, $10.95$, sits well below the exact one at $16.86$. Use the exact
interval whenever the pivotal quantity is available, as it is here.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\subsection{The Multiparameter Case}

When $\theta = (\theta_1, \dots, \theta_k)^{\mathsf T}$ the likelihood equation is a
system of $k$ equations in $k$ unknowns. Under regularity conditions like those of
theorems~\ref{thm:mleconsistent} and~\ref{thm:mlenormal}, there is a consistent root
$\hat\theta_n$, and
\[\sqrt n\left(\hat\theta_n - \theta\right)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}\text{MVN}\left(0, J_1(\theta)^{-1}\right),\]
where $J_1(\theta)$ is the Fisher information matrix of one observation. For a
differentiable function $\tau(\theta)$ with gradient
$D(\theta) = \left(\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial\theta_1}, \dots, \frac{\partial\tau}{\partial\theta_k}\right)^{\mathsf T}$,
the delta method gives
\[\sqrt n\left(\tau(\hat\theta_n) - \tau(\theta)\right)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}N\left(0, D(\theta)^{\mathsf T}J_1(\theta)^{-1}D(\theta)\right).\]
Approximate confidence intervals for a single component $\theta_j$ come from the
approximate normality of
\[\frac{\hat\theta_j - \theta_j}{\sqrt{\left[J(\hat\theta_n)^{-1}\right]_{jj}}}\qquad\text{or}\qquad
\frac{\hat\theta_j - \theta_j}{\sqrt{\left[I(\hat\theta_n)^{-1}\right]_{jj}}},\]
where $\left[J^{-1}\right]_{jj}$ is the $(j, j)$ entry of the \emph{inverse} matrix, not
the reciprocal of $J_{jj}$. The difference matters: the reciprocal of the diagonal
entry is the variance the estimator would have if the other parameters were known.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:gammaci}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{GAM}(\alpha, \beta)$. Find
approximate $95\%$ confidence intervals for $\beta$ and for the mean
$\tau(\theta) = \alpha\beta$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
From example~\ref{ex:gammamle},
$J(\theta) = n\begin{pmatrix}\psi'(\alpha) & \frac1\beta\\ \frac1\beta & \frac{\alpha}{\beta^2}\end{pmatrix}$,
with determinant $\frac{n^2\left(\alpha\psi'(\alpha) - 1\right)}{\beta^2}$, so
\[J(\theta)^{-1} = \frac{\beta^2}{n\left(\alpha\psi'(\alpha) - 1\right)}
\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\alpha}{\beta^2} & -\frac1\beta\\[1ex] -\frac1\beta & \psi'(\alpha)\end{pmatrix}.\]
The $(2, 2)$ entry is $\frac{\beta^2\psi'(\alpha)}{n\left(\alpha\psi'(\alpha) - 1\right)}$, and
the approximate interval for $\beta$ is
\[\hat\beta\pm 1.96\,\hat\beta\sqrt{\frac{\psi'(\hat\alpha)}{n\left(\hat\alpha\psi'(\hat\alpha) - 1\right)}} .\]
For $\tau = \alpha\beta$, $D(\theta) = (\beta, \alpha)^{\mathsf T}$ and
\[D^{\mathsf T}J^{-1}D = \frac{\beta^2}{n\left(\alpha\psi'(\alpha) - 1\right)}
\left[\alpha - 2\alpha + \alpha^2\psi'(\alpha)\right]
= \frac{\alpha\beta^2}{n} .\]
So the interval for the mean is $\hat\alpha\hat\beta\pm 1.96\,\hat\beta\sqrt{\frac{\hat\alpha}{n}}$.
This has a simple explanation: the second score equation of example~\ref{ex:gammamle}
says $\hat\alpha\hat\beta = \bar x$, and $\var(\bar X) = \frac{\alpha\beta^2}{n}$ exactly.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
For problem~\ref{prob:multinomialmle}, find an approximate $95\%$ C.I for \[\ \tau (\theta) = \cov (X_1, X_2) = -n\theta_1\theta_2.\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
From the multinomial problem, $\widehat{\theta}_j = X_j/n$ and
\[J(\theta) = n\begin{pmatrix}
\dfrac{1}{\theta_1}+\dfrac{1}{\theta_3} & \dfrac{1}{\theta_3}\\[6pt]
\dfrac{1}{\theta_3} & \dfrac{1}{\theta_2}+\dfrac{1}{\theta_3}\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
J^{-1}(\theta) = \frac{1}{n}\begin{pmatrix}
\theta_1(1-\theta_1) & -\theta_1\theta_2\\
-\theta_1\theta_2 & \theta_2(1-\theta_2)\end{pmatrix}.\]
With $\tau(\theta) = -n\theta_1\theta_2$,
\[D(\theta) = \left(\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial\theta_1},\ \frac{\partial\tau}{\partial\theta_2}\right)^{t}
= -n\left(\theta_2,\ \theta_1\right)^{t},\]
and the delta method gives
\[\var\left(\widehat{\tau}\right) \approx D^{t}J^{-1}D
= n\,\theta_1\theta_2\left(\theta_1+\theta_2-4\theta_1\theta_2\right).\]
Substituting the estimates, an approximate $95\%$ interval for
$\cov(X_1,X_2)$ is
\[-n\widehat{\theta}_1\widehat{\theta}_2 \ \pm\ 1.96
\sqrt{n\,\widehat{\theta}_1\widehat{\theta}_2
\left(\widehat{\theta}_1+\widehat{\theta}_2-4\widehat{\theta}_1\widehat{\theta}_2\right)} .\]
The interval should be truncated at zero on the right, since a multinomial
covariance is always negative.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
In problem~\ref{prob:lomaxmle}, find an approximate $95\%$ C.I for $\beta$ and $E(X)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
From the earlier problem the information matrix for $\theta=(\alpha,\beta)^{t}$ is
\[J(\theta) = n\begin{pmatrix}
\dfrac{1}{\alpha^{2}} & \dfrac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)}\\[6pt]
\dfrac{1}{\beta(\alpha+1)} & \dfrac{\alpha}{\beta^{2}(\alpha+2)}\end{pmatrix},\]
whose determinant is
\begin{align*}
\left|J\right| &= n^{2}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha\beta^{2}(\alpha+2)}
- \frac{1}{\beta^{2}(\alpha+1)^{2}}\right]\\
&= \frac{n^{2}}{\alpha\beta^{2}(\alpha+2)(\alpha+1)^{2}}
\left[(\alpha+1)^{2}-\alpha(\alpha+2)\right]\\
&= \frac{n^{2}}{\alpha\beta^{2}(\alpha+2)(\alpha+1)^{2}} ,
\end{align*}
using $(\alpha+1)^{2}-\alpha(\alpha+2)=1$.

\subhead{Interval for $\beta$}
The relevant entry of $J^{-1}$ is
\[\left[J^{-1}\right]_{22} = \frac{n/\alpha^{2}}{\left|J\right|}
= \frac{\beta^{2}(\alpha+1)^{2}(\alpha+2)}{n\,\alpha},\]
so an approximate $95\%$ interval is
\[\widehat{\beta} \pm 1.96\,\frac{\widehat{\beta}\left(\widehat{\alpha}+1\right)}{\sqrt{n}}
\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{\alpha}+2}{\widehat{\alpha}}} .\]

\subhead{Interval for $E(X)$}
Integration gives $\tau(\theta)=E(X)=\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha-1)}$ for $\alpha>1$,
so
\[D(\theta) = \left(-\frac{1}{\beta(\alpha-1)^{2}},\ -\frac{1}{\beta^{2}(\alpha-1)}\right)^{t},\]
and the interval is $\widehat{\tau} \pm 1.96\sqrt{D^{t}J^{-1}D}$ with everything
evaluated at the maximum likelihood estimates. Note the mean does not exist at
all unless $\alpha>1$, so the interval is meaningful only when
$\widehat{\alpha}$ is comfortably above one.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Suppose $X_1, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ is a random sample from  the $BETA(\alpha\, ,\, \beta)$ distribution. Explain how you would find the $ML$ estimate of $\theta = (\alpha, \beta)^t$, approximate $95\%$ C.I's for $\alpha$ and \[\tau(\theta) = E(X) = \frac{\alpha}{\alpha + \beta}.\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\subhead{The estimate}
\[\log L = n\log\Gamma(\alpha+\beta) - n\log\Gamma(\alpha) - n\log\Gamma(\beta)
+ (\alpha-1)\sum_i\log x_i + (\beta-1)\sum_i\log(1-x_i).\]
Differentiating and writing $\psi=\Gamma'/\Gamma$ for the digamma function, the
likelihood equations are
\[\psi(\alpha+\beta)-\psi(\alpha) = -\frac{1}{n}\sum_i\log x_i,\qquad
\psi(\alpha+\beta)-\psi(\beta) = -\frac{1}{n}\sum_i\log(1-x_i).\]
Neither can be solved in closed form; the pair is solved numerically by
Newton--Raphson, using method-of-moments values as starting points. This is the
same situation as the previous problem, but with no parameter eliminable in
closed form, so the search is two-dimensional.

\subhead{The information matrix}
Differentiating once more gives, with $\psi'$ the trigamma function,
\[J(\theta) = n\begin{pmatrix}
\psi'(\alpha)-\psi'(\alpha+\beta) & -\psi'(\alpha+\beta)\\
-\psi'(\alpha+\beta) & \psi'(\beta)-\psi'(\alpha+\beta)\end{pmatrix},\]
which is free of the data, so the observed and expected information coincide.

\subhead{Intervals}
For $\alpha$, use $\widehat{\alpha}\pm1.96\sqrt{\left[J^{-1}\right]_{11}}$
evaluated at the estimates. For $\tau(\theta)=\alpha/(\alpha+\beta)$,
\[D(\theta) = \left(\frac{\beta}{(\alpha+\beta)^{2}},\ \frac{-\alpha}{(\alpha+\beta)^{2}}\right)^{t},\]
and the interval is $\widehat{\tau}\pm1.96\sqrt{D^{t}J^{-1}D}$. Since $\tau$ is
a probability the interval should be truncated to $[0,1]$; a logit
transformation before applying the delta method usually behaves better near the
endpoints.
\end{solution}

\subsection*{Further reading}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Fisher, R. A. (1925), Theory of statistical estimation, \emph{Proceedings of
the Cambridge Philosophical Society}, 22, 700--725.
\item Cram\'er, H. (1946), \emph{Mathematical Methods of Statistics}, Princeton
University Press, chapters 32 and 33.
\item Wald, A. (1949), Note on the consistency of the maximum likelihood estimate,
\emph{Annals of Mathematical Statistics}, 20, 595--601.
\item Le Cam, L. (1953), On some asymptotic properties of maximum likelihood
estimates and related Bayes' estimates, \emph{University of California
Publications in Statistics}, 1, 277--330.
\end{enumerate}



\chapter{Other Estimation Criteria}

Unbiasedness with minimum variance and maximum likelihood are not the only ways
to choose an estimator. This chapter looks at four others: restricting to linear
estimators, requiring the estimator to respect a symmetry of the model, solving an
unbiased estimating equation, and averaging over a prior distribution.

\section{Best Linear Unbiased Estimators}

An estimator is a \emph{best linear unbiased estimator} (BLUE) if it is a linear
function of the observations, is unbiased, and has the smallest variance among all
linear unbiased estimators.

Suppose $Y = (Y_1, \dots, Y_n)^{\mathsf T}$ has expectation $E(Y) = X\beta$, where
$X$ is a known $n\times k$ matrix of rank $k$ and $\beta = (\beta_1, \dots, \beta_k)^{\mathsf T}$
is a vector of unknown parameters, and covariance matrix $\var(Y) = \sigma^2V$, where
$V$ is a known non-singular matrix and $\sigma^2$ an unknown scalar. We want the BLUE
of a linear combination $\theta = a^{\mathsf T}\beta$, where $a$ is a known
$k$-vector.

\begin{thm}[Gauss--Markov]
\label{thm:gaussmarkov}
Under these assumptions the BLUE of $\theta = a^{\mathsf T}\beta$ is
\[\hat\theta = a^{\mathsf T}\hat\beta,\qquad
\hat\beta = \left(X^{\mathsf T}V^{-1}X\right)^{-1}X^{\mathsf T}V^{-1}Y,\]
and its variance is $\sigma^2a^{\mathsf T}\left(X^{\mathsf T}V^{-1}X\right)^{-1}a$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Write $M = \left(X^{\mathsf T}V^{-1}X\right)^{-1}$, which exists because $X$ has full
rank and $V$ is positive definite. The estimator $\hat\theta = c_0^{\mathsf T}Y$ with
$c_0 = V^{-1}XMa$ is linear, and
$E(\hat\theta) = a^{\mathsf T}MX^{\mathsf T}V^{-1}X\beta = a^{\mathsf T}\beta$, so it is
unbiased. Any linear estimator $c^{\mathsf T}Y$ has mean $c^{\mathsf T}X\beta$, and it is
unbiased for $a^{\mathsf T}\beta$ for every $\beta$ exactly when $X^{\mathsf T}c = a$.
Write $c = c_0 + d$; since $X^{\mathsf T}c_0 = X^{\mathsf T}V^{-1}XMa = a$, unbiasedness
forces $X^{\mathsf T}d = 0$. Then
\begin{align*}
\var(c^{\mathsf T}Y) &= \sigma^2(c_0 + d)^{\mathsf T}V(c_0 + d)\\
&= \sigma^2\left[c_0^{\mathsf T}Vc_0 + 2d^{\mathsf T}Vc_0 + d^{\mathsf T}Vd\right].
\end{align*}
The cross term vanishes because $Vc_0 = XMa$ and $d^{\mathsf T}X = 0$. So
$\var(c^{\mathsf T}Y) = \var(\hat\theta) + \sigma^2d^{\mathsf T}Vd\ge\var(\hat\theta)$,
with equality only when $d = 0$. Finally
$\var(\hat\theta) = \sigma^2c_0^{\mathsf T}Vc_0 = \sigma^2a^{\mathsf T}MX^{\mathsf T}V^{-1}XMa = \sigma^2a^{\mathsf T}Ma$.
\end{proof}

When $V = I$ the BLUE is the ordinary least squares estimator
$\hat\beta = (X^{\mathsf T}X)^{-1}X^{\mathsf T}Y$; for general $V$ it is the generalised
least squares (Aitken) estimator. The theorem needs no distributional assumption
beyond the first two moments, but it compares only \emph{linear} estimators. For
normal errors, $\hat\beta$ is also the UMVUE, so nothing is lost by the restriction;
for heavy-tailed errors a nonlinear estimator can be much better.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:blueequal}
Let $Y_1, \dots, Y_n$ be uncorrelated, each with mean $\theta$ and the same unknown
variance $\sigma^2$. Find the BLUE of $\theta$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
In the notation of theorem~\ref{thm:gaussmarkov}, $k = 1$, $\beta = \theta$, $a = 1$,
$X = \mathbf 1 = (1, \dots, 1)^{\mathsf T}$ and $V = I$. Then
$X^{\mathsf T}V^{-1}X = \mathbf 1^{\mathsf T}\mathbf 1 = n$ and
$X^{\mathsf T}V^{-1}Y = \sum Y_i$, so
\[\hat\theta = \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n Y_i = \bar Y,\qquad \var(\hat\theta) = \frac{\sigma^2}{n} .\]
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:blueline}
Let $Y_i = \beta_0 + \beta_1x_i + \varepsilon_i$, $i = 1, \dots, n$, where the $x_i$ are
known constants, not all equal, and the $\varepsilon_i$ are uncorrelated with mean $0$
and variance $\sigma^2$. Find the BLUE of the slope $\beta_1$ and its variance.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Here $V = I$ and $X$ has rows $(1, x_i)$, so
\[X^{\mathsf T}X = \begin{pmatrix} n & \sum x_i\\ \sum x_i & \sum x_i^2\end{pmatrix},\qquad
X^{\mathsf T}Y = \begin{pmatrix}\sum Y_i\\ \sum x_iY_i\end{pmatrix}.\]
The determinant is $n\sum x_i^2 - \left(\sum x_i\right)^2 = nS_{xx}$, where
$S_{xx} = \sum (x_i - \bar x)^2>0$. Taking $a = (0, 1)^{\mathsf T}$,
\[\hat\beta_1 = \frac{n\sum x_iY_i - \sum x_i\sum Y_i}{nS_{xx}} = \frac{\sum (x_i - \bar x)Y_i}{S_{xx}},\qquad
\var(\hat\beta_1) = \sigma^2\left[(X^{\mathsf T}X)^{-1}\right]_{22} = \frac{\sigma^2}{S_{xx}} .\]
This is the least squares slope. The variance is smallest when the $x_i$ are spread
out, which is why experiments to estimate a slope put the design points at the
extremes of the range.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Suppose $Y_1, \, \cdots\, , \, Y_n$ are independent unbiased estimators of $\theta$ with known variances $\var (Y_i) = \sigma_i^2, \, i = 1, \, 2, \, \cdots\, , \, n$. Find the BLUE of $\theta$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
A linear estimator $\sum_i a_iY_i$ is unbiased for $\theta$ if and only if
$\sum_i a_i = 1$, since $E\left(\sum_i a_iY_i\right)=\theta\sum_i a_i$. Its
variance is $\sum_i a_i^{2}\sigma_i^{2}$ by independence, so the problem is to
minimise $\sum_i a_i^{2}\sigma_i^{2}$ subject to $\sum_i a_i=1$.

With a Lagrange multiplier $\lambda$, differentiating
$\sum_i a_i^{2}\sigma_i^{2} - \lambda\left(\sum_i a_i - 1\right)$ gives
$2a_i\sigma_i^{2}=\lambda$, so $a_i \propto 1/\sigma_i^{2}$. Normalising to make
the weights sum to one,
\[\widehat{\theta} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} Y_i/\sigma_i^{2}}
{\sum_{i=1}^{n} 1/\sigma_i^{2}},
\qquad
\var\left(\widehat{\theta}\right) = \frac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}1/\sigma_i^{2}} .\]

\begin{note}
Each estimator is weighted by its precision, the reciprocal of its variance, and
the precision of the combination is the \emph{sum} of the individual precisions.
So adding even a poor estimator never hurts --- it can only increase the total
precision --- which is the formal basis of meta-analysis and of inverse-variance
weighting generally.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Suppose $Y_{ij}, \, i = 1, \, 2, \ j  = 1, \, 2, \, \cdots\, , n$ are independent random variables with $E(Y_{ij}) = \mu  + \alpha_i$ and $\var (Y_{ij}) = \sigma^2$ where $\alpha_1 + \alpha_2 = 0$. Find the BLUE of $\alpha_1$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The constraint $\alpha_1+\alpha_2=0$ means $\alpha_2=-\alpha_1$, so
\[E\left(\overline{Y}_{1\cdot}\right) = \mu+\alpha_1,\qquad
E\left(\overline{Y}_{2\cdot}\right) = \mu-\alpha_1 ,\]
where $\overline{Y}_{i\cdot}=n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}Y_{ij}$. Subtracting removes
$\mu$:
\[E\left(\overline{Y}_{1\cdot}-\overline{Y}_{2\cdot}\right) = 2\alpha_1 ,\]
so
\[\widehat{\alpha_1} = \frac{\overline{Y}_{1\cdot}-\overline{Y}_{2\cdot}}{2}\]
is linear and unbiased. Its variance is
\[\var\left(\widehat{\alpha_1}\right) = \frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\right)
= \frac{\sigma^{2}}{2n} .\]
That it is \emph{best} follows from the Gauss--Markov theorem: the model is
linear with uncorrelated errors of constant variance, and
$\widehat{\alpha_1}$ is the least squares estimator of the estimable function
$\alpha_1$ under the constraint.
\end{solution}

\section{Equivariant Estimators}

Some models have a symmetry, and it is natural to ask that the estimator have the
same symmetry.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:locationfamily}
A model $\{f_\theta(x) : \theta\in\mathbb{R}\}$ with $f_\theta(x) = f_0(x - \theta)$ for a
known density $f_0$ is a \emph{location family}, and $\theta$ is a \emph{location
parameter}.
\end{defn}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:locationfamilies}
Show that $N(\theta, 1)$, the Cauchy distribution with location $\theta$, and the
shifted exponential $f_\theta(x) = e^{-(x - \theta)}$, $x>\theta$, are location families.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
In each case $f_\theta(x) = f_0(x - \theta)$, with respectively
\[f_0(z) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-z^2/2},\qquad
f_0(z) = \frac{1}{\pi(1 + z^2)},\qquad
f_0(z) = e^{-z}I(z>0) .\]
In each, $X = \theta + Z$ where $Z$ has the fixed density $f_0$.
\end{solution}

If $X_i = \theta + Z_i$, shifting every observation by $a$ is the same as shifting the
parameter by $a$. A sensible estimator should therefore shift by $a$ too.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:locationequivariant}
An estimator $\tilde\theta(X_1, \dots, X_n)$ is \emph{location equivariant} if
\[\tilde\theta(x_1 + a, \dots, x_n + a) = \tilde\theta(x_1, \dots, x_n) + a\]
for every constant $a$ and every sample $(x_1, \dots, x_n)$.
\end{defn}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:meanequivariant}
Show that the sample mean, the sample median and $X_{(1)}$ are location equivariant.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$\frac1n\sum(x_i + a) = \frac1n\sum x_i + \frac1n\cdot na = \bar x + a$. Adding $a$ to
every observation does not change their order, so every order statistic, and in
particular the median and $X_{(1)}$, increases by $a$. By contrast,
$\frac1n\sum x_i^2$ is not equivariant.
\end{solution}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:equivariantrisk}
In a location family, the bias, variance and mean square error of a location
equivariant estimator do not depend on $\theta$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Write $X_i = \theta + Z_i$, where $Z_1, \dots, Z_n$ are independent with the known
density $f_0$. By equivariance with $a = \theta$,
\[\tilde\theta(X_1, \dots, X_n) - \theta = \tilde\theta(Z_1, \dots, Z_n),\]
whose distribution involves $f_0$ only. Hence the distribution of the error
$\tilde\theta - \theta$, and every moment of it, is the same for all $\theta$.
\end{proof}

Since every equivariant estimator has constant risk, they can be compared by a
single number, and there is a best one. It is called the \emph{Pitman estimator}.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:bestequivariant}
For a random sample from the shifted exponential $f_\theta(x) = e^{-(x - \theta)}$,
$x>\theta$, find the best estimator, in mean square error, of the form
$X_{(1)} - c$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Each such estimator is equivariant. By example~\ref{ex:shiftedexp},
$X_{(1)} - \theta$ is exponential with mean $\frac1n$ and variance $\frac1{n^2}$, so
\[\MSE\left(X_{(1)} - c\right) = E\left(X_{(1)} - \theta - c\right)^2
= \frac{1}{n^2} + \left(\frac1n - c\right)^2 ,\]
which, as theorem~\ref{thm:equivariantrisk} says, is free of $\theta$. It is minimised
at $c = \frac1n$, giving the unbiased estimator $X_{(1)} - \frac1n$ with mean square
error $\frac{1}{n^2}$, half that of the ML estimator $X_{(1)}$.
\end{solution}

\begin{defn}
\label{def:scalefamily}
A model $\{f_\theta(x) : \theta>0\}$ with $f_\theta(x) = \frac1\theta f_1\left(\frac x\theta\right)$
for a known density $f_1$ is a \emph{scale family}, and $\theta$ is a \emph{scale
parameter}. An estimator $\tilde\theta_k$ of $\theta^k$ is \emph{scale equivariant} if
\[\tilde\theta_k(cx_1, \dots, cx_n) = c^k\,\tilde\theta_k(x_1, \dots, x_n)\]
for every $c>0$ and every sample.
\end{defn}

In a scale family $X = \theta Z$ with $Z$ having density $f_1$, so changing the unit
of measurement multiplies $\theta$ by $c$ and $\theta^k$ by $c^k$.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:normalscale}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(0, \theta^2)$, $\theta>0$. Show that
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] this is a scale family;
\item[(b)] the UMVUE of $\theta^2$ is scale equivariant.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $f_\theta(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\theta}e^{-\frac12(x/\theta)^2}
= \frac1\theta f_1\left(\frac x\theta\right)$ with $f_1(z) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-z^2/2}$.
\item[(b)] The family is a REF with natural sufficient statistic $\sum X_i^2$, which
is complete. Since $E(X_i^2) = \theta^2$, the UMVUE of $\theta^2$ is
$\tilde\theta_2 = \frac1n\sum X_i^2$, and
\[\tilde\theta_2(cx_1, \dots, cx_n) = \frac1n\sum(cx_i)^2 = c^2\,\tilde\theta_2(x_1, \dots, x_n),\]
so it is scale equivariant with $k = 2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Show that the $UNIF(\theta - 1, \theta + 1)$ distribution is a location invariant family.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The UNIF$(\theta-1,\theta+1)$ density is
\[f_{\theta}(x) = \frac12\,I\left(\theta-1<x<\theta+1\right)
= \frac12\,I\left(-1<x-\theta<1\right) = f_0(x-\theta),\]
where $f_0$ is the UNIF$(-1,1)$ density. Since every member of the family is
obtained from the single density $f_0$ by a shift, the family is location
invariant with location parameter $\theta$.

Equivalently: if $X\sim$ UNIF$(\theta-1,\theta+1)$ then $X+c\sim$
UNIF$(\theta+c-1,\ \theta+c+1)$, so translating the data by $c$ moves the
parameter by $c$ and leaves the family as a whole unchanged.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample the $UNIF(0,\theta)$ distribution. Show that then
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $UNIF(0,\theta)$ densities is a scale invariant family.
\item[(b)] $UMVUE$ of $\theta$ is a scale equivariant estimator.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\subhead{(a)}
The UNIF$(0,\theta)$ density is
\[f_{\theta}(x) = \frac{1}{\theta}\,I\left(0<x<\theta\right)
= \frac{1}{\theta}\,I\left(0<\frac{x}{\theta}<1\right)
= \frac{1}{\theta}f_1\!\left(\frac{x}{\theta}\right),\]
with $f_1$ the UNIF$(0,1)$ density. Every member is obtained from $f_1$ by the
scaling $x\mapsto x/\theta$, so the family is scale invariant. Equivalently, if
$X\sim$ UNIF$(0,\theta)$ and $c>0$ then $cX\sim$ UNIF$(0,c\theta)$.

\subhead{(b)}
Since $E\left(X_{(n)}\right)=n\theta/(n+1)$ and $X_{(n)}$ is complete and
sufficient, the UMVUE is
\[\widehat{\theta} = \frac{n+1}{n}X_{(n)} .\]
Scale equivariance asks that $\widehat{\theta}(cX_1,\dots,cX_n) = c\,\widehat{\theta}(X_1,\dots,X_n)$
for every $c>0$. The maximum of the rescaled sample is $c\,X_{(n)}$, so
\[\widehat{\theta}(cX) = \frac{n+1}{n}\,cX_{(n)} = c\,\widehat{\theta}(X),\]
and the estimator is scale equivariant.

\begin{note}
Equivariance is a consistency requirement, not an optimality one: it says only
that measuring the data in different units should change the estimate in the
same units. It is worth checking because an estimator that fails it is
answering a different question depending on whether the lifetimes were recorded
in days or in hours.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\section{Estimating Equations}

The ML estimator solves the likelihood equation $\sum_{i=1}^n S_1(\theta; X_i) = 0$. A
function of the data and the parameter used in this way is called an
\emph{estimating function}, and it need not come from a likelihood. For example, if
the $X_i$ are independent and identically distributed with $\var(X_i) = \theta$, the
estimating function
\[\Psi(\theta; X) = \sum^n_{i = 1}\left(X_i - \bar X\right)^2 - (n - 1)\theta\]
can be set equal to zero and solved, giving $\hat\theta = S^2$, without any knowledge
of the form of the distribution or of its mean.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:unbiasedee}
An estimating function $\Psi(\theta; X)$ is \emph{unbiased} if
$E_\theta\left[\Psi(\theta; X)\right] = 0$ for all $\theta$.
\end{defn}

The score is an unbiased estimating function (theorem~\ref{thm:scoreproperties}).
The large-sample theory of section~\ref{sec:largesample} carries over to roots of
unbiased estimating equations, with a different information.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:godambe}
Let $\Psi(\theta; X) = \sum_{i=1}^n\psi(\theta; X_i)$ be an unbiased estimating function
with $X_1, \dots, X_n$ independent and identically distributed, and let $\hat\theta$ be
a consistent root of $\Psi(\theta; X) = 0$. Under regularity conditions $\hat\theta$ is
approximately normal with mean $\theta$ and variance $\frac{1}{J(\Psi, \theta)}$, where
\[J(\Psi, \theta) = \frac{\left[E\left(\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\theta}\right)\right]^2}{\var\left[\Psi(\theta; X)\right]}\]
is the \emph{Godambe information} of the estimating function.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
As in theorem~\ref{thm:mlenormal}, $0 = \Psi(\hat\theta) \approx \Psi(\theta) +
(\hat\theta - \theta)\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\theta}(\theta)$, so
$\hat\theta - \theta\approx -\Psi(\theta)\big/\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\theta}(\theta)$. The
numerator is a sum of independent terms with mean zero and is approximately normal
with variance $\var(\Psi)$; the denominator is close to its expectation
$E\left(\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\theta}\right)$ by the law of large numbers. So
$\var(\hat\theta)\approx\var(\Psi)\big/\left[E\left(\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\theta}\right)\right]^2$.
\end{proof}

For the score, $E\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial\theta}\right) = -J(\theta)$ and
$\var(S) = J(\theta)$, so the Godambe information reduces to the Fisher information.
Godambe showed that among unbiased estimating functions the score has the largest
Godambe information, which is the estimating-equation version of the efficiency of
maximum likelihood.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:eelog}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from a distribution with
$E(\ln X_i) = e^\theta$ and $\var(\ln X_i) = e^{2\theta}$. Consider
\[\Psi(\theta; X) = \sum^n_{i = 1}\left(\ln X_i - e^\theta\right).\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Show that $\Psi$ is unbiased.
\item[(b)] Find the root $\hat\theta$ of $\Psi(\theta; X) = 0$.
\item[(c)] Construct an approximate $95\%$ confidence interval for $\theta$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $E(\Psi) = \sum\left[E(\ln X_i) - e^\theta\right] = 0$.
\item[(b)] $\sum\ln X_i = ne^\theta$ gives $\hat\theta = \ln\left(\frac1n\sum\ln X_i\right)$,
defined when $\sum\ln X_i>0$, which holds with probability tending to one because
the mean $e^\theta$ is positive.
\item[(c)] $\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\theta} = -ne^\theta$, which is not random, and
$\var(\Psi) = \sum\var(\ln X_i) = ne^{2\theta}$. So
\[J(\Psi, \theta) = \frac{n^2e^{2\theta}}{ne^{2\theta}} = n,\]
and the approximate interval is $\hat\theta\pm\frac{1.96}{\sqrt n}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:eeratio}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the Bernoulli$(p)$ distribution with
$0<p<1$, and let $Y_i = \theta X_i + \varepsilon_i$, where $\varepsilon_1, \dots, \varepsilon_n$
are independent $N(0, \sigma^2)$ variables independent of the $X_i$. Consider
\[\Psi\left(\theta; (X, Y)\right) = \sum^n_{i = 1}\left(Y_i - \theta X_i\right).\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Show that $\Psi$ is an unbiased estimating function for $\theta$.
\item[(b)] Find the root $\hat\theta$.
\item[(c)] Construct an approximate $95\%$ confidence interval for $\theta$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $Y_i - \theta X_i = \varepsilon_i$, so $E(\Psi) = \sum E(\varepsilon_i) = 0$ for every
$\theta$, whatever $p$ and $\sigma^2$ are.
\item[(b)] $\sum Y_i = \theta\sum X_i$ gives $\hat\theta = \frac{\sum Y_i}{\sum X_i}$, defined when
at least one $X_i = 1$.
\item[(c)] $\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\theta} = -\sum X_i$, with expectation $-np$, and
$\var(\Psi) = \var\left(\sum\varepsilon_i\right) = n\sigma^2$. So
\[J(\Psi, \theta) = \frac{n^2p^2}{n\sigma^2} = \frac{np}{\sigma^2} .\]
Estimating $p$ by $\bar X$ and $\sigma^2$ by
$\hat\sigma^2 = \frac{1}{n - 1}\sum\left(Y_i - \hat\theta X_i\right)^2$, the approximate
interval is
\[\hat\theta\pm 1.96\frac{\hat\sigma}{\sqrt{\sum X_i}} .\]
Only the observations with $X_i = 1$ carry information about $\theta$, and the
interval width depends on how many of them there are.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\section{Bayes Estimation}

There are two main schools of statistical inference. The \emph{frequentist} school
regards a parameter as an unknown constant. The \emph{Bayesian} school regards
$\theta$ as a random variable with a \emph{prior distribution} $\pi(\theta)$, expressing
what is believed about $\theta$ before the data are seen. The data then update the
prior to a \emph{posterior distribution}, by Bayes' theorem.

\begin{thm}[Posterior distribution]
\label{thm:posterior}
If $\theta$ has prior density $\pi(\theta)$ and, given $\theta$, the data
$x = (x_1, \dots, x_n)$ are a random sample from $f_\theta$, then the conditional
density of $\theta$ given $x$ is
\[\pi(\theta\mid x) = \frac{\pi(\theta)L(\theta)}{\int\pi(t)L(t)\,dt},\qquad
L(\theta) = \prod^n_{i = 1}f_\theta(x_i) .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The joint density of $(\theta, X)$ is $\pi(\theta)L(\theta)$, and the marginal density of
$X$ at $x$ is $\int\pi(t)L(t)\,dt$. Their ratio is the conditional density.
\end{proof}

In practice one writes $\pi(\theta\mid x)\propto\pi(\theta)L(\theta)$, that is, posterior is
proportional to prior times likelihood, and identifies the normalising constant at
the end by recognising the form of the density. Factors of $L(\theta)$ that do not
involve $\theta$ can be dropped.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:betabinomial}
A coin with probability $\theta$ of heads is tossed $n$ times and gives $x$ heads.
With the prior $\theta\sim\text{BETA}(10, 10)$, find the posterior distribution of
$\theta$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$L(\theta)\propto\theta^x(1 - \theta)^{n - x}$ and
$\pi(\theta)\propto\theta^9(1 - \theta)^9$ on $(0, 1)$, so
\[\pi(\theta\mid x)\propto\theta^{x + 9}(1 - \theta)^{n - x + 9},\qquad 0<\theta<1 .\]
This is a beta density, and the constant must be the reciprocal of
$B(x + 10, n - x + 10) = \int_0^1\theta^{x + 9}(1 - \theta)^{n - x + 9}\,d\theta$, where
$B(\alpha, \beta) = \frac{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha + \beta)}$. So
$\theta\mid x\sim\text{BETA}(x + 10, n - x + 10)$.
\end{solution}

\begin{defn}
\label{def:conjugate}
A family of prior distributions is \emph{conjugate} for a model if the posterior
distribution is in the same family as the prior for every data set.
\end{defn}

Example~\ref{ex:betabinomial} shows that the beta family is conjugate for the
binomial. The prior parameters act like extra data: a $\text{BETA}(10, 10)$ prior
behaves like $20$ earlier tosses with $10$ heads.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:gammaconjugate}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $f_\theta(x) = \theta x^{\theta - 1}$,
$0<x<1$, $\theta>0$. Show that the prior $\theta\sim\text{GAM}\left(a + 1, \frac1b\right)$,
with density proportional to $\theta^ae^{-b\theta}$, is conjugate.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$L(\theta) = \theta^n\left(\prod x_i\right)^{\theta - 1}\propto\theta^n e^{\theta\sum\ln x_i}$, so
\[\pi(\theta\mid x)\propto\theta^ae^{-b\theta}\,\theta^ne^{\theta\sum\ln x_i}
= \theta^{a + n}\exp\left\{-\theta\left(b - \sum\ln x_i\right)\right\}.\]
Since every $\ln x_i<0$, the coefficient $b - \sum\ln x_i$ is positive, and the
posterior is
\[\theta\mid x\sim\text{GAM}\left(a + n + 1, \frac{1}{b - \sum\ln x_i}\right),\]
again a gamma distribution.
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{Bayes point estimation}

A point estimate can be obtained from the posterior distribution by choosing a loss
function and minimising the expected loss under the posterior. Which estimator
results depends on the loss.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:bayessquared}
For squared error loss $L(\theta, t) = (t - \theta)^2$, the Bayes estimator, the value $t$
that minimises the posterior expected loss $E\left[(t - \theta)^2\mid x\right]$, is the
posterior mean
\[\tilde\theta(x) = E(\theta\mid x) = \int\theta\,\pi(\theta\mid x)\,d\theta .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Write $m = E(\theta\mid x)$. Then
\[E\left[(t - \theta)^2\mid x\right] = E\left[(t - m + m - \theta)^2\mid x\right]
= (t - m)^2 + \var(\theta\mid x),\]
since the cross term $2(t - m)E(m - \theta\mid x)$ is zero. This is smallest at
$t = m$.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:bayesabsolute}
For absolute error loss $L(\theta, t) = |t - \theta|$, the Bayes estimator is any median
of the posterior distribution.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Let $F$ be the posterior distribution function and $g(t) = E\left[|t - \theta|\mid x\right]
= \int_{-\infty}^t(t - \theta)\,dF(\theta) + \int_t^\infty(\theta - t)\,dF(\theta)$. For a
continuous posterior, differentiating gives $g'(t) = F(t) - (1 - F(t)) = 2F(t) - 1$,
which is negative for $F(t)<\frac12$ and positive for $F(t)>\frac12$. So $g$ is
minimised where $F(t) = \frac12$.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:bayespower}
In example~\ref{ex:gammaconjugate}, find the Bayes estimator of $\theta$ under
squared error loss, and compare it with the ML estimator.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
A $\text{GAM}(\alpha, \beta)$ distribution has mean $\alpha\beta$, so the posterior mean
is
\[\tilde\theta = \frac{a + n + 1}{b - \sum\ln X_i} .\]
With $T = -\sum\ln X_i$, the ML estimator is $\frac nT$ (example~\ref{ex:powerci}) and
the Bayes estimator is $\frac{n + a + 1}{T + b}$. The prior adds $a + 1$ to the
numerator and $b$ to the denominator, and its influence fades as $n$ and $T$ grow.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:bayesbinomialnumbers}
In example~\ref{ex:betabinomial}, suppose $n = 20$ tosses give $x = 14$ heads. Find
the Bayes estimates of $\theta$ under squared and absolute error loss, and compare
them with the ML estimate.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
The posterior is $\text{BETA}(24, 16)$. Its mean is $\frac{24}{24 + 16} = 0.600$,
and its median, found numerically, is $0.602$ (for a beta distribution with both
parameters above $1$ the median is close to $\frac{\alpha - 1/3}{\alpha + \beta - 2/3} = 0.602$).
The ML estimate is $\frac{14}{20} = 0.70$. The posterior mean can be written as
\[\frac{24}{40} = \frac{20}{40}\cdot\frac{14}{20} + \frac{20}{40}\cdot\frac{10}{20},\]
a weighted average of the sample proportion $0.7$ and the prior mean $0.5$, with
weights proportional to the sample size $20$ and the prior's equivalent sample size
$20$. This shrinkage towards the prior mean is typical of Bayes estimators.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let  $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $POI(\theta)$ distribution.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Show that the prior distribution \[\theta \, \thicksim\, GAM\left(b + 1, \frac{1}{a}\right)\] is a conjugate prior.
\item[(b)] using the conjugate prior in (a) find the Bayes estimator of $\theta$ for a squared error loss function.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\subhead{(a) Conjugacy}
Write $T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i$. The likelihood and the GAM$\left(b+1,1/a\right)$
prior are
\[L(\theta) \propto e^{-n\theta}\theta^{T},
\qquad \pi(\theta) \propto \theta^{b}e^{-a\theta},\quad \theta>0 .\]
Multiplying,
\[\pi\left(\theta \mid x\right) \propto \theta^{\,T+b}\,e^{-(n+a)\theta},\]
which is the kernel of a GAM$\left(T+b+1,\ \frac{1}{n+a}\right)$ density. The
posterior belongs to the same gamma family as the prior, so the family is
conjugate for the Poisson likelihood.

\subhead{(b) The Bayes estimator}
Under squared error loss the Bayes estimator is the posterior mean. A
GAM$(\kappa,\lambda)$ variable has mean $\kappa\lambda$, so
\[\widetilde{\theta} = \frac{T+b+1}{n+a}
= \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i + b + 1}{n+a} .\]

\begin{note}
Write it as a weighted average:
\[\widetilde{\theta} = \frac{n}{n+a}\,\overline{X} + \frac{a}{n+a}\cdot\frac{b+1}{a},\]
a compromise between the sample mean and the prior mean $(b+1)/a$, with weights
$n$ and $a$. So $a$ acts as a prior sample size: the prior carries as much
influence as $a$ observations, and its influence vanishes as $n$ grows.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let  $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $X\thicksim N(0, \sigma^2)$ distribution where $\sigma^2$ is known. Assuming the prior distribution is $\theta \, \thicksim\, N\left(\frac{b}{a}, \frac{\sigma^2}{a}\right),$ fined the Bayes estimator using a squared error loss function.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The model must be $X_i \sim N\left(\theta,\sigma^{2}\right)$ with $\sigma^{2}$
known --- as printed the mean is zero and $\theta$ does not appear in the
likelihood at all, so there would be nothing to estimate.

With $T=\sum_i X_i$ and prior $\theta\sim N\left(b/a,\ \sigma^{2}/a\right)$,
\[\pi\left(\theta\mid x\right)
\propto \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\sum_i\left(x_i-\theta\right)^{2}\right\}
\exp\left\{-\frac{a}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(\theta-\frac{b}{a}\right)^{2}\right\}.\]
Both exponents are quadratic in $\theta$, so the posterior is normal and it is
enough to collect the coefficients. Discarding terms free of $\theta$, the
exponent is
\[-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[(n+a)\theta^{2} - 2\theta\left(T+b\right)\right]
= -\frac{n+a}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[\theta - \frac{T+b}{n+a}\right]^{2} + \text{const} .\]
Hence
\[\theta \mid x \ \sim\ N\!\left(\frac{T+b}{n+a},\ \frac{\sigma^{2}}{n+a}\right),\]
and under squared error loss the Bayes estimator is the posterior mean
\[\widetilde{\theta} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i + b}{n+a} .\]

As in the Poisson problem this is a weighted average of $\overline{X}$ and the
prior mean $b/a$, with weights $n$ and $a$; the prior is worth $a$ observations,
and the posterior variance $\sigma^{2}/(n+a)$ says the same thing.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let  $X_1, \, X_2, \, \cdots\, , \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $POI(\theta)$ distribution. Assuming the prior distribution of $\theta$ is $\theta \thicksim BETA(1/2,1)$, find the Bayes estimator of $\theta$ using the squared error loss function.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The BETA$(1/2,1)$ prior is $\pi(\theta)\propto\theta^{-1/2}$ on $(0,1)$, so with
$T=\sum_i X_i$,
\[\pi\left(\theta\mid x\right) \propto \theta^{\,T-1/2}e^{-n\theta},
\qquad 0<\theta<1 .\]
This is a gamma kernel \emph{truncated} to the unit interval, not a gamma
density, so the normalising constant is an incomplete gamma function. Using
\[\int_0^{1}\theta^{\,s-1}e^{-n\theta}\,d\theta = n^{-s}\,\gamma(s,n),\]
where $\gamma$ is the lower incomplete gamma function, the posterior mean is
\[\widetilde{\theta}
= \frac{\int_0^{1}\theta^{\,T+1/2}e^{-n\theta}d\theta}{\int_0^{1}\theta^{\,T-1/2}e^{-n\theta}d\theta}
= \frac{n^{-(T+3/2)}\gamma\left(T+\tfrac32,\,n\right)}{n^{-(T+1/2)}\gamma\left(T+\tfrac12,\,n\right)}
= \frac{\gamma\left(T+\tfrac32,\,n\right)}{n\,\gamma\left(T+\tfrac12,\,n\right)} .\]
This is the Bayes estimator under squared error loss. It has no expression in
elementary functions and is evaluated numerically.

\begin{remark}
The prior deserves comment. A BETA$(1/2,1)$ distribution lives on $(0,1)$, so it
asserts with certainty that the Poisson rate is below one. That is a strong
claim, and it is what destroys conjugacy --- the gamma prior of the previous
problem is supported on all of $(0,\infty)$ and returns a gamma posterior. If
the restriction is genuinely believed the answer above is correct; if it was
chosen for convenience it is the wrong prior, and it is worth noticing that the
inconvenience of the answer is the model telling us so.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\subsection*{Further reading}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Bayes, T. (1763), An essay towards solving a problem in the doctrine of
chances, \emph{Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society}, 53, 370--418.
\item Aitken, A. C. (1935), On least squares and linear combination of
observations, \emph{Proceedings of the Royal Society of Edinburgh}, 55, 42--48.
\item Pitman, E. J. G. (1939), The estimation of the location and scale parameters
of a continuous population of any given form, \emph{Biometrika}, 30, 391--421.
\item Godambe, V. P. (1960), An optimum property of regular maximum likelihood
estimation, \emph{Annals of Mathematical Statistics}, 31, 1208--1211.
\end{enumerate}


\chapter{Hypothesis Tests}

\section{Introduction}

A hypothesis test divides the parameter space $\Omega$ into two disjoint parts,
$\Omega_0$ and $\Omega - \Omega_0$. The \emph{null hypothesis}
\[H_0\colon\theta\in\Omega_0\]
is usually the status quo, rejected only when there is reasonably strong evidence
against it. The \emph{alternative hypothesis}
\[H_1\colon\theta\in\Omega - \Omega_0\]
describes the departures from $H_0$ that we want to detect. A hypothesis is
\emph{simple} if its set contains a single point and \emph{composite} otherwise.

Since $\theta$ is unknown, the decision is based on the data. The sample space is
divided into a \emph{critical region} (or rejection region) $R$ and its complement
$R^c$, the acceptance region, and $H_0$ is rejected in favour of $H_1$ when the
observed $x$ falls in $R$. Two errors are possible: a \emph{type I error} is
rejecting $H_0$ when it is true, and a \emph{type II error} is accepting $H_0$ when
it is false.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:powerfunction}
The \emph{power function} of a test with critical region $R$ is
\[\beta(\theta) = P_\theta(X\in R),\]
the probability of rejecting $H_0$, as a function of $\theta$.
\end{defn}

For $\theta\in\Omega_0$, $\beta(\theta)$ is the probability of a type I error; for
$\theta\notin\Omega_0$, $1 - \beta(\theta)$ is the probability of a type II error. A good
test has $\beta(\theta)$ small on $\Omega_0$ and large off it.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:levelsize}
A test has \emph{level of significance} $\alpha$ if $\beta(\theta)\le\alpha$ for all
$\theta\in\Omega_0$. Its \emph{size} is $\sup_{\theta\in\Omega_0}\beta(\theta)$.
\end{defn}

The level is only an upper bound for the probability of a type I error, and need
not be attained; the size is the smallest such bound. When $H_0$ is simple, the size
is just $\beta(\theta_0)$.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:pvaluesi}
For a test that rejects $H_0$ for large values of a statistic $T$, the
\emph{$p$-value} of the observed value $t$ is $\sup_{\theta\in\Omega_0}P_\theta(T\ge t)$. The
test of size $\alpha$ rejects $H_0$ exactly when the $p$-value is at most $\alpha$.
\end{defn}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:fourcoin}
A coin is tossed $1000$ times and $X$, the number of heads, is recorded. To test
whether the coin is fair, consider the critical region
$R = \{x : x\le 469\ \text{or}\ x\ge 531\} = \{x : |x - 500|>30\}$. Find the size and
the power function of the test.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$X\sim\text{BIN}(1000, \theta)$ with $\Omega = (0, 1)$. The hypotheses are
$H_0\colon\theta = \frac12$, which is simple, and $H_1\colon\theta\ne\frac12$, which is
composite. Using the normal approximation with continuity correction, under $H_0$
$X$ has mean $500$ and variance $250$, so the size is
\[\beta\left(\tfrac12\right) = P(X\le 469) + P(X\ge 531) \approx 2P\left(Z\le\frac{469.5 - 500}{\sqrt{250}}\right) = 2\Phi(-1.929) = 0.054 .\]
For general $\theta$,
\[\beta(\theta)\approx\Phi\left(\frac{469.5 - 1000\theta}{\sqrt{1000\theta(1 - \theta)}}\right)
+ 1 - \Phi\left(\frac{530.5 - 1000\theta}{\sqrt{1000\theta(1 - \theta)}}\right).\]
For example $\beta(0.53) = \beta(0.47) = 0.487$ and $\beta(0.55) = \beta(0.45) = 0.892$.
The power function is drawn in figure~\ref{fig:coinpower}. Since $H_0$ is simple, the
level and the size are both $0.054$.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[declare function={Phi(\x)=1/(1+exp(-0.07056*\x*\x*\x-1.5976*\x));}]
\begin{axis}[
    width=10cm, height=6cm,
    xlabel={$\theta$}, ylabel={$\beta(\theta)$},
    xmin=0.42, xmax=0.58, ymin=0, ymax=1.05,
    axis lines=left, grid=major, grid style={draw=gray!25},
    xtick={0.44,0.47,0.5,0.53,0.56}, ytick={0.054,0.5,1},
    yticklabels={$0.054$,$0.5$,$1$},
    x tick label style={/pgf/number format/fixed, /pgf/number format/precision=2}]
\addplot[very thick, blue, smooth, samples=120, domain=0.42:0.58]
  {Phi((469.5-1000*x)/sqrt(1000*x*(1-x))) + 1 - Phi((530.5-1000*x)/sqrt(1000*x*(1-x)))};
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Power function of the test that rejects fairness when the number of heads in
$1000$ tosses differs from $500$ by more than $30$.}
\label{fig:coinpower}
\end{figure}

\section{Uniformly Most Powerful Tests}

Tests are usually constructed by fixing the size, which controls the probability of
a type I error, and then making the probability of a type II error as small as
possible; equivalently, making the power $\beta(\theta)$ as large as possible for
$\theta\in\Omega - \Omega_0$.

\subsection{Uniformly Most Powerful Tests and the Monotone Likelihood Ratio}

\begin{defn}
\label{def:umpsi}
A test of size $\alpha$ with power function $\beta(\theta)$ is \emph{uniformly most
powerful} (UMP) if $\beta(\theta)\ge\beta^*(\theta)$ for every $\theta\in\Omega - \Omega_0$ and
the power function $\beta^*$ of every other test of level $\alpha$.
\end{defn}

\emph{Uniformly} means that one power function dominates all others at every
alternative. When the alternative is simple, a single point $\theta_1$, the word is
dropped and the test is called \emph{most powerful}.

\begin{thm}[Neyman--Pearson lemma]
\label{thm:npsi}
Let $X$ have probability (density) function $f_\theta(x)$, and consider testing the
simple hypothesis $H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$ against the simple alternative
$H_1\colon\theta = \theta_1$. Suppose the critical region
\[R = \left\{x : \frac{f_{\theta_1}(x)}{f_{\theta_0}(x)}>C\right\},\]
for a constant $C\ge 0$, has size $\alpha$. Then $R$ is a most powerful test of size
$\alpha$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Let $R_1$ be any critical region with $P_{\theta_0}(X\in R_1)\le\alpha$, and write
$f_0 = f_{\theta_0}$ and $f_1 = f_{\theta_1}$. Removing the common part $R\cap R_1$,
\[\beta(\theta_1) - \beta_1(\theta_1) = \int_{R\cap R_1^c}f_1\,dx - \int_{R_1\cap R^c}f_1\,dx,\]
where $\beta_1$ is the power function of $R_1$, and in the same way
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:npsizes}
\int_{R\cap R_1^c}f_0\,dx - \int_{R_1\cap R^c}f_0\,dx = \alpha - P_{\theta_0}(X\in R_1)\ge 0 .
\end{equation}
On $R\cap R_1^c\subset R$ we have $f_1>Cf_0$, and on $R_1\cap R^c$, outside $R$, we have
$f_1\le Cf_0$. Hence
\[\beta(\theta_1) - \beta_1(\theta_1)\ge C\left[\int_{R\cap R_1^c}f_0\,dx - \int_{R_1\cap R^c}f_0\,dx\right]\ge 0\]
by \eqref{eq:npsizes}. For discrete $X$ replace the integrals by sums.
\end{proof}

The method for a composite one-sided alternative is to fix an arbitrary
$\theta_1\in\Omega - \Omega_0$, construct the most powerful test against $\theta_1$, and
check whether the critical region depends on $\theta_1$. If it does not, the same test
is most powerful against every alternative, and hence UMP.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:umpnormalsi}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be independent $N(\theta, 1)$ with $\Omega = [0, \infty)$, and test
$H_0\colon\theta = 0$ against $H_1\colon\theta>0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] For an arbitrary $\theta_1>0$, find the most powerful test of size
$\alpha = 0.05$ of $H_0$ against $\theta = \theta_1$.
\item[(b)] Does the test depend on $\theta_1$? Is it UMP?
\item[(c)] Find and sketch its power function.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] The joint density is
$f_\theta(x) = (2\pi)^{-n/2}\exp\left\{-\frac12\sum x_i^2 + n\theta\bar x - \frac n2\theta^2\right\}$, so
\[\frac{f_{\theta_1}(x)}{f_0(x)} = \exp\left\{n\theta_1\bar x - \frac n2\theta_1^2\right\}.\]
Because $\theta_1>0$, this exceeds $C$ exactly when $\bar x>K$ for some constant $K$.
Under $H_0$, $\sqrt n\,\bar X\sim N(0, 1)$, so
$0.05 = P_0(\bar X>K) = P(Z>K\sqrt n)$ gives $K\sqrt n = 1.645$
(figure~\ref{fig:normaltail}). The most powerful test rejects $H_0$ when
$\bar x>\frac{1.645}{\sqrt n}$.
\item[(b)] The construction used only the sign of $\theta_1$, so the same critical region
is most powerful against every $\theta_1>0$. It is therefore the UMP test of size
$0.05$ of $H_0\colon\theta = 0$ against $H_1\colon\theta>0$.
\item[(c)] Since $\sqrt n(\bar X - \theta)\sim N(0, 1)$,
\[\beta(\theta) = P_\theta\left(\bar X>\frac{1.645}{\sqrt n}\right) = P\left(Z>1.645 - \theta\sqrt n\right) = 1 - \Phi\left(1.645 - \theta\sqrt n\right).\]
It equals $0.05$ at $\theta = 0$ and increases to $1$; for $n = 9$, $\beta(0.5) = 0.442$ and
$\beta(1) = 0.912$. The curve is shown in figure~\ref{fig:umppowersi}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\draw(-3.5,0)--(3.5,0)
(1.5,0)--(1.5,0.75)
(1.5,0)node[below, scale = 0.9]{1.645}
(1.7,0.4)--(2.3,1)
(2.3,1)node[right,scale = 0.9]{0.05};
\draw[dashed](0,0)--(0,2.39);
\path[fill=teal,opacity=0.25, smooth, domain = 1.5:3.5, variable = \x]plot(\x, {6*1/sqrt(2*pi)*exp(-1/2*\x*\x)})--(3.5,0)--(1.5,0)--(1.5,0.75)--cycle;
\draw[smooth, domain = -3.5:3.5, variable = \x]plot(\x,{6*1/sqrt(2*pi)*exp(-1/2*\x*\x)});
\end{tikzpicture}
\label{fig:normaltail}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[declare function={Phi(\x)=1/(1+exp(-0.07056*\x*\x*\x-1.5976*\x));}]
\begin{axis}[
    width=10cm, height=6cm,
    xlabel={$\theta$}, ylabel={$\beta(\theta)$},
    xmin=0, xmax=1.5, ymin=0, ymax=1.05,
    axis lines=left, grid=major, grid style={draw=gray!25},
    ytick={0.05,0.5,1}, yticklabels={$0.05$,$0.5$,$1$}]
\addplot[very thick, blue, smooth, samples=100, domain=0:1.5] {1-Phi(1.645-3*x)};
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Power function of the UMP test of $H_0\colon\theta = 0$ against $H_1\colon\theta>0$
for samples of $n = 9$ from $N(\theta, 1)$.}
\label{fig:umppowersi}
\end{figure}

The same argument works whenever the likelihood ratio is monotone in a statistic.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:mlrsi}
A family $f_\theta(x)$, $\theta\in\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}$, has a \emph{monotone likelihood
ratio} (MLR) in the statistic $T(X)$ if for every $\theta_1<\theta_2$ the ratio
$\frac{f_{\theta_2}(x)}{f_{\theta_1}(x)}$ is a nondecreasing function of $T(x)$.
\end{defn}

\begin{thm}[Karlin--Rubin]
\label{thm:karlinrubinsi}
If the family has an MLR in $T$, and $c$ is chosen so that $P_{\theta_0}(T>c) = \alpha$,
then the test that rejects $H_0$ when $T(x)>c$ is a UMP test of size $\alpha$ of
$H_0\colon\theta\le\theta_0$ against $H_1\colon\theta>\theta_0$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\theta_1>\theta_0$. The ratio $\frac{f_{\theta_1}}{f_{\theta_0}}$ is a nondecreasing function
$g(T)$, so $\{T>c\}$ contains $\{g(T)>g(c)\}$ and is contained in $\{g(T)\ge g(c)\}$.
The proof of theorem~\ref{thm:npsi} goes through for such a region (on
$R\cap R_1^c$ we have $f_1\ge g(c)f_0$ and on $R_1\cap R^c$ we have $f_1\le g(c)f_0$), so
$\{T>c\}$ is most powerful against $\theta_1$ among tests with size at most $\alpha$ at
$\theta_0$. It does not depend on $\theta_1$. It remains to see that the size over the
composite null is still $\alpha$: applying the same argument to a pair
$\theta'<\theta_0$, the region $\{T>c\}$ is most powerful of its own size $\beta(\theta')$ for
$\theta'$ against $\theta_0$, and is therefore at least as powerful as the test that
ignores the data and rejects with probability $\beta(\theta')$. So
$\beta(\theta_0)\ge\beta(\theta')$: the power function is nondecreasing, and
$\sup_{\theta\le\theta_0}\beta(\theta) = \beta(\theta_0) = \alpha$.
\end{proof}

A one-parameter exponential family $f_\theta(x) = C(\theta)e^{q(\theta)T(x)}h(x)$ with $q$
increasing has an MLR in the natural statistic, since the ratio is
$\frac{C(\theta_2)}{C(\theta_1)}e^{[q(\theta_2) - q(\theta_1)]T(x)}$. For $q$ decreasing, or for the
alternative $\theta<\theta_0$, the inequality is reversed.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:umpexponentialsi}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the exponential distribution with mean
$\theta$. Find the UMP test of size $\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\theta\le\theta_0$ against
$H_1\colon\theta>\theta_0$, and give it for $n = 10$, $\theta_0 = 5$ and $\alpha = 0.05$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
The joint density $\theta^{-n}\exp\left\{-\frac1\theta\sum x_i\right\}$ is an exponential family
with $q(\theta) = -\frac1\theta$, which is increasing, and $T = \sum X_i$. So there is an MLR
in $T$, and by theorem~\ref{thm:karlinrubinsi} the UMP test rejects $H_0$ when
$\sum x_i>c$. Since $\frac{2T}{\theta}\sim\chi^2(2n)$,
\[\alpha = P_{\theta_0}(T>c) = P\left(\chi^2(2n)>\frac{2c}{\theta_0}\right),\]
so $c = \frac{\theta_0}{2}\chi^2_{2n}(\alpha)$, where $\chi^2_{2n}(\alpha)$ is the upper $\alpha$ point.
For $n = 10$, $\theta_0 = 5$ and $\alpha = 0.05$, $\chi^2_{20}(0.05) = 31.410$, so the test
rejects $H_0$ when the ten lifetimes total more than $c = 78.5$. Its power at
$\theta = 10$ is $P\left(\chi^2(20)>15.705\right) = 0.735$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, \cdots\,, \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $GAM(1/2,\theta)$ distribution. Find the 
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Most powerful test of size $\alpha = 0.05$ of $H_0:\, \theta = 2$ against the alternative $H_1:\, \theta = \theta_1$ where $\theta_1 < 2$.
\item[(b)] Uniformly most powerful test of size $\alpha = 0.05$ of $H_0:\, \theta = 2$ against the alternative $H_1:\, \theta < 2$ and sketch the power function of this test.
\item[(c)] Uniformly most powerful test of size $\alpha = 0.05$ of $H_0:\, \theta = 2$ against the alternative $H_1:\, \theta > 2$. On the same graph as (b) sketch the power function of this test.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
For GAM$(1/2,\theta)$,
\[L(\theta) = \frac{\prod_i x_i^{-1/2}}{\Gamma(1/2)^{n}\,\theta^{n/2}}
\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_i x_i\right\}.\]

\subhead{(a)}
By Neyman--Pearson the most powerful test rejects for large values of
\[\frac{L(\theta_1)}{L(2)} = \left(\frac{2}{\theta_1}\right)^{n/2}
\exp\left\{-\left(\frac{1}{\theta_1}-\frac12\right)\sum_i x_i\right\}.\]
Since $\theta_1<2$ the coefficient $1/\theta_1-1/2$ is positive, so the ratio is
a \emph{decreasing} function of $\sum_i x_i$ and the test rejects for
\emph{small} values of it. Under $H_0$, $\sum_i X_i\sim$ GAM$(n/2,2)$, so
$\sum_i X_i \sim \chi^{2}_{(n)}$ exactly, and the size-$0.05$ region is
\[R = \left\{x:\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}x_i < \chi^{2}_{n,\,0.05}\right\}.\]

\subhead{(b)}
The region does not involve $\theta_1$ --- every alternative below $2$ gives the
same rejection rule --- so by definition the same test is uniformly most
powerful for $H_1:\theta<2$. Its power function is
\[\beta(\theta) = P_{\theta}\left(\sum_i X_i < \chi^{2}_{n,\,0.05}\right)
= P\left(\chi^{2}_{(n)} < \frac{2\,\chi^{2}_{n,\,0.05}}{\theta}\right),\]
which decreases from $1$ as $\theta\downarrow0$, passes through $0.05$ at
$\theta=2$, and tends to $0$ as $\theta\rightarrow\infty$.

\subhead{(c)}
For $\theta_1>2$ the coefficient changes sign, the ratio increases in
$\sum_i x_i$, and the UMP test of $H_1:\theta>2$ rejects for large values:
\[R = \left\{x:\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}x_i > \chi^{2}_{n,\,0.95}\right\},\]
with power function increasing from $0$, through $0.05$ at $\theta=2$, to $1$.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.0]
\draw[->](0,0)--(6.4,0)node[right]{$\theta$};
\draw[->](0,0)--(0,3.4)node[above]{$\beta(\theta)$};
\draw[dashed](0,0.3)--(6.2,0.3)node[right,scale=0.8]{$0.05$};
\draw[dashed](2.4,0)--(2.4,3)node[above,scale=0.8]{};
\draw(2.4,0)node[below,scale=0.8]{$2$};
\draw[thick,blue] plot[smooth,domain=0.25:6] (\x, {3/(1+exp(2.4*(\x-2.4)))+0.02});
\draw[thick,red]  plot[smooth,domain=0.25:6] (\x, {3/(1+exp(-2.4*(\x-2.4)))*0.98});
\node[blue,scale=0.8] at (1.15,2.55) {$H_1:\theta<2$};
\node[red,scale=0.8]  at (5.0,2.55) {$H_1:\theta>2$};
\draw(0,3)node[left,scale=0.8]{$1$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
Both curves pass through $0.05$ at $\theta=2$, which is the size constraint, and
each rises towards $1$ on the side its alternative occupies. No test can be UMP
against the two-sided alternative $\theta\neq2$, because the two regions are
different and a test cannot reject simultaneously for small and for large
$\sum_i x_i$ while keeping size $0.05$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, \cdots\,, \, X_n$ be a random sample from the distribution with pdf \[f_{\theta}(x) = \frac{\theta}{x^{1 + \theta}}\, , \, \, x> 1, \, \, \theta > 0\] Find the
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] most powerful tests of size $\alpha = 0.05$ of $H_0:\, \theta = 1$ against $H_1:\, \theta = \theta_1$ were $\theta_1  > 1$.
\item[(b)] uniformly most powerful test of size $\alpha = 0.05$ of $H_0:\, \theta = 1$ and sketch the power function.
\item[(c)] uniformly most powerful test of size $\alpha = 0.05$ of $H_0:\, \theta = 1$ against $H_1:\, \theta < 1$. On the same graph as in (b), sketch the power function of this test.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The density is Pareto: $f_{\theta}(x)=\theta x^{-(1+\theta)}$ for $x>1$. Writing
$T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log X_i$,
\[L(\theta) = \theta^{n}\exp\left\{-(1+\theta)\sum_i\log x_i\right\}
= \theta^{n}e^{-(1+\theta)T}.\]
Note that $\log X_i \sim$ EXP$(1/\theta)$, since
$P\left(\log X>t\right)=P\left(X>e^{t}\right)=e^{-\theta t}$, so
$T\sim$ GAM$(n,1/\theta)$ and $2\theta T\sim\chi^{2}_{(2n)}$.

\subhead{(a)}
\[\frac{L(\theta_1)}{L(1)} = \theta_1^{n}\,e^{-(\theta_1-1)T},\]
which for $\theta_1>1$ is decreasing in $T$. The most powerful test therefore
rejects for small $T$, and under $H_0$ we have $2T\sim\chi^{2}_{(2n)}$, so
\[R = \left\{x:\ 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log x_i < \chi^{2}_{2n,\,0.05}\right\}.\]

\subhead{(b)}
The region is free of $\theta_1$, so the same test is UMP for $H_1:\theta>1$.
Its power is
\[\beta(\theta) = P_{\theta}\left(2T<\chi^{2}_{2n,\,0.05}\right)
= P\left(\chi^{2}_{(2n)} < \theta\,\chi^{2}_{2n,\,0.05}\right),\]
increasing in $\theta$, equal to $0.05$ at $\theta=1$ and tending to $1$ as
$\theta\rightarrow\infty$.

\subhead{(c)}
For $\theta_1<1$ the ratio increases in $T$, so the UMP test of $H_1:\theta<1$
rejects for large $T$:
\[R = \left\{x:\ 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log x_i > \chi^{2}_{2n,\,0.95}\right\},\]
with power decreasing in $\theta$, again passing through $0.05$ at $\theta=1$.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.0]
\draw[->](0,0)--(6.4,0)node[right]{$\theta$};
\draw[->](0,0)--(0,3.4)node[above]{$\beta(\theta)$};
\draw[dashed](0,0.3)--(6.2,0.3)node[right,scale=0.8]{$0.05$};
\draw[dashed](2.4,0)--(2.4,3);
\draw(2.4,0)node[below,scale=0.8]{$1$};
\draw[thick,red]  plot[smooth,domain=0.25:6] (\x, {3/(1+exp(-2.4*(\x-2.4)))*0.98});
\draw[thick,blue] plot[smooth,domain=0.25:6] (\x, {3/(1+exp(2.4*(\x-2.4)))+0.02});
\node[red,scale=0.8]  at (5.0,2.55) {$H_1:\theta>1$};
\node[blue,scale=0.8] at (1.15,2.55) {$H_1:\theta<1$};
\draw(0,3)node[left,scale=0.8]{$1$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{Tests and confidence intervals}

There is a close relationship between tests and confidence intervals, and it is
worth seeing it on a concrete case before stating it in general.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:testci}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\theta, 1)$, and test
$H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$ against $H_1\colon\theta\ne\theta_0$. The critical region
$\left\{x : |\bar x - \theta_0|>\frac{1.96}{\sqrt n}\right\}$ has size $0.05$, and its acceptance
region is $\left\{x : |\bar x - \theta_0|\le\frac{1.96}{\sqrt n}\right\}$.
\end{exa}

The hypothesis $\theta = \theta_0$ is accepted exactly when
\[\bar x - \frac{1.96}{\sqrt n}\le\theta_0\le\bar x + \frac{1.96}{\sqrt n},\]
that is, when $\theta_0$ lies in the usual $95\%$ confidence interval for $\theta$. The
confidence interval is the set of null values that the test would not reject.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:inversion}
For each $\theta_0\in\Omega$ let $A(\theta_0)$ be the acceptance region of a test of size
$\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$. Then
\[C(x) = \{\theta_0 : x\in A(\theta_0)\}\]
is a $100(1 - \alpha)\%$ confidence set for $\theta$. Conversely, if $C(X)$ is a
$100(1 - \alpha)\%$ confidence set, then $A(\theta_0) = \{x : \theta_0\in C(x)\}$ is the
acceptance region of a test of size $\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For every $\theta$, $\theta\in C(X)$ if and only if $X\in A(\theta)$, so
$P_\theta\left(\theta\in C(X)\right) = P_\theta\left(X\in A(\theta)\right) = 1 - \alpha$. The converse is the
same identity read the other way.
\end{proof}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, \cdots\,, \, X_n$ be a random sample from the $GAM(2, \theta)$ distributions. Show that
\[R = \left\{x:\, \sum^5_{i = 1}x_i  < 4.7955\, \theta_0\, \, \text{or}\,\, \sum^5_{i = 1} x_i > 17.085\, \theta_0\right\}\]
is a size $\alpha = 0.05$ critical region for testing $H_0:\, \theta = \theta_0$. Show that this critical region may be used to construct a $95\%$ C.I for $\theta$. 
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Here $n=5$ and each $X_i\sim$ GAM$(2,\theta)$, so
$\sum_{i=1}^{5}X_i \sim$ GAM$(10,\theta)$ and
\[\frac{2}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{5}X_i \ \sim\ \chi^{2}_{(20)} .\]

\subhead{The size}
Under $H_0:\theta=\theta_0$ the two boundaries transform to
\[\frac{2(4.7955\,\theta_0)}{\theta_0} = 9.591,
\qquad \frac{2(17.085\,\theta_0)}{\theta_0} = 34.170,\]
and these are exactly the $2.5\%$ and $97.5\%$ points of $\chi^{2}_{(20)}$.
Hence
\[P_{\theta_0}(X\in R) = P\left(\chi^{2}_{(20)}<9.591\right)
+ P\left(\chi^{2}_{(20)}>34.170\right) = 0.025+0.025 = 0.05 ,\]
so $R$ has size $\alpha=0.05$.

\subhead{The confidence interval}
Acceptance means $4.7955\,\theta_0 \leq \sum_i x_i \leq 17.085\,\theta_0$, which
rearranges to
\[\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{5}x_i}{17.085} \ \leq\ \theta_0 \ \leq\ \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{5}x_i}{4.7955} .\]
The set of $\theta_0$ that would \emph{not} be rejected is therefore an
interval, and since the test has size $0.05$ this interval covers the true
$\theta$ with probability $0.95$:
\[\left(\frac{\sum_i X_i}{17.085},\ \frac{\sum_i X_i}{4.7955}\right)\]
is a $95\%$ confidence interval for $\theta$.

\begin{note}
This is the duality of the earlier example, used in the useful direction:
inverting a family of size-$\alpha$ tests produces a $100(1-\alpha)\%$
confidence set, and the set is an interval exactly when the acceptance regions
are intervals in the statistic. Nothing new is computed --- the same two
constants do both jobs.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\section{Locally Most Powerful Tests}

A UMP test does not always exist. Alternatives close to the null value are the hardest
to detect, so it makes sense to ask for a test whose power rises as fast as possible
as $\theta$ moves away from $\theta_0$.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:lmp}
A test of $H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$ against $H_1\colon\theta>\theta_0$ with power function
$\beta(\theta)$ is \emph{locally most powerful} (LMP) if for every other test of the
same size, with power function $\beta^*(\theta)$, there is an $\varepsilon>0$ such that
$\beta(\theta)\ge\beta^*(\theta)$ for all $\theta_0<\theta<\theta_0 + \varepsilon$.
\end{defn}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:lmp}
For a random sample from a regular model, the test with critical region
\[R = \{x : S(\theta_0; x)>C\},\]
where $C$ is determined by the size, is locally most powerful for $H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$
against $H_1\colon\theta>\theta_0$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
All tests of size $\alpha$ have $\beta(\theta_0) = \alpha$, so for $\theta$ just above $\theta_0$,
$\beta(\theta)\approx\alpha + (\theta - \theta_0)\beta'(\theta_0)$, and the LMP test is the one that
maximises the slope $\beta'(\theta_0)$. Differentiating under the integral sign,
\begin{align*}
\beta'(\theta_0) &= \int_R\frac{\partial f_\theta(x)}{\partial\theta}\Big|_{\theta_0}dx\\
&= \int_R S(\theta_0; x)f_{\theta_0}(x)\,dx .
\end{align*}
Maximising $\int_R S(\theta_0; x)f_{\theta_0}(x)\,dx$ subject to $\int_Rf_{\theta_0}(x)\,dx = \alpha$ is the
problem of the Neyman--Pearson lemma with $f_1$ replaced by $S(\theta_0; x)f_{\theta_0}(x)$, and the
same argument shows that the optimal region is $\{x : S(\theta_0; x)>C\}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:lmpnormal}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\theta, 1)$. Show that the LMP test of
$H_0\colon\theta = 0$ against $H_1\colon\theta>0$ is the UMP test.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$\mathcal{L}(\theta) = -\frac12\sum(x_i - \theta)^2 - \frac n2\ln 2\pi$, so
$S(\theta) = \sum(x_i - \theta) = n(\bar x - \theta)$ and $S(0; x) = n\bar x$. The LMP region
$\{n\bar x>C\}$ is $\{\bar x>K\}$, the UMP region of example~\ref{ex:umpnormalsi}.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:lmpcauchy}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the Cauchy distribution with location
$\theta$, $f_\theta(x) = \frac{1}{\pi[1 + (x - \theta)^2]}$. Find the LMP test of $H_0\colon\theta = 0$
against $H_1\colon\theta>0$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$\ln f_\theta(x) = -\ln\pi - \ln\left[1 + (x - \theta)^2\right]$, so
\[S(\theta; x) = \sum_{i=1}^n\frac{2(x_i - \theta)}{1 + (x_i - \theta)^2},\qquad
S(0; x) = \sum_{i=1}^n\frac{2x_i}{1 + x_i^2} .\]
The LMP test rejects $H_0$ when $\sum\frac{2x_i}{1 + x_i^2}>C$. Each term is bounded by
$1$ in absolute value, so a single wild observation cannot force rejection, unlike a
test based on $\bar x$ (which for Cauchy data is no better than a single observation).
Under $H_0$ each term has mean $0$ and variance $E\left[\frac{4X^2}{(1 + X^2)^2}\right] = \frac12$,
so for large $n$, $C\approx z_\alpha\sqrt{n/2}$. No UMP test exists here, because the
likelihood ratio is not monotone in any single statistic.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Consider a single observation $X$ from the LOG$(1,\theta)$ distribution. Find the locally most powerful test of $H_0:\, \theta = 0$ against $H_1:\, \theta > 0$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The logistic density with location $\theta$ and scale $1$ is
\[f_{\theta}(x) = \frac{e^{-(x-\theta)}}{\left[1+e^{-(x-\theta)}\right]^{2}} .\]
A locally most powerful test of $H_0:\theta=0$ against $H_1:\theta>0$ rejects
for large values of the score at $\theta=0$. Now
\[\log f_{\theta}(x) = -(x-\theta) - 2\log\left[1+e^{-(x-\theta)}\right],\]
so
\[S(\theta,x) = \frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\log f_{\theta}(x)
= 1 - \frac{2e^{-(x-\theta)}}{1+e^{-(x-\theta)}}
= 1 - \frac{2}{1+e^{\,x-\theta}} ,\]
and at $\theta=0$,
\[S(0,x) = 1 - \frac{2}{1+e^{x}} = \frac{e^{x}-1}{e^{x}+1} = \tanh\!\left(\frac{x}{2}\right).\]
This is a strictly increasing function of $x$, so rejecting for large $S(0,X)$
is the same as rejecting for large $X$, and the locally most powerful test is
\[R = \left\{x:\ x > c\right\}.\]
Under $H_0$ the standard logistic distribution has
$P_0(X>c) = 1-\frac{1}{1+e^{-c}} = \frac{1}{1+e^{c}}$, so setting this equal to
$\alpha$ gives
\[c = \log\left(\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\right).\]
For $\alpha=0.05$, $c=\log 19 \approx 2.944$.

\begin{note}
The test is only \emph{locally} most powerful --- best against alternatives just
above zero. Here it happens also to be UMP, because the logistic location family
has monotone likelihood ratio in $x$, but that is a bonus rather than something
the locally most powerful construction guarantees.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\section{Likelihood Ratio Tests}

For composite hypotheses the Neyman--Pearson ratio is replaced by a ratio of maximised
likelihoods.

\begin{defn}
\label{def:lrt}
The \emph{likelihood ratio test} of $H_0\colon\theta\in\Omega_0$ against
$H_1\colon\theta\in\Omega - \Omega_0$ has critical region $R = \{x : \Lambda(x)>C\}$, where
\[\Lambda(x) = \frac{\sup_{\theta\in\Omega}L(\theta)}{\sup_{\theta\in\Omega_0}L(\theta)} = \frac{L(\hat\theta)}{L(\hat\theta_R)}\]
and $C$ is determined by the size. Here $\hat\theta$ is the usual ML estimate and
$\hat\theta_R$ is the ML estimate under the restriction $\theta\in\Omega_0$.
\end{defn}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:lrtt}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$ with both parameters
unknown, and test $H_0\colon\mu = 0$ against $H_1\colon\mu\ne 0$. Show that the
likelihood ratio test has critical region $\left\{x : \frac{n\bar x^2}{s^2}>C\right\}$, that
$\frac{n\bar X^2}{S^2}\sim F(1, n - 1)$ under $H_0$, and find the test of size $0.05$ for
$n = 20$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Here $\Omega = \{(\mu, \sigma^2) : \mu\in\mathbb{R}, \sigma^2>0\}$ and
$\Omega_0 = \{(0, \sigma^2) : \sigma^2>0\}$. From example~\ref{ex:normalmle}, the unrestricted
ML estimates are $\hat\mu = \bar x$ and $\hat\sigma^2 = \frac1n\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2$, and
substituting them,
\[L(\hat\theta) = (2\pi\hat\sigma^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left\{-\frac{n\hat\sigma^2}{2\hat\sigma^2}\right\} = (2\pi\hat\sigma^2)^{-n/2}e^{-n/2} .\]
Under $H_0$, $\mathcal{L} = -\frac n2\ln(2\pi\sigma^2) - \frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum x_i^2$; setting
$\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\sigma^2} = -\frac{n}{2\sigma^2} + \frac{1}{2\sigma^4}\sum x_i^2 = 0$ gives
$\hat\sigma_R^2 = \frac1n\sum x_i^2$, and in the same way
$L(\hat\theta_R) = (2\pi\hat\sigma_R^2)^{-n/2}e^{-n/2}$. Since
