$\sum x_i^2 = \sum(x_i - \bar x)^2 + n\bar x^2 = (n - 1)s^2 + n\bar x^2$,
\[\Lambda(x) = \left(\frac{\hat\sigma_R^2}{\hat\sigma^2}\right)^{n/2}
= \left(\frac{(n - 1)s^2 + n\bar x^2}{(n - 1)s^2}\right)^{n/2}
= \left(1 + \frac{1}{n - 1}\cdot\frac{n\bar x^2}{s^2}\right)^{n/2} .\]
This increases with $\frac{n\bar x^2}{s^2}$, so $\Lambda>K$ is equivalent to
$\frac{n\bar x^2}{s^2}>C$.

Under $H_0$,
\[T = \frac{n\bar X^2}{S^2} = \frac{\left(\frac{\bar X}{\sigma/\sqrt n}\right)^2\big/1}{\frac{(n - 1)S^2}{\sigma^2}\big/(n - 1)},\]
a ratio of a $\chi^2(1)$ variable to an independent $\chi^2(n - 1)$ variable, each
divided by its degrees of freedom, so $T\sim F(1, n - 1)$. For $n = 20$,
$0.05 = P(F(1, 19)>C)$ gives $C = 4.38$. Since $T$ is the square of the one-sample $t$
statistic and $t_{19}(0.025)^2 = 2.093^2 = 4.38$, this is the two-sided $t$ test.
\end{solution}

When the exact distribution of $\Lambda$ is not available, the following
large-sample result is used. It is proved in problem~\ref{prob:wilks}.

\begin{thm}[Wilks]
\label{thm:wilkssi}
Suppose the model is regular, $\Omega$ is an open subset of $\mathbb{R}^k$, and
$\Omega_0 = \{\theta(\eta) : \eta\in E\}$ for an open set $E\subset\mathbb{R}^q$ and a smooth
one-to-one map $\theta(\cdot)$, with $q<k$. Then under $H_0$,
\[2\ln\Lambda(X)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}\chi^2(k - q) .\]
\end{thm}

The number of degrees of freedom, $k - q$, is the number of parameters that must be
estimated in the general model minus the number that must be estimated under $H_0$.
The approximate test of size $\alpha$ rejects $H_0$ when $2\ln\Lambda>\chi^2_{k - q}(\alpha)$.

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:lrtpoisson}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be independent $\text{POI}(\lambda)$ and, independently,
$Y_1, \dots, Y_n$ be independent $\text{POI}(\mu)$. Find the likelihood ratio test of
$H_0\colon\lambda = \mu$ against $H_1\colon\lambda\ne\mu$, and apply it when $n = 20$,
$\bar x = 3.1$ and $\bar y = 2.2$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Here $\Omega = \{(\lambda, \mu) : \lambda, \mu>0\}$, with $k = 2$, and
$\Omega_0 = \{(\lambda, \lambda) : \lambda>0\}$, with $q = 1$. Dropping the factorials, which
cancel in the ratio,
\[L(\lambda, \mu)\propto e^{-n\lambda}\lambda^{n\bar x}e^{-n\mu}\mu^{n\bar y} .\]
The unrestricted ML estimates are $\hat\lambda = \bar x$ and $\hat\mu = \bar y$. Under $H_0$,
$L\propto e^{-2n\lambda}\lambda^{n(\bar x + \bar y)}$, maximised at
$\hat\lambda_R = \frac{\bar x + \bar y}{2} = m$, say. The factors $e^{-n(\bar x + \bar y)}$ appear in
both maxima and cancel, leaving
\[\Lambda = \frac{\bar x^{n\bar x}\bar y^{n\bar y}}{m^{n(\bar x + \bar y)}}
= \left(\frac{\bar x^{\bar x}\bar y^{\bar y}}{m^{\bar x + \bar y}}\right)^n,\qquad
2\ln\Lambda = 2n\left[\bar x\ln\frac{\bar x}{m} + \bar y\ln\frac{\bar y}{m}\right].\]
The test rejects $H_0$ when this is large. By theorem~\ref{thm:wilkssi}, under $H_0$,
$2\ln\Lambda$ is approximately $\chi^2(2 - 1) = \chi^2(1)$.

For the data, $m = 2.65$ and
\[2\ln\Lambda = 40\left[3.1\ln\frac{3.1}{2.65} + 2.2\ln\frac{2.2}{2.65}\right] = 40\,[0.48621 - 0.40943] = 3.071 .\]
Since $3.071<\chi^2_1(0.05) = 3.841$, $H_0$ is not rejected at the $5\%$ level; the
approximate $p$-value is $P(\chi^2(1)>3.071) = 0.080$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Suppose $X\thicksim GAM(2,\beta_1)$ and $Y\thicksim GAM(2,\beta_2)$ independently. Show that the likelihood ratio statistic for testing the hypothesis $H_0: \beta_1 = \beta_2\,$ against the alternative $\, H_1:\beta_1\neq \beta_2$ is a function of the statistic 
\[T = \frac{X}{X + Y}.\]
Find the distribution of $T$ under $H_0$. Find the critical region for a size $\alpha = 0.01$ test.\\

\textit{Hint:} If $X\thicksim GAM(a,1)$ and $Y\thicksim GAM(b,1)$ are independent random variable then \[\frac{X}{X + Y} \thicksim BETA(a,b).\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
With $X\sim$ GAM$(2,\beta_1)$ and $Y\sim$ GAM$(2,\beta_2)$ independent,
\[L(\beta_1,\beta_2) = \frac{x\,e^{-x/\beta_1}}{\beta_1^{2}}\cdot
\frac{y\,e^{-y/\beta_2}}{\beta_2^{2}} .\]
Unrestricted, the maximum likelihood estimates are
$\widehat{\beta}_1 = x/2$ and $\widehat{\beta}_2 = y/2$. Under
$H_0:\beta_1=\beta_2=\beta$ the total $x+y$ has shape $4$, so
$\widehat{\beta} = (x+y)/4$. Substituting,
\[\Lambda = \frac{\sup_{H_0}L}{\sup L}
= \frac{\left[(x+y)/4\right]^{-4}e^{-4}}
       {\left(x/2\right)^{-2}\left(y/2\right)^{-2}e^{-2}e^{-2}}
= \frac{\left(x/2\right)^{2}\left(y/2\right)^{2}}{\left[(x+y)/4\right]^{4}}
= \frac{64\,x^{2}y^{2}}{(x+y)^{4}} .\]
Writing $t = x/(x+y)$, so that $x=t(x+y)$ and $y=(1-t)(x+y)$, the factors of
$(x+y)$ cancel and
\[\Lambda = 64\,t^{2}(1-t)^{2} = 64\left[t(1-t)\right]^{2},\]
a function of $T$ alone.

\subhead{The distribution of $T$ under $H_0$}
Under $H_0$, $X$ and $Y$ are independent gamma variables with the same scale and
shapes $2$ and $2$, so
\[T = \frac{X}{X+Y} \ \sim\ \text{BETA}(2,2),\]
with density $6t(1-t)$ on $(0,1)$. The common scale $\beta$ cancels, which is
what makes the null distribution free of the nuisance parameter and the test
usable.

\begin{note}
$\Lambda$ is largest when $t=\tfrac12$ and falls away symmetrically, so rejecting
for small $\Lambda$ means rejecting when $T$ is far from $\tfrac12$ in either
direction --- exactly what a two-sided test of $\beta_1=\beta_2$ should do.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Suppose $X_1, \, \cdots\, , X_n$ are independent $EXP(\lambda)$ random variables and independently $Y_1, \, \cdots\, , \, Y_n$  are independent $EXP(\mu)$ random variables.\\
Show that the likelihood ratio statistic for testing the hypothesis $H_0: \lambda = \mu$ against the alternative $H_1: \lambda \neq \mu$ is  a function of 
\[T = \frac{\sum^n_{i = 1} X_i}{\sum^n_{i =1} X_i + \sum^n_{i = 1} Y_i}.\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Write $S=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i$ and $U=\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_i$. With the exponential
parameterised by its mean,
\[L(\lambda,\mu) = \lambda^{-n}e^{-S/\lambda}\,\mu^{-n}e^{-U/\mu},\]
so unrestricted $\widehat{\lambda}=S/n$, $\widehat{\mu}=U/n$, while under
$H_0:\lambda=\mu$ the pooled estimate is $\widehat{\lambda}_0=(S+U)/(2n)$.
Hence
\[\Lambda = \frac{\left[(S+U)/2n\right]^{-2n}e^{-2n}}
{\left(S/n\right)^{-n}\left(U/n\right)^{-n}e^{-n}e^{-n}}
= \frac{\left(S/n\right)^{n}\left(U/n\right)^{n}}{\left[(S+U)/2n\right]^{2n}}
= 2^{2n}\,\frac{S^{n}U^{n}}{(S+U)^{2n}} .\]
Putting $T = S/(S+U)$ gives
\[\Lambda = 2^{2n}\left[T(1-T)\right]^{n},\]
so the statistic is a function of $T$ alone --- the same shape as the previous
problem with $n$ in place of $1$.

Under $H_0$ the sums $S$ and $U$ are independent GAM$(n,\lambda)$ variables with
a common scale, so
\[T = \frac{S}{S+U}\ \sim\ \text{BETA}(n,n),\]
free of $\lambda$. The test rejects for small $\Lambda$, that is when $T$ departs
from $\tfrac12$ in either direction, with the critical values taken from the
BETA$(n,n)$ distribution.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:wilks}
Suppose $X_1, \, \cdots\, , X_n$  is a random sample from a regular statistical model $\{f_{\theta}(x); \theta \in \Omega\}$ with $\Omega$ being an open set in a $k$-dimensional Euclidean space. Consider a subset of $\Omega$ defined by \[\Omega_0 = \{\theta(\eta):\, \eta \in \, \text{ open subset of  a}\, q-\text{dimensional Euclidean space}\}.\]
Then the likelihood ratio statistic defined by 
\[\Lambda_n(x) = \frac{\sup_{\theta\in \Omega} \prod^n_{i = 1} f_{\theta}(x_i)}{\sup_{\theta\in \Omega_0} \prod^n_{i =1} f_{\theta}(x_i)} = \frac{\sup_{\theta\in \Omega}L(\theta)}{\sup_{\theta\in \Omega_0} L(\theta)}\]
is such that, under $H_0: \theta \in \Omega_0$,
\[2\, \log \Lambda_n(x) \, \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} \, W\thicksim \chi^2_{(k - q)}.\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
This is Wilks' theorem. A full proof belongs to asymptotic theory, but the
argument is short enough to give in outline, and it explains where the degrees
of freedom come from.

Let $\widehat{\theta}$ maximise the likelihood over $\Omega$ and
$\widehat{\theta}_0$ over $\Omega_0$, and expand $\log L$ about $\widehat{\theta}$
to second order. The first-order term vanishes because $\widehat{\theta}$ is a
stationary point, leaving
\[2\log\Lambda_n = 2\left[\log L\left(\widehat{\theta}\right) - \log L\left(\widehat{\theta}_0\right)\right]
\approx \left(\widehat{\theta}-\widehat{\theta}_0\right)^{t} J\left(\theta_0\right)
\left(\widehat{\theta}-\widehat{\theta}_0\right),\]
since $-\partial^{2}\log L/\partial\theta\partial\theta^{t}$ converges to
$J(\theta_0)$.

Under $H_0$ the large-sample theory of the previous section gives
\[J\left(\theta_0\right)^{1/2}\left(\widehat{\theta}-\theta_0\right)
\underset{D}{\longrightarrow} Z \sim N_k(0,I),\]
so the quadratic form above is asymptotically $Z^{t}PZ$, where $P$ is the
orthogonal projection onto the orthogonal complement of the tangent space of
$\Omega_0$ at $\theta_0$. That tangent space has dimension $q$, so $P$ has rank
$k-q$. A quadratic form $Z^{t}PZ$ in a standard normal vector, with $P$ an
orthogonal projection of rank $r$, has the $\chi^{2}_{(r)}$ distribution. Hence
\[2\log\Lambda_n \underset{D}{\longrightarrow} W \sim \chi^{2}_{(k-q)} .\]

\begin{note}
The degrees of freedom are the number of restrictions --- the dimension the null
hypothesis removes --- and not the dimension of either space on its own. That is
the practical content of the theorem: to use it, count constraints. The
regularity conditions matter too: $\Omega_0$ must be a smooth surface in the
interior of $\Omega$, which is why the result fails when the null puts a
parameter on the boundary, as in testing a variance component equal to zero.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\section{Score and Wald Tests}

The likelihood ratio test needs the likelihood maximised twice. Two asymptotically
equivalent tests need less: the score test uses only the null value, and the Wald
test only the unrestricted estimate.

\begin{thm}[Score test]
\label{thm:scoretest}
Under the usual regularity conditions, when $H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$ is true,
\[S(\theta_0; X)\,J(\theta_0)^{-1/2}\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}N(0, 1),\qquad\text{so}\qquad
R(\theta_0; X) = \frac{S(\theta_0; X)^2}{J(\theta_0)}\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}\chi^2(1) .\]
For a vector parameter $\theta = (\theta_1, \dots, \theta_k)^{\mathsf T}$,
\[R(\theta_0; X) = S(\theta_0; X)^{\mathsf T}J(\theta_0)^{-1}S(\theta_0; X)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}\chi^2(k) .\]
The (Rao) \emph{score test} rejects $H_0$ when $R(\theta_0; x)>c$, where
$P\left(\chi^2(k)>c\right) = \alpha$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Take the scalar case. The score $S(\theta_0; X) = \sum_{i=1}^nS_1(\theta_0; X_i)$ is a sum of
independent, identically distributed terms which, under $H_0$, have mean $0$ and
variance $J_1(\theta_0)$ by theorem~\ref{thm:scoreproperties}. So $E(S) = 0$ and
$\var(S) = nJ_1(\theta_0) = J(\theta_0)$, and by the central limit theorem
$S(\theta_0; X)/\sqrt{J(\theta_0)}\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}Z\sim N(0, 1)$. The map
$z\mapsto z^2$ is continuous, so by part (2) of the limit theorems
$R(\theta_0; X)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}Z^2\sim\chi^2(1)$.

For $\theta\in\mathbb{R}^k$ the multivariate central limit theorem gives
$J(\theta_0)^{-1/2}S(\theta_0; X)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}Z\sim\text{MVN}(0, I_k)$, and
$Z^{\mathsf T}Z\sim\chi^2(k)$.
\end{proof}

The score test is computed entirely at $\theta_0$ and never needs the ML estimate.
When the likelihood is awkward to maximise but easy to differentiate, it is the test
to use.

\begin{thm}[Wald test]
\label{thm:waldtest}
Let $\hat\theta$ be the ML estimator over $\Omega$. Under the usual regularity conditions,
when $H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$ is true,
\[w(\theta_0; X) = (\hat\theta - \theta_0)^{\mathsf T}J(\hat\theta)(\hat\theta - \theta_0)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}\chi^2(k) .\]
The \emph{Wald test} rejects $H_0$ when $w(\theta_0; x)>c$, where
$P\left(\chi^2(k)>c\right) = \alpha$. The information $J(\hat\theta)$ may be replaced by
$J(\theta_0)$ or by the observed information $I(\hat\theta)$ without changing the limit.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For $k = 1$, theorem~\ref{thm:mlenormal} gives
$\sqrt{J(\theta_0)}(\hat\theta - \theta_0)\underset{D}{\longrightarrow}N(0, 1)$, so its square tends to
$\chi^2(1)$. Since $\hat\theta$ is consistent and $J_1$ is continuous,
$\frac{J(\hat\theta)}{J(\theta_0)} = \frac{J_1(\hat\theta)}{J_1(\theta_0)}\underset{P}{\longrightarrow}1$, and by Slutsky's
theorem $w(\theta_0; X) = \frac{J(\hat\theta)}{J(\theta_0)}\cdot J(\theta_0)(\hat\theta - \theta_0)^2$ has the same
limit. The vector case uses the multivariate normal limit of
section~\ref{sec:largesample} in the same way.
\end{proof}

\begin{exa}
\label{ex:scorewaldbinomial}
Let $X\sim\text{BIN}(n, \theta)$. Find the score test and the Wald test of
$H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$ against $H_1\colon\theta\ne\theta_0$, and compare them with the
likelihood ratio test when $n = 100$, $x = 60$ and $\theta_0 = 0.5$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
$\mathcal{L}(\theta) = \ln\binom nx + x\ln\theta + (n - x)\ln(1 - \theta)$, so
\[S(\theta) = \frac x\theta - \frac{n - x}{1 - \theta} = \frac{x - n\theta}{\theta(1 - \theta)},\qquad
I(\theta) = \frac{x}{\theta^2} + \frac{n - x}{(1 - \theta)^2} .\]
The ML estimate is $\hat\theta = \frac xn$, and since $E(X) = n\theta$,
\[J(\theta) = \frac{n}{\theta} + \frac{n}{1 - \theta} = \frac{n}{\theta(1 - \theta)} .\]

\subhead{Score test}
\[R(\theta_0; x) = \frac{S(\theta_0)^2}{J(\theta_0)} = \frac{(x - n\theta_0)^2}{\theta_0^2(1 - \theta_0)^2}\cdot\frac{\theta_0(1 - \theta_0)}{n}
= \frac{(x - n\theta_0)^2}{n\theta_0(1 - \theta_0)},\]
the square of the familiar statistic $\frac{\hat\theta - \theta_0}{\sqrt{\theta_0(1 - \theta_0)/n}}$.

\subhead{Wald test}
\[w(\theta_0; x) = (\hat\theta - \theta_0)^2J(\hat\theta) = \frac{(x - n\theta_0)^2}{n\hat\theta(1 - \hat\theta)} .\]
The two differ only in where the variance is evaluated: at $\theta_0$ for the score
test, at $\hat\theta$ for the Wald test. If $J(\theta_0)$ is used in the Wald statistic the
two coincide.

\subhead{Comparison}
With $n = 100$, $x = 60$ and $\theta_0 = 0.5$:
\begin{gather*}
R = \frac{100}{25} = 4.000,\qquad w = \frac{100}{100(0.6)(0.4)} = 4.167,\\
2\ln\Lambda = 2\left[60\ln\frac{0.6}{0.5} + 40\ln\frac{0.4}{0.5}\right] = 4.027 .
\end{gather*}
All three exceed $\chi^2_1(0.05) = 3.841$, so $H_0$ is rejected at the $5\%$ level by
each. The three statistics are asymptotically equivalent under $H_0$, and here they
agree to within about $4\%$; for small samples, or estimates near the boundary of
$\Omega$, they can disagree much more.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Suppose $(X_1, \, \cdots\, , X_n)$ is a random sample from the $POI(\theta)$ distribution. Find the Score test and Wald test for testing $H_0:\, \theta = \theta_0$ against $H_1:\, \theta \neq \theta_0$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
With $T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i$,
\[\log L(\theta) = -n\theta + T\log\theta + \text{const},\qquad
S(\theta,X) = \frac{T}{\theta} - n = \frac{T-n\theta}{\theta},\]
and $J(\theta) = n/\theta$. The maximum likelihood estimator is
$\widehat{\theta}=\overline{X}$.

\subhead{Score test}
\[R(\theta_0,X) = \frac{\left[S(\theta_0,X)\right]^{2}}{J(\theta_0)}
= \frac{(T-n\theta_0)^{2}}{\theta_0^{2}}\cdot\frac{\theta_0}{n}
= \frac{(T-n\theta_0)^{2}}{n\theta_0}
= \frac{n\left(\overline{X}-\theta_0\right)^{2}}{\theta_0},\]
and $H_0$ is rejected when $R>\chi^{2}_{1,\,1-\alpha}$.

\subhead{Wald test}
\[w(\theta_0,X) = \left(\widehat{\theta}-\theta_0\right)^{2}J\left(\widehat{\theta}\right)
= \left(\overline{X}-\theta_0\right)^{2}\frac{n}{\overline{X}}
= \frac{n\left(\overline{X}-\theta_0\right)^{2}}{\overline{X}},\]
rejected on the same scale.

\begin{note}
The two differ only in where the information is evaluated --- at $\theta_0$ for
the score test, at $\widehat{\theta}$ for the Wald test --- and they agree to
first order, as the theory says they must. The score version needs no maximum
likelihood estimate at all, and its null distribution uses only $\theta_0$,
which is why it is preferred when the null value is the natural reference point.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1, \, \cdots\, , X_n$ be a random sample from the distribution with pdf \[f_{\theta}(x) = \frac{\theta}{x^{1 + \theta}}\, , \  x\geq 1\]
Find the Score test and the Wald test for testing $H_0:\, \theta = \theta_0$ against $H_1:\, \theta \neq \theta_0.$
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
For $f_{\theta}(x)=\theta x^{-(1+\theta)}$ on $x\geq1$, write
$T=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log X_i$. Then
\[\log L(\theta) = n\log\theta - (1+\theta)T,\qquad
S(\theta,X) = \frac{n}{\theta} - T,\]
\[-\frac{\partial^{2}\log L}{\partial\theta^{2}} = \frac{n}{\theta^{2}}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad J(\theta) = \frac{n}{\theta^{2}},\]
the information being free of the data here. Setting the score to zero gives
$\widehat{\theta} = n/T$.

\subhead{Score test}
\[R(\theta_0,X) = \frac{\left[S(\theta_0,X)\right]^{2}}{J(\theta_0)}
= \left(\frac{n}{\theta_0}-T\right)^{2}\frac{\theta_0^{2}}{n}
= \frac{\left(n-\theta_0 T\right)^{2}}{n} .\]

\subhead{Wald test}
\[w(\theta_0,X) = \left(\widehat{\theta}-\theta_0\right)^{2}J\left(\widehat{\theta}\right)
= \left(\frac{n}{T}-\theta_0\right)^{2}\frac{n}{\left(n/T\right)^{2}}
= \frac{\left(n-\theta_0T\right)^{2}}{n} .\]

Both are referred to $\chi^{2}_{(1)}$ and $H_0$ is rejected for large values.

\begin{remark}
The two statistics are \emph{identical} here, which does not happen in general.
It occurs because the information $n/\theta^{2}$ and the score $n/\theta - T$
combine so that the $\theta$ at which the information is evaluated cancels
exactly. Recalling that $T\sim$ GAM$(n,1/\theta)$, the common statistic is
$n\left(1-\theta_0T/n\right)^{2}$, a squared standardised deviation of $T$ from
its null mean $n/\theta_0$.
\end{remark}
\end{solution}

\subsection*{Further reading}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Neyman, J., and Pearson, E. S. (1933), On the problem of the most efficient
tests of statistical hypotheses, \emph{Philosophical Transactions of the Royal
Society A}, 231, 289--337.
\item Wilks, S. S. (1938), The large-sample distribution of the likelihood ratio
for testing composite hypotheses, \emph{Annals of Mathematical Statistics}, 9,
60--62.
\item Wald, A. (1943), Tests of statistical hypotheses concerning several
parameters when the number of observations is large, \emph{Transactions of the
American Mathematical Society}, 54, 426--482.
\item Rao, C. R. (1948), Large sample tests of statistical hypotheses concerning
several parameters with applications to problems of estimation, \emph{Proceedings
of the Cambridge Philosophical Society}, 44, 50--57.
\item Karlin, S., and Rubin, H. (1956), The theory of decision procedures for
distributions with monotone likelihood ratio, \emph{Annals of Mathematical
Statistics}, 27, 272--299.
\end{enumerate}
